## Description

Joel David Hamkins is a mathematician and philosopher specializing in set theory, the foundations of mathematics, and the nature of infinity, and he's the #1 highest-rated user on MathOverflow. He is also the author of several books, including Proof and the Art of Mathematics and Lectures on the Philosophy of Mathematics. And he has a great blog called Infinitely More.

## Transcript

00:00:00 --> 00:00:04
- The following is a conversation with Joel David Hamkins, a

00:00:03 --> 00:00:08
mathematician and philosopher specializing in set theory,

00:00:07 --> 00:00:11
the foundation of mathematics and the nature of

00:00:10 --> 00:00:15
infinity. He is the number one highest

00:00:14 --> 00:00:18
rated user on MathOverflow, which I think is a legendary

00:00:17 --> 00:00:22
accomplishment. MathOverflow, by the way, is like StackOverflow

00:00:21 --> 00:00:25
but for research mathematicians. He is

00:00:25 --> 00:00:29
also the author of several books, including Proof

00:00:29 --> 00:00:33
in the Art of Mathematics and Lectures on the

00:00:32 --> 00:00:38
Philosophy of Mathematics. And he has a great blog,

00:00:37 --> 00:00:44
infinitelymore.xyz. This is a super technical and

00:00:44 --> 00:00:48
super fun conversation about the foundation of modern mathematics

00:00:48 --> 00:00:53
and some mind-bending ideas about infinity,

00:00:52 --> 00:00:56
nature of reality, truth, and the mathematical

00:00:55 --> 00:01:01
paradoxes that challenged some of the greatest minds of the 20th century.

00:01:02 --> 00:01:11
I have been hiding from the world a bit, reading, thinking, writing, soul-searching,

00:01:10 --> 00:01:15
as we all do every once in a while. But mostly, just deeply focused

00:01:14 --> 00:01:19
on work and preparing mentally for some challenging travel I

00:01:18 --> 00:01:26
plan to take on in the new year. Through all of it, a recurring thought comes to me,

00:01:25 --> 00:01:30
how damn lucky I am to be alive and to get to

00:01:29 --> 00:01:33
experience so much love from folks across the

00:01:32 --> 00:01:37
world. I want to take this moment to say thank you

00:01:37 --> 00:01:41
from the bottom of my heart for everything, for your

00:01:40 --> 00:01:45
support, for the many amazing conversations I've had

00:01:44 --> 00:01:50
with people across the world. I got a little bit of hate

00:01:50 --> 00:01:55
and a whole lot of love, and I wouldn't have it

00:01:54 --> 00:01:59
any other way. I'm grateful for all of it.

00:01:58 --> 00:02:02
This is the Lex Fridman Podcast. To support it, please check out our

00:02:01 --> 00:02:06
sponsors in the description, where you can also

00:02:05 --> 00:02:14
find ways to contact me, ask questions, give feedback, and so on. And now, dear

00:02:13 --> 00:02:17
friends, here's Joel David Hamkins.

00:02:17 --> 00:02:22
Some infinities are bigger than others. This idea from

00:02:21 --> 00:02:26
Cantor at the end of the 19th century, I think it's fair to

00:02:25 --> 00:02:30
say, broke mathematics before rebuilding it. And

00:02:29 --> 00:02:33
I also read that this was a devastating and transformative discovery for

00:02:32 --> 00:02:37
several reasons. So one, it created a theological

00:02:36 --> 00:02:41
crisis. Because infinity is associated with God, how could there be multiple

00:02:40 --> 00:02:44
infinities? And also, Cantor was deeply religious

00:02:43 --> 00:02:48
himself. Second, there's a kind of mathematical civil

00:02:47 --> 00:02:54
war. The leading German mathematician Kronecker called Cantor a corrupter of youth

00:02:54 --> 00:02:59
and tried to block his career. Third, many

00:02:58 --> 00:03:03
fascinating paradoxes emerged from this, like Russell's

00:03:02 --> 00:03:07
paradox, about the set of all sets that don't contain themselves,

00:03:06 --> 00:03:10
and those threatened to make all of mathematics

00:03:09 --> 00:03:14
inconsistent. And finally, on the psychological side and the personal

00:03:13 --> 00:03:17
side, Cantor's own breakdown. He literally went mad,

00:03:16 --> 00:03:21
spending his final years in and out of sanatoriums, obsessed with

00:03:20 --> 00:03:25
proving the continuum hypothesis. So, laying that all out on the

00:03:24 --> 00:03:31
table, can you explain the idea of infinity, that some infinities are

00:03:31 --> 00:03:36
larger than others, and why was this so transformative to mathematics?

00:03:35 --> 00:03:40
- Well, that's a really great question. I would want to start

00:03:39 --> 00:03:44
alking about infinity and telling the story much

00:03:43 --> 00:03:48
earlier than Cantor, actually, because, I mean, you can go all the way

00:03:47 --> 00:03:51
back to Ancient Greek times when Aristotle

00:03:51 --> 00:03:56
mphasized the potential aspect of infinity as opposed

00:03:55 --> 00:04:00
to the impossibility, according to him, of achieving an actual

00:03:59 --> 00:04:03
infinity. And Archimedes' method of exhaustion where

00:04:02 --> 00:04:07
he is trying to understand the area of a region

00:04:06 --> 00:04:11
by carving it into more and more triangles, say, and

00:04:10 --> 00:04:15
sort of exhausting the area and thereby understanding the total area in terms

00:04:14 --> 00:04:18
of the sum of the areas of the pieces that he put into it.

00:04:18 --> 00:04:23
And it proceeded on this kind of potential understanding of infinity for hundreds of years,

00:04:22 --> 00:04:26
thousands of years. Almost all mathematicians were

00:04:26 --> 00:04:30
almost all mathematicians were potentialists only and

00:04:30 --> 00:04:34
thought that it was incoherent to speak of an actual

00:04:33 --> 00:04:42
infinity at all. Galileo is an extremely prominent exception to this, though he

00:04:41 --> 00:04:45
argued against this sort of potentialist orthodoxy

00:04:45 --> 00:04:49
in The Dialogue of Two New Sciences. Really lovely

00:04:48 --> 00:04:57
account there that he gave. And that the... In many ways, Galileo was anticipating

00:04:56 --> 00:05:01
Cantor's developments, except he couldn't quite push it all the way

00:05:00 --> 00:05:06
through and ended up throwing up his hands in confusion

00:05:06 --> 00:05:11
in a sense. I mean, the Galileo paradox is the idea or the

00:05:10 --> 00:05:15
observation that if you think about the natural numbers,

00:05:14 --> 00:05:19
I would start with zero but I think maybe he would start with one. The numbers one,

00:05:18 --> 00:05:22
two, three, four, and so on, and you think about which of those

00:05:22 --> 00:05:26
numbers are perfect squares. So zero squared is

00:05:25 --> 00:05:30
zero and one squared is one and two squared is four, three

00:05:29 --> 00:05:34
squared is nine, 16, 25, and so on. And Galileo

00:05:33 --> 00:05:38
bserved that, that the perfect squares can be

00:05:37 --> 00:05:41
put into a one-to-one correspondence with all of the

00:05:41 --> 00:05:45
numbers. I mean, we just did it. I associated every number

00:05:44 --> 00:05:49
with its square. And so it seems

00:05:48 --> 00:05:53
like on the basis of this one-to-one correspondence that

00:05:52 --> 00:05:56
here should be exactly the same number of

00:05:56 --> 00:06:00
squares, perfect squares as there are numbers, and

00:05:59 --> 00:06:04
yet there's all the gaps in between the perfect squares,

00:06:03 --> 00:06:11
right? And, and this suggests that there should be fewer perfect squares, more

00:06:10 --> 00:06:15
numbers than squares because the numbers include all the squares plus a lot more in

00:06:14 --> 00:06:21
between them, right? And Galileo was quite troubled by this observation because

00:06:21 --> 00:06:25
he took it to cause a kind of incoherence in the

00:06:24 --> 00:06:29
comparison of infinite quantities, right? And another example

00:06:28 --> 00:06:33
is, if you take two line segments of different lengths,

00:06:33 --> 00:06:38
and you can imagine drawing a kind of foliation,

00:06:37 --> 00:06:42
a fan of lines that connect them. So the endpoints are

00:06:41 --> 00:06:46
matched from the shorter to the longer segment, and the midpoints are matched and

00:06:45 --> 00:06:49
so on. So spreading out the lines as you go. And so every

00:06:48 --> 00:06:53
point on the shorter line would be associated with

00:06:52 --> 00:06:56
a, a unique distinct point on the longer line in a

00:06:55 --> 00:07:00
one-to-one way. And so it seems like the two

00:06:59 --> 00:07:04
line segments have the same number of points on them because of that, even

00:07:03 --> 00:07:08
though the longer one is longer. And so it

00:07:07 --> 00:07:12
makes, again, a kind of confusion over our ideas about infinity. And

00:07:11 --> 00:07:14
also with two circles, if you just place them

00:07:13 --> 00:07:18
concentrically and draw the rays from the center,

00:07:17 --> 00:07:21
then every point on the smaller circle is associated

00:07:21 --> 00:07:25
with a corresponding point on the larger circle, you know, in a

00:07:25 --> 00:07:29
one-to-one way. And, and again, that seems to show that the

00:07:28 --> 00:07:33
smaller circle has the same number of points on it as the larger one, precisely

00:07:32 --> 00:07:37
because they can be put into this one-to-one correspondence.

00:07:36 --> 00:07:41
Of course, the contemporary attitude about this situation is that those two

00:07:40 --> 00:07:45
infinities are exactly the same, and that Galileo was right in those

00:07:44 --> 00:07:48
observations about the equinumerosity. We would talk about it now

00:07:47 --> 00:07:52
by appealing to what I call the Cantor-Hume principle, or some people

00:07:51 --> 00:07:56
just call it Hume's principle, which is the idea that if you have two

00:07:55 --> 00:08:00
collections, whether they're finite or infinite, then we want to

00:07:59 --> 00:08:03
say that those two collections have the same size. They're

00:08:03 --> 00:08:07
quinumerous if and only if there's a one-to-one

00:08:06 --> 00:08:11
correspondence between those collections. Galileo was

00:08:10 --> 00:08:15
observing that line segments of different lengths are equinumerous,

00:08:14 --> 00:08:19
and the perfect squares are equinumerous with all of the

00:08:18 --> 00:08:23
natural numbers, and any two circles are equinumerous, and so

00:08:22 --> 00:08:26
n. The tension between the Cantor-Hume

00:08:25 --> 00:08:30
principle and what could be called Euclid's principle, which is that the

00:08:30 --> 00:08:35
whole is always greater than the part, is a principle that Euclid

00:08:34 --> 00:08:38
appealed to in the Elements many times when he's calculating

00:08:38 --> 00:08:42
area and so on. It's a basic idea

00:08:42 --> 00:08:46
that if something is just a part of another thing, then the

00:08:45 --> 00:08:50
whole is greater than the part. So what Galileo

00:08:50 --> 00:08:54
was troubled by was this tension between

00:08:54 --> 00:08:58
what we call the Cantor-Hume principle and Euclid's

00:08:57 --> 00:09:02
principle. It wasn't fully resolved, I

00:09:01 --> 00:09:06
think, until Cantor. He's the one who really explained

00:09:05 --> 00:09:10
so clearly about these different sizes of infinity and so on

00:09:09 --> 00:09:13
in a way that was so compelling. He

00:09:13 --> 00:09:17
xhibited two different infinite sets and proved that

00:09:17 --> 00:09:21
hey're not equinumerous; they can't be put into one-to-one

00:09:20 --> 00:09:24
correspondence. It's traditional to talk about the

00:09:23 --> 00:09:28
uncountability of the real numbers. Cantor's big result was that the

00:09:27 --> 00:09:32
set of all real numbers is an uncountable set. Maybe if we're

00:09:31 --> 00:09:36
going to talk about countable sets, then I would suggest that we talk

00:09:35 --> 00:09:40
about Hilbert's Hotel, which really makes that idea perfectly clear.

00:09:39 --> 00:09:42
- Yeah, let's talk about Hilbert's Hotel.

00:09:41 --> 00:09:46
- Hilbert's Hotel is a hotel with infinitely many rooms. Each

00:09:45 --> 00:09:50
room is a full floor suite. So there's floor zero... I always

00:09:49 --> 00:09:54
tart with zero because for me, the natural numbers start with zero, although that's

00:09:54 --> 00:09:58
maybe a point of contention for some mathematicians. The other

00:09:57 --> 00:09:59
mathematicians are wrong.

00:09:58 --> 00:10:02
- Like I mentioned, I'm a programmer, so starting at zero is a wonderful place to start.

00:10:01 --> 00:10:06
- Exactly. So there's floor zero, floor one, floor two, or room zero,

00:10:05 --> 00:10:10
one, two, three, and so on, just like the natural numbers. So Hilbert's Hotel has a

00:10:09 --> 00:10:12
room for every natural number,

00:10:13 --> 00:10:17
and it's completely full. There's a person occupying room N for

00:10:17 --> 00:10:21
every N. But meanwhile, a new guest comes up to the

00:10:20 --> 00:10:25
desk and wants a room. "Can I have a room, please?" The manager says,

00:10:24 --> 00:10:27
"Hang on a second, just give me a moment."

00:10:27 --> 00:10:32
You see, when the other guests had checked in, they had to sign an

00:10:31 --> 00:10:36
agreement with the hotel that maybe

00:10:35 --> 00:10:39
there would be some changing of the rooms during this stay.

00:10:39 --> 00:10:44
So the manager sent a message up to all the current

00:10:43 --> 00:10:48
occupants and told every person, "Hey, can you

00:10:47 --> 00:10:52
move up one room, please?" So the person in room five would move to

00:10:51 --> 00:10:55
room six, and the person in room six would move to room seven and so on. And everyone

00:10:55 --> 00:10:59
moved at the same time. And of course, we never want to be placing two different

00:10:58 --> 00:11:03
guests in the same room, and we want everyone to have their own private room

00:11:02 --> 00:11:07
and... But when you move everyone up one room, then the bottom

00:11:06 --> 00:11:11
room, room zero, becomes available, of course. And so he can put the new guest in

00:11:10 --> 00:11:14
that room. So even when you have infinitely many

00:11:13 --> 00:11:18
things, then the new guest can be accommodated. And that's a way of

00:11:17 --> 00:11:22
showing how the particular infinity of the occupants of

00:11:21 --> 00:11:26
Hilbert's Hotel, it violates Euclid's principle. I

00:11:25 --> 00:11:29
mean, it exactly illustrates this idea because

00:11:28 --> 00:11:33
adding one more element to a set didn't make it larger, because we

00:11:32 --> 00:11:37
can still have a one-to-one correspondence between the total new

00:11:36 --> 00:11:41
guests and the old guests by the room number, right?

00:11:40 --> 00:11:45
- So, to just say one more time, the hotel is full.

00:11:45 --> 00:11:46
- The hotel is full.

00:11:46 --> 00:11:50
- And then you could still squeeze in one more, and that breaks

00:11:50 --> 00:11:55
the traditional notion of mathematics and breaks

00:11:54 --> 00:11:59
people's brains when they try to think about infinity, I suppose. This is

00:11:58 --> 00:12:00
a property of infinity.

00:11:59 --> 00:12:04
- It's a property of infinity that sometimes when you add an

00:12:03 --> 00:12:08
element to a set, it doesn't get larger. That's what this

00:12:07 --> 00:12:12
example shows. But one can go on with Hilbert's

00:12:11 --> 00:12:16
Hotel, for example. I mean, maybe the next day, you know,

00:12:15 --> 00:12:20
20 people show up all at once. We can easily do the same trick again, just move

00:12:19 --> 00:12:24
verybody up 20 rooms. And then we would have 20

00:12:23 --> 00:12:27
empty rooms at the bottom, and those new 20 guests could go

00:12:26 --> 00:12:34
in. But on the following weekend, a giant bus pulled up, Hilbert's

00:12:33 --> 00:12:38
bus. And Hilbert's bus has, of course,

00:12:37 --> 00:12:41
infinitely many seats. There's Seat Zero, Seat One, Seat Two, Seat Three, and so

00:12:41 --> 00:12:46
n. And so one wants to... You know, all the people on the bus want

00:12:45 --> 00:12:50
o check into the hotel, but the hotel is completely full. So what is the manager going to

00:12:49 --> 00:12:54
do? And when I talk about Hilbert's Hotel,

00:12:53 --> 00:12:57
when I teach Hilbert's Hotel in class, I always demand that the

00:12:56 --> 00:13:01
students provide, you know, the explanation of- of how to do it.

00:13:00 --> 00:13:03
So maybe I'll ask you.

00:13:02 --> 00:13:07
Can you tell me, yeah, what is your idea about how to fit them all in the hotel, everyone

00:13:06 --> 00:13:09
on the bus, and also the current occupants?

00:13:08 --> 00:13:15
- You separate the hotel into even and odd rooms, and you squeeze in the new

00:13:15 --> 00:13:20
Hilbert bus people into the odd rooms and the previous occupants

00:13:19 --> 00:13:21
go into the even rooms.

00:13:20 --> 00:13:24
- That's exactly right. That's a very easy way to do it. If you

00:13:23 --> 00:13:28
just tell all the current guests to double their room number, so in

00:13:27 --> 00:13:32
Room N, you move to Room 2 times N. So they're all going to get their own

00:13:31 --> 00:13:36
private room, the new room, and it will always be an even number 'cause 2 times N is always an even

00:13:35 --> 00:13:40
umber. And so all the odd rooms become empty that way. And now we can

00:13:39 --> 00:13:43
put the bus occupants into the odd-numbered rooms.

00:13:42 --> 00:13:48
- And by doing so, you have now shoved an infinity into another infinity.

00:13:47 --> 00:13:52
- That's right. So what it really shows... I mean, another way of thinking

00:13:51 --> 00:13:55
about it is that, well, we can define that a set is

00:13:54 --> 00:13:59
countable if it is equinumerous with a set of natural

00:13:58 --> 00:14:03
numbers. And a kind of easy way to understand what that's

00:14:02 --> 00:14:04
aying in terms of Hilbert's Hotel is that

00:14:04 --> 00:14:09
a set is countable if it fits into Hilbert's Hotel, 'cause Hilbert's Hotel

00:14:08 --> 00:14:13
basically is the set of natural numbers in terms of the room numbers. So to be

00:14:12 --> 00:14:17
quinumerous with a set of natural numbers is just the same thing as to fit into Hilbert's

00:14:16 --> 00:14:19
Hotel. And so what we've shown is that

00:14:20 --> 00:14:25
if you have two countably infinite sets, then their

00:14:24 --> 00:14:29
union is also countably infinite. If you put them together and form a new set

00:14:28 --> 00:14:33
with all of the elements of either of them, then that union set is still

00:14:33 --> 00:14:36
only countably infinite. It didn't get bigger.

00:14:36 --> 00:14:41
And that's a remarkable property for a notion

00:14:40 --> 00:14:44
of infinity to have, I suppose. But if you thought that there was only

00:14:43 --> 00:14:48
one kind of infinity, then it wouldn't be surprising at all, because if you take

00:14:48 --> 00:14:52
two infinite sets and put them together, then it's still infinite. And so if there were only one kind of

00:14:51 --> 00:14:54
infinity, then it shouldn't be surprising...

00:14:53 --> 00:14:58
that the union of two countable sets is countable. So there's another way

00:14:57 --> 00:15:02
to push this a bit harder, and that is when Hilbert's train

00:15:02 --> 00:15:09
arrives, and Hilbert's train has infinitely many train cars...

00:15:08 --> 00:15:14
and each train car has infinitely many seats.

00:15:13 --> 00:15:17
And so we have an infinity of infinities of the train

00:15:16 --> 00:15:21
passengers together with the current occupants of the

00:15:20 --> 00:15:25
hotel, and everybody on the train wants to check in to Hilbert's Hotel.

00:15:26 --> 00:15:30
So the manager can, again, of course, send a message up to all the

00:15:30 --> 00:15:34
rooms telling every person to double

00:15:33 --> 00:15:38
their room number again. And so that will occupy all the

00:15:37 --> 00:15:41
ven-numbered rooms again, and but free up again the

00:15:41 --> 00:15:45
odd-numbered rooms. So somehow, we want to put the train passengers

00:15:45 --> 00:15:50
into the odd-numbered rooms. And so while

00:15:49 --> 00:15:55
very train passenger is on some car, let's say Car C and Seat S,

00:15:55 --> 00:16:00
so somehow, we have to take these two coordinates, you know, C,

00:15:59 --> 00:16:04
S, the car number and the seat number, and produce from

00:16:03 --> 00:16:07
it an odd number in a one-to-one way, you

00:16:06 --> 00:16:11
know? And that's actually not very difficult. In

00:16:10 --> 00:16:17
fact, one can just use, say... An easy way to do it is to just use the number

00:16:18 --> 00:16:26
3 to the C times 5 to the S. 3 to the C, 3 to the car number, so 3 x 3 x

00:16:25 --> 00:16:30
3, you know, the number of the car. You multiply 3 by itself, the

00:16:29 --> 00:16:34
number of the train car, and then you multiply 5 by itself

00:16:33 --> 00:16:38
the seat number of times, and then you multiply those two numbers together. So

00:16:37 --> 00:16:42
3 to the C times 5 to the S. That's

00:16:41 --> 00:16:45
always an odd number, 'cause the prime factorization has only 3s and

00:16:45 --> 00:16:49
5s in it. There's no 2 there. So therefore, it's

00:16:48 --> 00:16:53
definitely an odd number, and it's always different because

00:16:53 --> 00:16:57
of the uniqueness of prime factorization. So every number

00:16:57 --> 00:17:01
can be factored uniquely into primes. So if you have a number of that form,

00:17:00 --> 00:17:05
then you can just factor it, and that tells you the exponent on 3 and the

00:17:04 --> 00:17:09
xponent on 5. And so you know exactly which person it was, which car

00:17:08 --> 00:17:11
they came from, and which seat they came from.

00:17:10 --> 00:17:15
- And prime factorization is every single number can be

00:17:15 --> 00:17:20
decomposed into the atoms of mathematics, which is the prime

00:17:19 --> 00:17:24
numbers. You can multiply them together to achieve that number.

00:17:23 --> 00:17:28
And that's prime factorization. You're showing 3 and 5 are both prime numbers,

00:17:28 --> 00:17:34
odd. So through this magical formula, you can deal with this

00:17:35 --> 00:17:42
train, infinite number of cars, with each car having infinite number of seats.

00:17:41 --> 00:17:46
- Exactly right. We've proved that if you have countably many

00:17:45 --> 00:17:49
countable sets, then the union of those

00:17:48 --> 00:17:53
ts, putting all those sets together into one giant set,

00:17:52 --> 00:17:56
is still countable. You know, because the train cars are each countable, plus the current

00:17:55 --> 00:18:00
hotel. It's sort of like another train car, if you want to think about it that way. The current

00:17:59 --> 00:18:04
occupants of the hotel could, you know, have the same number as

00:18:03 --> 00:18:08
any of the train cars. So putting countably many

00:18:07 --> 00:18:12
countable sets together to make one big union set is

00:18:11 --> 00:18:16
till countable. It's quite remarkable, I think. I mean when

00:18:15 --> 00:18:20
I first learned this many, many years ago, I was completely shocked by it and

00:18:19 --> 00:18:24
transfixed by it. It was quite amazing to me that this notion

00:18:23 --> 00:18:27
of countable infinity could be closed under this process of

00:18:26 --> 00:18:31
infinitely many infinities adding up still to the very same

00:18:30 --> 00:18:35
infinity, which is a strong instance, a

00:18:34 --> 00:18:39
strong violation of Euclid's principle once again, right? So, the new

00:18:38 --> 00:18:43
set that we built is... has many more elements than the old

00:18:42 --> 00:18:47
set in the sense that there are additional elements, but it doesn't have many

00:18:46 --> 00:18:51
more elements in terms of its size because it's still just a countable

00:18:50 --> 00:18:54
infinity and it fits into Hilbert's Hotel.

00:18:53 --> 00:18:58
- Have you been able to sort of internalize a good intuition about

00:18:58 --> 00:19:05
countable infinity? Because that is a pretty weird thing. You can have a countably

00:19:04 --> 00:19:09
infinite set of countably infinite sets, and you can shove it

00:19:08 --> 00:19:12
all in and it still is a countable infinite set.

00:19:11 --> 00:19:16
- Yeah, that's exactly right. I mean, I guess,

00:19:15 --> 00:19:20
of course, when you work with these notions, the argument of

00:19:19 --> 00:19:24
Hilbert's Hotel becomes kind of clear. There are many other ways to

00:19:23 --> 00:19:28
talk about it too. For example, let's think about, say,

00:19:27 --> 00:19:32
the integer lattice, the grid of points that you

00:19:31 --> 00:19:36
get by taking pairs of natural numbers, say, so the upper

00:19:35 --> 00:19:38
ight quadrant of the integer lattice, yeah?

00:19:38 --> 00:19:43
So there's the, you know, row zero, row one, row two and so on, column zero, column one, column two and so on,

00:19:42 --> 00:19:50
and each row and column has a countable infinity of points on it, right?

00:19:50 --> 00:19:54
So those dots, if you think about them as dots,

00:19:54 --> 00:19:58
are really the same as the train cars if you think about each column

00:19:58 --> 00:20:02
of... in that integer lattice, it's a countable infinity. It's like one

00:20:02 --> 00:20:06
train car and then there's the next train car next to it, and then the next column

00:20:05 --> 00:20:08
ext to that, the next train car.

00:20:08 --> 00:20:13
And so, but if we think about it in this grid manner, then I can imagine

00:20:12 --> 00:20:17
a kind of winding path winding through these grid

00:20:16 --> 00:20:20
points, like up and down the diagonals.

00:20:19 --> 00:20:24
Winding back and forth. So I start at the corner point and then I go down,

00:20:23 --> 00:20:28
up and to the left, and then down and to the right, up and to the left, down and to the right, and so on,

00:20:28 --> 00:20:35
in such a way that I'm going to hit every grid point on this path.

00:20:34 --> 00:20:39
So, this gives me a way of assigning room numbers to the points.

00:20:38 --> 00:20:43
Because every grid point is going to be the

00:20:42 --> 00:20:47
Nth point on that path for some N. And that

00:20:46 --> 00:20:50
gives a correspondence between the grid points and the natural numbers

00:20:49 --> 00:20:54
themselves. So it's a kind of different picture. Before, we used this 3

00:20:53 --> 00:20:58
to the C, 5 times 5 to the S, which is a kind of, you

00:20:57 --> 00:21:02
know, overly arithmetic way to think about it. But there's a kind

00:21:01 --> 00:21:06
of direct way to understand that it's still a

00:21:05 --> 00:21:09
countable infinity when you have countably many countable sets, because you can just

00:21:09 --> 00:21:14
art putting them on this list. And as long as you give each of the infinite collections a

00:21:13 --> 00:21:18
chance to add one more person to the list, then

00:21:17 --> 00:21:21
you're going to accommodate everyone in any of the sets in one list.

00:21:20 --> 00:21:25
- Yeah, it's a really nice visual way to think about it. You just zigzag your way across the grid

00:21:25 --> 00:21:30
to make sure everybody's included. That gives you an algorithm for including

00:21:29 --> 00:21:34
everybody. So, can you speak to the uncountable infinities?

00:21:33 --> 00:21:34
- Yeah, absolutely.

00:21:33 --> 00:21:36
- What are the integers and the real numbers-

00:21:35 --> 00:21:36
Correct

00:21:36 --> 00:21:39
- and what is the line that Cantor was able to find?

00:21:38 --> 00:21:44
- Maybe there's one more step I want to insert before doing that.

00:21:43 --> 00:21:51
Which is the rational numbers. So we did pairs of natural numbers.

00:21:50 --> 00:21:55
Right? That's the train car, basically. But maybe it's a little

00:21:54 --> 00:21:58
bit informative to think about the rational, the fractions, the set of

00:21:57 --> 00:22:00
ractions, or rational numbers,

00:22:00 --> 00:22:05
because a lot of people maybe have an expectation that maybe this is a bigger

00:22:04 --> 00:22:09
infinity because the rational numbers are densely ordered.

00:22:08 --> 00:22:13
Between any two fractions, you can find another fraction, right?

00:22:12 --> 00:22:18
The average of two fractions is another fraction. And so, sometimes people,

00:22:18 --> 00:22:23
it seems to be a different character than the

00:22:22 --> 00:22:27
integers, which are discretely ordered, right? From any integer, there's a next one and a

00:22:26 --> 00:22:30
previous one, and so on. But that's not true in the rational

00:22:29 --> 00:22:34
numbers. And yet, the rational numbers are

00:22:33 --> 00:22:40
also still only a countable infinity. And the way to see that is actually

00:22:40 --> 00:22:44
it's just exactly the same as Hilbert's train again, because every fraction

00:22:43 --> 00:22:48
consists of two integers: the numerator and the

00:22:47 --> 00:22:52
denominator. And so if I tell you two natural

00:22:51 --> 00:22:55
numbers, then you know what fraction I'm talking about. I mean, plus the

00:22:55 --> 00:22:59
sign issue, I mean if it's positive or negative. But if you just think about the

00:22:58 --> 00:23:03
positive fractions, then, you know, you have the numbers of the form

00:23:02 --> 00:23:07
P over Q, where Q is not zero. So you can still do

00:23:06 --> 00:23:11
3 to the P times 5 to the Q. The same idea works.

00:23:11 --> 00:23:15
with the rational numbers. So this is still a countable

00:23:14 --> 00:23:18
set. And you might think, "Well, every set

00:23:18 --> 00:23:23
is going to be countable because there's only one infinity." I mean, if that's a kind of

00:23:22 --> 00:23:27
perspective maybe that you're adopting, but it's not

00:23:26 --> 00:23:31
rue, and that's the profound achievement that Cantor made is proving that the set of

00:23:30 --> 00:23:34
real numbers is not a countable infinity. It's a strictly larger

00:23:33 --> 00:23:38
infinity, and therefore there's more than one

00:23:37 --> 00:23:41
concept of infinity, more than one size of infinity.

00:23:40 --> 00:23:45
- So let's talk about the real numbers. What are the real numbers? Why do they break infinity?

00:23:44 --> 00:23:50
The countable infinity. Looking it up on Perplexity, real numbers

00:23:49 --> 00:23:54
include all the numbers that can be represented on the number line, encompassing both

00:23:53 --> 00:23:58
rational and irrational numbers. We've spoken about the rational numbers, and the

00:23:57 --> 00:24:02
rational numbers, by the way, are by definition, the numbers that can be represented

00:24:01 --> 00:24:06
as a fraction of two integers.

00:24:05 --> 00:24:10
- That's right. So with the real numbers, we have the algebraic numbers. We

00:24:09 --> 00:24:14
have, of course, all the rational numbers. The integers and the rationals are all part of the

00:24:13 --> 00:24:17
real number system. But then also, we have the algebraic numbers like the square root of

00:24:16 --> 00:24:21
2 or the cube root of 5 and so on. Numbers that solve an

00:24:20 --> 00:24:25
algebraic equation over the integers, those are known as algebraic

00:24:24 --> 00:24:28
numbers. It was an open question for a long time whether that was

00:24:27 --> 00:24:32
all of the real numbers or whether there would

00:24:31 --> 00:24:36
exist numbers that are the transcendental numbers. The

00:24:35 --> 00:24:39
transcendental numbers are real numbers that are not algebraic.

00:24:38 --> 00:24:43
- And we won't even go to the surreal numbers about which you have a wonderful blog post.

00:24:42 --> 00:24:44
We'll talk about that a little bit later.

00:24:43 --> 00:24:48
- Oh, great. So it was Liouville who first proved that

00:24:47 --> 00:24:52
here are transcendental numbers, and he exhibited a very specific number

00:24:51 --> 00:24:56
that's now known as the Liouville constant, which is a transcendental

00:24:55 --> 00:25:00
number. Cantor also famously proved that there are many,

00:24:59 --> 00:25:04
many transcendental numbers. In fact, it follows from his

00:25:03 --> 00:25:08
argument on the uncountability of the real numbers that there are

00:25:07 --> 00:25:13
uncountably many transcendental numbers. So most real numbers are transcendental.

00:25:12 --> 00:25:16
- And again, going to Perplexity, "Transcendental numbers are 'real' or 'complex'

00:25:15 --> 00:25:20
numbers; they are not the root of any nonzero polynomial with

00:25:19 --> 00:25:24
integer or rational coefficients. This means they cannot

00:25:23 --> 00:25:28
be expressed as solutions to algebraic equations with integer coefficients,

00:25:27 --> 00:25:31
setting them apart from algebraic numbers."

00:25:31 --> 00:25:36
- So some of the famous transcendental numbers would include the number pi,

00:25:35 --> 00:25:41
you know, the 3.14159265 and so on.

00:25:41 --> 00:25:45
So that's a transcendental number. Also, Euler's constant, the

00:25:44 --> 00:25:48
, like e to the x, the exponential function.

00:25:47 --> 00:25:52
- So you could say that some of the sexiest numbers in mathematics are all transcendental numbers?

00:25:51 --> 00:25:56
- Absolutely. That's true. Yeah, yeah. Although, you know, I don't know, square root of two is

00:25:55 --> 00:25:56
pretty

00:25:56 --> 00:26:01
- Square root. All right. So it depends. Let's not... Beauty can be found in

00:26:00 --> 00:26:03
all the different kinds of sets, but yeah.

00:26:02 --> 00:26:07
- That's right. And if you have a kind of simplicity attitude, then zero and one are looking pretty good too,

00:26:06 --> 00:26:08
so... And they're definitely not

00:26:07 --> 00:26:11
- Sorry to take that tangent, but what is your favorite number? Do you have one?

00:26:10 --> 00:26:13
- Oh, gosh. You know-

00:26:12 --> 00:26:14
Is it zero?

00:26:13 --> 00:26:18
- Did you know there's a proof that every number is

00:26:17 --> 00:26:23
interesting? You can prove it, because...

00:26:22 --> 00:26:24
- Yeah? What's that proof look like?

00:26:23 --> 00:26:24
- Yeah, okay.

00:26:23 --> 00:26:25
- How do you even begin?

00:26:24 --> 00:26:26
- I'm gonna prove to you-

00:26:25 --> 00:26:26
Okay

00:26:26 --> 00:26:29
- ...that every natural number is interesting.

00:26:28 --> 00:26:29
- Okay.

00:26:29 --> 00:26:33
- Yeah. I mean, zero's interesting because it's the additive identity, right?

00:26:32 --> 00:26:37
That's pretty interesting. And one is the multiplicative identity, so when you

00:26:36 --> 00:26:41
multiply it by any other number, you just get that number back, right? And

00:26:40 --> 00:26:47
two is, you know, the first prime number. That's super interesting, right? And-

00:26:46 --> 00:26:51
Okay. So one can go on this way and give specific reasons, but I wanna

00:26:50 --> 00:26:55
prove as a general principle that every number is interesting. And this is

00:26:54 --> 00:27:03
the proof. Suppose, toward contradiction, that there were some boring numbers.

00:27:02 --> 00:27:04
Okay?

00:27:03 --> 00:27:05
- Oh, okay.

00:27:04 --> 00:27:07
- But if there was an uninteresting number-

00:27:06 --> 00:27:07
Yes

00:27:07 --> 00:27:12
- ...then there would have to be a smallest uninteresting number.

00:27:11 --> 00:27:14
- Mm-hmm. Yes.

00:27:13 --> 00:27:18
- But that's a contradiction, because the smallest uninteresting number is a super

00:27:17 --> 00:27:24
interesting property to have. So therefore-

00:27:23 --> 00:27:25
Ah, that's good.

00:27:24 --> 00:27:27
- ...there cannot be any boring numbers.

00:27:26 --> 00:27:31
- I'm gonna have to try to find a hole in that proof- because there's a lot of

00:27:30 --> 00:27:34
baked in in the word interesting, but yeah, that's a beautiful.

00:27:33 --> 00:27:35
- Right.

00:27:34 --> 00:27:39
- That doesn't say anything about the transcendental numbers, about the real numbers that you

00:27:38 --> 00:27:40
just proved from just-

00:27:39 --> 00:27:40
That's right

00:27:39 --> 00:27:40
- ...four natural numbers.

00:27:40 --> 00:27:43
- Yeah. Okay, should we get back to Cantor's argument, or-

00:27:42 --> 00:27:44
Sure.

00:27:43 --> 00:27:47
You've masterfully avoided the question. Well, you basically said, "I love all

00:27:46 --> 00:27:48
numbers."

00:27:47 --> 00:27:49
- Yeah, basically.

00:27:48 --> 00:27:50
- Is that what you said?

00:27:49 --> 00:27:50
- Yeah. That was my intention.

00:27:49 --> 00:27:52
- Back to Cantor's argument. Let's go.

00:27:51 --> 00:27:56
- Okay, so Cantor wants to prove that

00:27:55 --> 00:28:00
he infinity of the real numbers is different and

00:27:59 --> 00:28:04
strictly larger than the infinity of the natural numbers. So the natural

00:28:03 --> 00:28:07
numbers are the numbers that start with zero and add one

00:28:06 --> 00:28:11
successively, so zero, one, two, three, and so on. And the real numbers, as

00:28:10 --> 00:28:15
we said, are the numbers that come from the number

00:28:14 --> 00:28:19
line, including all the integers and the rationals and the algebraic numbers and

00:28:18 --> 00:28:22
the transcendental numbers and all of those numbers altogether. Now,

00:28:21 --> 00:28:26
obviously, since the natural numbers are included in the real numbers,

00:28:25 --> 00:28:30
we know that the real numbers are at least as large as

00:28:29 --> 00:28:34
the natural numbers. And so the claim that we want to prove is that

00:28:34 --> 00:28:40
it's strictly larger. So suppose that it wasn't strictly larger. So

00:28:40 --> 00:28:45
then they would have the same size. But to have the same size, remember,

00:28:44 --> 00:28:49
means by definition that there's a one-to-one

00:28:48 --> 00:28:53
correspondence between them. So we suppose that the real

00:28:52 --> 00:28:57
numbers can be put into one-to-one correspondence with the natural numbers.

00:28:57 --> 00:29:02
So therefore, for every natural number N, we have a real number, let's call it R sub N.

00:29:01 --> 00:29:06
R sub N is the Nth real number on the

00:29:05 --> 00:29:10
list. Basically, our assumption allows us to think of the real numbers as

00:29:09 --> 00:29:14
having been placed on a list, R1, R2, and so on.

00:29:13 --> 00:29:17
Okay, and now I'm going to define the number Z, and it's going to

00:29:16 --> 00:29:21
be... The integer part is going to be a zero, and then I'm going to

00:29:20 --> 00:29:25
put a decimal place, and then I'm going to start specifying the digits of this number

00:29:24 --> 00:29:29
Z. D1, D2, D3, and so on. And what

00:29:28 --> 00:29:33
I'm going to make sure is that the Nth digit after the

00:29:32 --> 00:29:41
decimal point of Z is different from the Nth digit of the Nth number on the list.

00:29:40 --> 00:29:44
Okay? So, to specify the Nth digit of Z, I

00:29:44 --> 00:29:48
go to the Nth number on the list, R sub N, and I look at its Nth

00:29:47 --> 00:29:52
digit after the decimal point. And whatever that digit

00:29:51 --> 00:29:57
is, I make sure that my digit is different from it. Okay?

00:29:57 --> 00:30:02
And then I want to do something a little bit more, and that is

00:30:01 --> 00:30:06
I'm going to make it different in a way that I'm never using the

00:30:05 --> 00:30:11
digits zero or nine. I'm just always using the other digits and not zero

00:30:10 --> 00:30:41
and it would form a kind of diagonal going down and to the right, and that,

00:30:41 --> 00:30:45
for that reason, this argument is called the diagonal argument because we're looking at the

00:30:44 --> 00:30:49
Nth digit of the Nth number, and those exist on a kind of diagonal going

00:30:48 --> 00:30:52
down. And we've made our number Z

00:30:52 --> 00:30:58
so that the Nth digit of Z is different from the Nth digit of the Nth number.

00:30:58 --> 00:31:04
But now it follows that Z is not on the list because

00:31:04 --> 00:31:09
Z is different from R1 because, well,

00:31:08 --> 00:31:13
the first digit after the decimal point of Z is different from the first

00:31:12 --> 00:31:16
digit of R1 after the decimal point. That's exactly how we built

00:31:15 --> 00:31:20
it. And the second digit of Z is different from the second digit

00:31:19 --> 00:31:24
of R2 and so on. The Nth digit of Z is different

00:31:23 --> 00:31:27
from the Nth digit of R sub N for every N. So

00:31:26 --> 00:31:31
therefore, Z is not equal to any of these numbers R sub N. And

00:31:32 --> 00:31:37
but that's a contradiction because we had assumed that we had every real number

00:31:36 --> 00:31:40
on the list, but yet here is a real number Z that's not on the

00:31:39 --> 00:31:44
list, okay? And so that's the main contradiction.

00:31:43 --> 00:31:46
- And so it's a kind of proof by construction.

00:31:45 --> 00:31:49
- Exactly. So given a list of numbers, Cantor's

00:31:48 --> 00:31:53
proving... It's interesting that you say that actually, because there's a kind of philosophical

00:31:52 --> 00:31:57
controversy that occurs in connection with

00:31:56 --> 00:32:01
is observation about whether Cantor's construction is constructive or not.

00:32:00 --> 00:32:04
Given a list of numbers, Cantor gives us a specific

00:32:03 --> 00:32:08
means of constructing a real number that's not on the list, is a

00:32:07 --> 00:32:15
way of thinking about it. There's this one aspect, which I alluded to earlier, but

00:32:14 --> 00:32:17
some real numbers have more than one decimal

00:32:16 --> 00:32:21
representation, and it causes this slight problem

00:32:21 --> 00:32:28
in the argument. For example, the number one, you can write it as 1.0000

00:32:27 --> 00:32:35
forever, but you can also write it as 0.999 forever. Those are two

00:32:34 --> 00:32:39
different decimal representations of exactly the same number.

00:32:38 --> 00:32:42
- You beautifully got rid of the zeros and the nines. Therefore, we don't need to even consider that,

00:32:42 --> 00:32:45
and the proof still works.

00:32:44 --> 00:32:49
- Exactly, because the only kind of case where that phenomenon occurs is

00:32:48 --> 00:32:53
when the number is eventually zero or eventually nine. And so since our

00:32:52 --> 00:32:56
number Z never had any zeros or nines in it, it wasn't one of those

00:32:55 --> 00:33:00
numbers. And so actually, in those cases, we didn't need to do anything special to

00:32:59 --> 00:33:04
diagonalize. Just the mere fact that our number has a unique representation

00:33:03 --> 00:33:08
already means that it's not equal to those numbers. So maybe it

00:33:07 --> 00:33:12
was controversial in Cantor's day more than 100 years ago, but I think it's

00:33:11 --> 00:33:16
most commonly looked at today as, you know, one of the

00:33:15 --> 00:33:19
initial main results in set theory, and it's

00:33:19 --> 00:33:23
profound and amazing and insightful and the beginning

00:33:22 --> 00:33:27
point of so many later arguments. And this diagonalization

00:33:26 --> 00:33:31
idea has proved to be an extremely fruitful proof method,

00:33:31 --> 00:33:36
and almost every major result in mathematical logic

00:33:35 --> 00:33:39
is using in an abstract way the idea of

00:33:39 --> 00:33:43
diagonalization. It was really the start of so

00:33:42 --> 00:33:46
many other observations that were made, including

00:33:46 --> 00:33:51
Russell's paradox and the halting problem and the recursion

00:33:50 --> 00:33:57
theorem, and so many other principles are using diagonalization at their core. So...

00:33:56 --> 00:33:59
- Can we just step back a little bit?

00:33:58 --> 00:33:59
- Sure.

00:33:58 --> 00:34:07
- This infinity crisis led to a kind of rebuilding of mathematics. So it'd be

00:34:06 --> 00:34:11
nice if you lay out the things it resulted in. So one is

00:34:10 --> 00:34:15
et theory became the foundation of mathematics. All mathematics could now

00:34:14 --> 00:34:17
be built from sets, giving math its first truly rigorous

00:34:16 --> 00:34:21
foundation. The axiomatization

00:34:21 --> 00:34:26
of mathematics, the paradoxes forced mathematicians to develop ZFC and other

00:34:25 --> 00:34:32
axiomatic systems, and mathematical logic emerged. Gödel, Turing, and

00:34:31 --> 00:34:38
others created entire new fields. So can you explain what set theory is and,

00:34:38 --> 00:34:42
how does it serve as a foundation of modern mathematics and maybe even the

00:34:41 --> 00:34:44
foundation of truth?

00:34:43 --> 00:34:47
- That's a great question. Set theory really has two

00:34:47 --> 00:34:52
roles that it's serving. There's kind

00:34:51 --> 00:34:56
of two ways that set theory emerges. On the one hand, set theory

00:34:56 --> 00:35:01
is its own subject of mathematics, with its

00:35:00 --> 00:35:05
own problems and questions and answers and proof

00:35:04 --> 00:35:08
methods. And so really, from this point of view, set theory is

00:35:07 --> 00:35:15
about the transfinite recursive constructions or well-founded definitions and

00:35:14 --> 00:35:19
constructions. And those ideas have been enormously

00:35:18 --> 00:35:23
fruitful and set theorists have looked into them and

00:35:22 --> 00:35:27
eveloped so many ideas coming out of that. But set

00:35:26 --> 00:35:30
heory has also happened to serve in this other

00:35:29 --> 00:35:34
foundational role. It's very common to hear things said about

00:35:33 --> 00:35:38
set theory that really aren't taking account of this distinction between

00:35:37 --> 00:35:42
the two roles that it's serving. It's its own subject, but it's also serving as a

00:35:41 --> 00:35:45
foundation of mathematics. So in its foundational role, set

00:35:45 --> 00:35:50
heory provides a way to think of a collection of things as one thing.

00:35:49 --> 00:35:53
That's the central idea of set theory. A set is a

00:35:52 --> 00:35:57
collection of things, but you think of the set

00:35:56 --> 00:36:01
itself as one abstract thing. So when you form the

00:36:00 --> 00:36:04
set of real numbers, then that is a set. It's one thing. It's a

00:36:03 --> 00:36:08
set, and it has elements inside of it. So it's sort of

00:36:07 --> 00:36:12
like a bag of objects. A set is kind of like a bag of objects. And so we have a

00:36:11 --> 00:36:16
lot of different axioms that describe the nature of this

00:36:15 --> 00:36:21
idea of thinking of a collection of things as one thing itself, one abstract thing.

00:36:20 --> 00:36:28
- And axioms are, I guess, facts that we assume are true, based on which we then

00:36:27 --> 00:36:34
build the ideas of mathematics. So there's a bunch of facts, axioms about sets

00:36:34 --> 00:36:38
that we can put together, and if they're sufficiently......

00:36:37 --> 00:36:43
powerful, we can then build on top of that a lot of really interesting mathematics.

00:36:42 --> 00:36:47
- Yeah, I think that's right. So, I mean, the history of the current set

00:36:46 --> 00:36:51
heory axioms, known as the Zermelo-Fraenkel axioms, came out in the

00:36:50 --> 00:36:55
arly 20th century with Zermelo's idea. I mean, the history is quite

00:36:54 --> 00:37:01
fascinating because Zermelo in 1904 offered a proof

00:37:00 --> 00:37:05
that what's called the axiom of choice implies the

00:37:04 --> 00:37:09
well-order principle. So he described his proof, and that was

00:37:08 --> 00:37:13
extremely controversial at the time. And there was no

00:37:12 --> 00:37:17
theory, there weren't any axioms there. Cantor was not working in an axiomatic

00:37:16 --> 00:37:21
framework. He didn't have a list of axioms in the way that we have for set theory

00:37:20 --> 00:37:25
now, and Zermelo didn't either. And his

00:37:24 --> 00:37:30
ideas were challenged so much with regard to the well-order theorem—

00:37:29 --> 00:37:34
that he was pressed to produce the theory

00:37:33 --> 00:37:38
in which his argument could be formalized, and that was the origin of

00:37:37 --> 00:37:40
what's known as Zermelo set theory.

00:37:39 --> 00:37:44
- And going to perplexity, the axiom of choice is a fundamental principle in set

00:37:43 --> 00:37:47
heory which states that for any collection of non-empty sets, it is

00:37:46 --> 00:37:51
possible to select exactly one element from each set, even

00:37:50 --> 00:37:55
if no explicit rule to make the choices given. This axiom

00:37:54 --> 00:37:59
allows the construction of a new set containing one element from each

00:37:58 --> 00:38:02
original set, even in cases where the collection is infinite

00:38:02 --> 00:38:06
or where there is no natural way to specify a selection

00:38:05 --> 00:38:10
rule. So this was controversial and this

00:38:09 --> 00:38:15
was described before there's even a language for axiomatic systems.

00:38:14 --> 00:38:19
- That's right. So on the one hand, I mean, the axiom of choice principle is

00:38:20 --> 00:38:25
completely obvious that we want this to be true, that it is

00:38:24 --> 00:38:28
true. I mean, a lot of people take it as a law of logic. If you have

00:38:28 --> 00:38:32
a bunch of sets, then there's a way of picking an element from each of them.

00:38:31 --> 00:38:36
There's a

00:38:35 --> 00:38:40
function. If I have a bunch of sets, then there's a function that,

00:38:39 --> 00:38:44
when you apply it to any one of those sets, gives you an element of that set.

00:38:43 --> 00:38:48
It's- it's a completely natural principle. I mean,

00:38:47 --> 00:38:51
it's called the axiom of choice, which is a way of sort of anthropomorphizing the

00:38:50 --> 00:38:55
mathematical idea. It's not like the function is choosing

00:38:54 --> 00:38:59
something. I mean, it's just that if you were to make such choices,

00:38:58 --> 00:39:03
there would be a function that consisted of the choices that you made.

00:39:02 --> 00:39:06
And the difficulty is that when you- when you can't specify a

00:39:05 --> 00:39:10
rule or a procedure by which you're making choices, then it's difficult to say

00:39:09 --> 00:39:13
what the function is that you're asserting exists.

00:39:13 --> 00:39:18
You know, you- you

00:39:17 --> 00:39:22
want to have the view that, well, there is a way of choosing. I don't have an

00:39:21 --> 00:39:26
easy way to say what the function is, but there definitely is one.

00:39:25 --> 00:39:29
Yeah, this is the way of thinking about the axiom of choice.

00:39:28 --> 00:39:32
- So we're going to say the- the- the three letters of ZFC may be a lot on this

00:39:31 --> 00:39:34
conversation. You already mentioned-

00:39:33 --> 00:39:34
Right

00:39:33 --> 00:39:38
- ...Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory, that's the Z and the F and the C in that is this...

00:39:37 --> 00:39:42
Comes from this axiom of choice.

00:39:41 --> 00:39:43
- That's right.

00:39:42 --> 00:39:47
- So ZFC sounds like a super technical thing, but it is the

00:39:46 --> 00:39:50
set of axioms that's the foundation of modern mathematics.

00:39:49 --> 00:39:54
- Yeah, absolutely. So one should be aware also that there's huge parts of

00:39:53 --> 00:39:58
mathematics that don't... That pay attention to whether the axiom of

00:39:57 --> 00:40:01
choice is being used and they don't want to use the axiom of choice, so they work out

00:40:01 --> 00:40:06
he consequences that's- that are possible without the axiom of choice or with weakened

00:40:05 --> 00:40:10
forms of- of Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory and so on. And that's quite

00:40:09 --> 00:40:14
a- there's quite a vibrant amount of work in that area. I mean, but going back to the

00:40:13 --> 00:40:22
axiom of choice for a bit, it's maybe interesting to- to give Russell's description

00:40:21 --> 00:40:25
of how to think about the axiom of choice. So Russell describes,

00:40:24 --> 00:40:33
this rich person who has a- an infinite closet. And in that closet, he has

00:40:32 --> 00:40:41
infinitely many pairs of shoes and he tells his butler to

00:40:40 --> 00:40:44
"Please go and give me one shoe from each pair."

00:40:43 --> 00:40:48
And- and the butler can do this easily because he can... For any pair of shoes, he

00:40:47 --> 00:40:52
can just always pick the left shoe. I mean, there's a way

00:40:51 --> 00:40:56
of picking that we can describe. We always take the left one or

00:40:55 --> 00:41:00
always take the right one, or take the left one if it's a red shoe and the right one if

00:40:59 --> 00:41:04
it's a brown shoe or, you know. We can invent rules that would

00:41:03 --> 00:41:08
result in these kind of choice functions, so we can describe

00:41:07 --> 00:41:12
xplicit choice functions. And for those cases, you don't need the axiom of choice

00:41:11 --> 00:41:16
you don't need the Axiom of Choice to know that there's a choice function.

00:41:15 --> 00:41:19
When you can describe a specific way of choosing, then you don't need to

00:41:18 --> 00:41:22
appeal to the axiom to know that there's a choice

00:41:21 --> 00:41:25
But the problematic case occurs when you think about the infinite collection

00:41:25 --> 00:41:30
of socks that the

00:41:29 --> 00:41:34
person has in their closet. And if we assume that socks are

00:41:33 --> 00:41:38
sort of indistinguishable within each pair, you know, they match each other,

00:41:37 --> 00:41:44
indiscernible, then the- the butler wouldn't have any kind of rule for

00:41:43 --> 00:41:52
which sock in each pair to pick. And so it's not so clear that he has a way of-

00:41:52 --> 00:41:56
of producing one sock from each pair

00:41:55 --> 00:42:00
because... Right? So that's what's at stake, is the question of whether

00:41:59 --> 00:42:07
you can specify a rule by which the choice function, you know, a rule that it obeys

00:42:06 --> 00:42:11
that defines the choice function, or whether there's sort of this arbitrary

00:42:10 --> 00:42:15
choosing aspect to it. That's when you need the axiom of choice to

00:42:14 --> 00:42:19
know that there is such a function. But of course, as a matter of

00:42:18 --> 00:42:23
mathematical ontology, we might find attractive the idea that, well,

00:42:22 --> 00:42:26
look, I mean, I don't- not every way of

00:42:25 --> 00:42:30
choosing the socks has to be defined by rule. Why should everything that

00:42:29 --> 00:42:34
exists in mathematical reality follow a rule or a

00:42:33 --> 00:42:37
procedure of that sort? If I have the

00:42:36 --> 00:42:41
idea that my mathematical ontology is rich with objects,

00:42:40 --> 00:42:45
then I think that, that there are all kinds of functions and

00:42:44 --> 00:42:49
ways of choosing. Those are all part of the mathematical reality that I

00:42:48 --> 00:42:52
wanna be talking about.... and so I don't have any problem

00:42:52 --> 00:42:56
asserting the Axiom of Choice. Yes, there is a way of choosing,

00:42:55 --> 00:43:00
But I can't t- necessarily tell you what it is.

00:42:59 --> 00:43:04
But in a mathematical argument, I can assume that I fix the choice

00:43:03 --> 00:43:07
function because I know that there is one. So it's a... The

00:43:06 --> 00:43:11
philosophical difference between working when you have the Axiom of Choice

00:43:10 --> 00:43:15
and when you don't is the question of this constructive nature of the

00:43:14 --> 00:43:19
argument. So if you make an argument and you appeal to the Axiom of Choice,

00:43:18 --> 00:43:22
then maybe you're admitting that the objects that

00:43:21 --> 00:43:26
you're producing in the proof are not gonna be constructive.

00:43:25 --> 00:43:30
You're not gonna be able to necessarily say specific things about them.

00:43:30 --> 00:43:35
But if you're just claiming to make an existence claim, that's totally fine. Whereas if you have

00:43:34 --> 00:43:39
a constructive attitude about ma- the nature of mathematics, and you

00:43:38 --> 00:43:43
think that mathematical claims maybe are only warranted when you can

00:43:42 --> 00:43:46
provide an explicit procedure for producing the mathematical objects that you're dealing

00:43:45 --> 00:43:52
with then you're probably gonna wanna deny the axiom of choice and maybe much more.

00:43:51 --> 00:43:56
- Can we maybe speak to the axioms that

00:43:55 --> 00:44:00
underlie ZFC? So cone of perplexity, ZFC, or Zermelo-Fraenkel

00:43:59 --> 00:44:04
set theory with the Axiom of Choice, as we mentioned, is the standard foundation for most

00:44:03 --> 00:44:08
modern mathematics. It consists of the following main axioms: Axiom

00:44:07 --> 00:44:12
of Extensionality, Axiom of Empty Set, Axiom of Pairing, Axiom

00:44:11 --> 00:44:16
of Union, Axiom of Power Set, Axiom of Infinity, Axiom of

00:44:15 --> 00:44:20
Separation, Axiom of Replacement, Axiom of Regularity,

00:44:19 --> 00:44:25
and Axiom of Choice. Some of these are quite basic, but it would be nice

00:44:25 --> 00:44:29
to kinda give people a sense-

00:44:28 --> 00:44:33
...of what it means to be an axiom. Like, what kind of basic

00:44:33 --> 00:44:38
facts we can lay on the table on which we can build some beautiful mathematics.

00:44:37 --> 00:44:42
- Yeah, so the history of it is really quite fascinating. So, Zermelo introduced

00:44:41 --> 00:44:46
most of these axioms, I mean, as part of what's now called Zermelo set

00:44:45 --> 00:44:50
heory, to formalize his proof from the Axiom of

00:44:49 --> 00:44:54
Choice to the Well-Order Principle, which was an extremely controversial result.

00:44:53 --> 00:44:58
So in 1904, he gave the proof without the theory, and then he was challenged to

00:44:57 --> 00:45:01
provide the theory. And so in 1908, he produced

00:45:01 --> 00:45:06
the Zermelo set theory and gave the proof that

00:45:05 --> 00:45:10
in that theory, you can prove that every set admits a well ordering.

00:45:09 --> 00:45:14
And so the axioms on the list, these things like extensionality, express

00:45:13 --> 00:45:17
the most fundamental principles of the

00:45:16 --> 00:45:21
understanding of sets that he wanted to be talking about. So for example,

00:45:20 --> 00:45:27
extensionality says if two sets have the same members, then they're equal. So

00:45:26 --> 00:45:31
it's this idea that the sets consist of the

00:45:30 --> 00:45:35
collection of their members, and that's it. There's nothing else that's going on in

00:45:34 --> 00:45:38
the set. So it's just if two sets have the same members, then they are the same

00:45:37 --> 00:45:45
set. So it's maybe the most primitive axiom in some respect.

00:45:44 --> 00:45:49
- Well, there's also, just to give a flavor, there exists a set

00:45:48 --> 00:45:53
with no elements, called the empty set. For any two sets, there's a

00:45:52 --> 00:45:56
set that contains exactly those two sets as

00:45:55 --> 00:46:00
elements. For any set, there's a set that contains exactly the elements of the

00:45:59 --> 00:46:04
lements of that set, so the union set. And then there's the power

00:46:03 --> 00:46:08
set. For any set, there's a set whose elements are exactly the

00:46:07 --> 00:46:11
subsets of the original set, the power set. In the axiom of

00:46:10 --> 00:46:15
infinity, there exists an infinite set, typically a set

00:46:14 --> 00:46:19
hat contains the empty set and is closed under the operation of adding one more

00:46:18 --> 00:46:23
lement. Back to our hotel example.

00:46:22 --> 00:46:24
- That's right.

00:46:23 --> 00:46:27
- And there's more, but this is kind of fascinating.

00:46:26 --> 00:46:31
Just put yourself in the mindset of people at

00:46:30 --> 00:46:35
he beginning of this, of trying to formalize set theory.

00:46:34 --> 00:46:37
It's fascinating that humans can do that.

00:46:37 --> 00:46:41
- I read some historical accounts by historians about

00:46:40 --> 00:46:45
hat time period, specifically about Zermelo's axioms and his

00:46:44 --> 00:46:52
proof of the well-order theorem. And the historians were saying never before in the

00:46:52 --> 00:46:56
history of mathematics has a mathematical theorem been argued

00:46:56 --> 00:47:02
about so publicly and so vociferously as that theorem of

00:47:01 --> 00:47:09
Zermelo's. And it's fascinating also because the axiom of choice was

00:47:08 --> 00:47:13
widely regarded as a kind of, you know, basic principle at first,

00:47:12 --> 00:47:16
but then when, but people were very suspicious of the well-order theorem because no one could

00:47:15 --> 00:47:20
imagine a well ordering, say, of the real numbers. And so

00:47:19 --> 00:47:24
this was a case when Zermelo seemed to be, from principles

00:47:23 --> 00:47:28
that seemed quite reasonable, proving this obvious untruth.

00:47:28 --> 00:47:33
And so people were, mathematicians were objecting. But then

00:47:32 --> 00:47:36
Zermelo and others actually looked into the

00:47:35 --> 00:47:40
mathematical papers and so on of some of the people who had been

00:47:39 --> 00:47:44
objecting so vociferously, and found, in many

00:47:43 --> 00:47:47
cases, that they were implicitly using the axiom of choice in their

00:47:46 --> 00:47:51
own arguments, even though they would argue publicly against

00:47:50 --> 00:47:55
it. Because it's so natural to use it because

00:47:54 --> 00:47:58
it's such an obvious principle in a way. I mean, it's

00:47:57 --> 00:48:02
easy to just use it by accident if you're not critical enough

00:48:01 --> 00:48:06
and you don't even realize that you're using the axiom of choice. That's true now, even.

00:48:05 --> 00:48:10
People like to pay attention to when the axiom of choice is used or not used in

00:48:09 --> 00:48:13
mathematical arguments, up until this day. It used to be more

00:48:12 --> 00:48:17
important. In the early 20th century it was very important because people didn't know if it

00:48:16 --> 00:48:21
was a consistent theory or not, and there were these antinomies arising.

00:48:20 --> 00:48:25
and so there was a worry about consistency of the axioms.

00:48:24 --> 00:48:29
but then, of course, eventually, with the result of, of

00:48:28 --> 00:48:33
Godel and Cohen and so on, they... this consistency question specifically about the

00:48:32 --> 00:48:37
axiom of choice sort of falls away. We know that the axiom of choice itself

00:48:36 --> 00:48:41
will never be the source of inconsistency in set

00:48:40 --> 00:48:45
heory. If there's inconsistency with the axiom of choice, then it's, it's already

00:48:44 --> 00:48:48
inconsistent without the axiom of choice. So it's not the cause of

00:48:47 --> 00:48:52
inconsistency. And so in that... from that point of view, the need to pay

00:48:51 --> 00:48:56
attention to whether you're using it or not from a consistency point of view is somehow less

00:48:55 --> 00:48:59
important. But still there's this

00:48:59 --> 00:49:03
reason to pay attention to it on the grounds of these constructivist

00:49:02 --> 00:49:06
ideas that I had mentioned earlier.

00:49:05 --> 00:49:10
- And we should say, in set theory, consistency means that it is impossible to

00:49:09 --> 00:49:13
derive a contradiction from the axioms of the theory.

00:49:12 --> 00:49:16
It means that there are no contradictions. That's a...

00:49:15 --> 00:49:16
- That's right

00:49:15 --> 00:49:20
- A consistent axiomatic system is that there are no contradictions.

00:49:19 --> 00:49:23
- A consistent theory is one for which you cannot prove a contradiction from that

00:49:22 --> 00:49:24
heory.

00:49:23 --> 00:49:27
- Maybe a quick pause, a quick break, a quick bathroom break.

00:49:28 --> 00:49:33
You mentioned to me offline we were talking about Russell's paradox and that

00:49:32 --> 00:49:38
here's a- a nice, another kind of anthropomorphizable proof of

00:49:37 --> 00:49:41
uncountability. I was wondering if you can lay that out.

00:49:41 --> 00:49:43
- Oh yeah, sure. Absolutely.

00:49:42 --> 00:49:45
- Both Russell's paradox and the proof.

00:49:44 --> 00:49:50
- Right. So we talked about Cantor's proof

00:49:49 --> 00:49:54
that the real numbers, the set of real numbers is an uncountable infinity,

00:49:53 --> 00:49:58
it's a strictly larger infinity than the natural numbers. But Cantor

00:49:57 --> 00:50:01
actually proved a- a much more general fact,

00:50:00 --> 00:50:05
namely that for any set whatsoever, the power

00:50:05 --> 00:50:10
set of that set is a strictly larger set. So the power set

00:50:09 --> 00:50:14
is the set containing all the subsets of the original set. So if you have a set

00:50:14 --> 00:50:19
and you look at the collection of all of its subsets, then Cantor proved

00:50:18 --> 00:50:23
that this is- this is a bigger set. They're not

00:50:22 --> 00:50:26
equinumerous. Of course, there's always at least as many subsets as elements

00:50:25 --> 00:50:30
because for any element you can make, the- the singleton subset

00:50:29 --> 00:50:34
hat has only that guy as a member, right? So

00:50:33 --> 00:50:38
there's always at least as many subsets as elements. But the question is

00:50:37 --> 00:50:42
whether they, whether it's strictly more or not. And so

00:50:41 --> 00:50:46
Cantor reasoned like this. It's very simple. It's a kind of

00:50:45 --> 00:50:49
distilling the abstract diagonalization idea

00:50:48 --> 00:50:56
without being encumbered by the complexity of the real numbers. So, we have a set X,

00:50:56 --> 00:51:01
and we're looking at all of its subsets. That's the power set of X.

00:51:00 --> 00:51:04
Suppose that X and the power set of X have the same

00:51:03 --> 00:51:08
size. Suppose, towards contradiction, they have the same size.

00:51:07 --> 00:51:14
So that means we can associate to every individual of X a subset.

00:51:14 --> 00:51:19
And so now let me define a new set. Another set, I'm going to

00:51:18 --> 00:51:23
define it. Let's call it D. And D is the subset

00:51:22 --> 00:51:30
of X that contains all the individuals that are not in their set. Every individual

00:51:30 --> 00:51:35
was associated with a subset of X, and I'm looking at the individuals that

00:51:34 --> 00:51:37
are not in their set.

00:51:37 --> 00:51:42
Maybe nobody's like that. Maybe there's no element of X that's like that,

00:51:41 --> 00:51:44
or maybe they're all like that, or maybe some of them are and some of them aren't.

00:51:43 --> 00:51:48
It doesn't really matter for the argument. I defined a subset

00:51:47 --> 00:51:52
D consisting of the individuals that are not in the set that's

00:51:51 --> 00:51:56
attached to them, but that's a perfectly good subset. And so

00:51:55 --> 00:51:59
because of the equinumerosity, it would have to be attached to a particular individual,

00:51:58 --> 00:52:07
you know? And- ...but that... let's call that person, it should

00:52:06 --> 00:52:11
be a name starting with D, so Diana.

00:52:10 --> 00:52:15
And now we ask, is Diana an element of D or not?

00:52:15 --> 00:52:19
But if Diana is an element of D, then she is in her

00:52:18 --> 00:52:23
set. So she shouldn't be because the

00:52:23 --> 00:52:28
set D was the set of individuals that are not in their set.

00:52:28 --> 00:52:32
So if Diana is in D, then she shouldn't be. But if she isn't in

00:52:31 --> 00:52:36
D, then she wouldn't be in her set. And so she should be in D.

00:52:35 --> 00:52:40
That's a contradiction. So therefore, the number

00:52:39 --> 00:52:45
of subsets is always greater than the number of elements for any set.

00:52:45 --> 00:52:49
And the anthropomorphizing idea is the following.

00:52:49 --> 00:52:54
I'd like to talk about it this way. For any collection

00:52:53 --> 00:52:58
of people, you can form more committees from them

00:52:57 --> 00:53:04
than there are people, even if you have infinetely many people.

00:53:03 --> 00:53:07
Suppose you have an infinite set of people. And what's a

00:53:06 --> 00:53:11
committee? Well, a committee is just a list of who's on the committee, basically.

00:53:10 --> 00:53:15
The members of the committee. So there's all the two-person committees

00:53:14 --> 00:53:18
and there's all the one-person committees, and there's the universal,

00:53:17 --> 00:53:22
the worst committee, the one that everyone is on. Okay. The best committee

00:53:22 --> 00:53:28
is the empty committee. With no members and never meets and so on. Or is the empty

00:53:27 --> 00:53:30
committee meeting all the time? I'm not sure.

00:53:29 --> 00:53:32
- Yeah. That's... wow, that's a profound question.

00:53:32 --> 00:53:36
And does a committee with just one member meet also as a-

00:53:35 --> 00:53:39
Yeah, maybe it's always in session. I don't know.

00:53:38 --> 00:53:45
So the claim is that there are more committees than people.

00:53:44 --> 00:53:49
Okay. Suppose not. Well, then we could make an association between the

00:53:48 --> 00:53:53
people and the committees. So we would have every committee

00:53:52 --> 00:53:57
could be named after a person in a one-to-one way.

00:53:56 --> 00:54:01
And I'm not saying that the person is on the committee that's named after them,

00:54:00 --> 00:54:05
or not on it, whatever. Maybe sometimes that happens, sometimes it doesn't. I don't know.

00:54:04 --> 00:54:11
It doesn't matter. But let's form what I call committee D, which consists of all the

00:54:10 --> 00:54:17
people that are not on the committee that's named after them.

00:54:16 --> 00:54:21
Okay. Maybe that's everyone, maybe it's no one, maybe it's half the people. It doesn't

00:54:20 --> 00:54:27
matter. That's a committee. It's a set of people. And so it has to be named after

00:54:26 --> 00:54:34
someone. Let's call that person Daniella. So now we ask, is

00:54:33 --> 00:54:38
Daniella on the committee that's named after her?

00:54:37 --> 00:54:42
Well, if she is, then she shouldn't be because it was the

00:54:41 --> 00:54:46
committee of people who aren't on their own committee.

00:54:45 --> 00:54:49
And if she isn't, then she should be. So again, it's a

00:54:48 --> 00:54:56
contradiction. So when I was teaching at Oxford, one of my students came up with the

00:54:55 --> 00:55:00
following different anthropomorphization of Cantor's

00:54:59 --> 00:55:04
argument. Let's consider all possible fruit

00:55:03 --> 00:55:08
salads. We have a given collection of fruits.

00:55:07 --> 00:55:12
You know, apples and oranges and grapes, whatever. And a fruit salad consists of

00:55:11 --> 00:55:16
some collection of those fruits. So there's the banana, pear, grape salad and

00:55:15 --> 00:55:20
so on. There are a lot of different kinds of salad. Every set of fruits

00:55:19 --> 00:55:24
makes a salad, a fruit salad. Okay. And we want to

00:55:23 --> 00:55:28
prove that for any collection of fruits, even if there are infinitely

00:55:27 --> 00:55:32
many different kinds of fruit, for any collection

00:55:31 --> 00:55:37
of fruits, there are more possible fruit salads than there are fruits.

00:55:38 --> 00:55:42
So if not, then you can put a one-to-one correspondence between the

00:55:41 --> 00:55:46
fruits and the fruit salads, so you could name every fruit

00:55:45 --> 00:55:50
salad after a fruit. That fruit might not be in

00:55:49 --> 00:55:53
that salad, it doesn't matter. We're just... it's a naming, a one-to-one correspondence.

00:55:53 --> 00:55:59
And then, of course, we form the diagonal salad, which consists

00:55:58 --> 00:56:03
of all the fruits that are not in the salad that's named after them.

00:56:04 --> 00:56:09
And that's a perfectly good salad. It might be

00:56:08 --> 00:56:12
the kind of diet salad, if it was the empty salad, or it might be the universal

00:56:11 --> 00:56:13
ad

00:56:12 --> 00:56:17
which had all fruits in it, if all the fruits were in it. Or it might have just some and

00:56:16 --> 00:56:21
not all. So that diagonal salad would have to be named after

00:56:20 --> 00:56:24
some fruit. So let's suppose it's named after durian,

00:56:23 --> 00:56:28
meaning that it was associated with durian in the one-to-one correspondence. And then

00:56:27 --> 00:56:36
we ask, well, is durian in the salad that it's named after? And if it is, then it

00:56:35 --> 00:56:40
shouldn't be. And if it isn't, then it should be. And so it's again the same

00:56:39 --> 00:56:43
contradiction. So all of those arguments are just the same as

00:56:42 --> 00:56:46
Cantor's proof that the power set of any set is

00:56:45 --> 00:56:50
bigger than the set. And this is exactly the same logic

00:56:49 --> 00:56:54
that comes up in Russell's paradox, because Russell is arguing

00:56:53 --> 00:56:59
that the class of all sets can't be a set,

00:56:59 --> 00:57:04
because if it were, then we could form the

00:57:03 --> 00:57:09
set of all sets that are not elements of themselves.

00:57:09 --> 00:57:13
So basically, what Russell is proving is that there are more

00:57:12 --> 00:57:19
collections of sets than elements. Because we can form the

00:57:18 --> 00:57:23
diagonal class, you know, the class of all sets that are not

00:57:22 --> 00:57:27
elements of themselves. If that were a set, then it would be an

00:57:26 --> 00:57:31
element of itself if and only if it was not an element of itself. It's

00:57:30 --> 00:57:35
exactly the same logic in all four of those arguments. Yeah.

00:57:34 --> 00:57:39
So there can't be a class of all sets, because if there were, then there would have to be a class of all

00:57:38 --> 00:57:43
sets that aren't elements of themselves. But that set

00:57:42 --> 00:57:46
would be an element of itself if and only if it's not an element of itself, which is a

00:57:45 --> 00:57:52
contradiction. So this is the essence of the Russell paradox. I don't call it the

00:57:51 --> 00:57:56
Russell paradox. Actually, when I teach it, I call it Russell's theorem. There's no

00:57:55 --> 00:57:59
universal set. And it's not really

00:57:58 --> 00:58:06
confusing anymore. At the time, it was very confusing, but now we've absorbed this

00:58:05 --> 00:58:10
nature of set theory into our fundamental understanding of how sets

00:58:09 --> 00:58:14
are, and it's not confusing anymore. I mean, the history is fascinating though about

00:58:13 --> 00:58:18
he Russell paradox, because before that

00:58:17 --> 00:58:21
ime, Frege was working on his monumental work

00:58:21 --> 00:58:26
undertaking, implementing the philosophy of logicism, which is

00:58:25 --> 00:58:30
the attempt to reduce all of mathematics to logic.

00:58:30 --> 00:58:34
So Frege wanted to give an account of all of mathematics in

00:58:33 --> 00:58:38
terms of logical notions, and he

00:58:37 --> 00:58:42
was writing this monumental work and had formulated his basic

00:58:41 --> 00:58:46
principles. And those principles happened to

00:58:45 --> 00:58:52
imply that for any property whatsoever, you could form the set of objects with that

00:58:51 --> 00:59:00
property. This is known as the general comprehension principle. And

00:58:59 --> 00:59:04
he was appealing to the principles that support that axiom,

00:59:03 --> 00:59:08
throughout his work. I mean, it wasn't just an

00:59:07 --> 00:59:13
incidental thing. He was really using this principle. And Russell wrote him a letter

00:59:13 --> 00:59:18
when he observed the work in progress, that there was

00:59:17 --> 00:59:22
this problem, because if you accept the principle that for any property whatsoever, you can make

00:59:21 --> 00:59:26
a set of objects with that property, then you could form the set of

00:59:25 --> 00:59:30
all sets that are not members of themselves. That's just an

00:59:29 --> 00:59:35
instance of the general comprehension principle. And

00:59:36 --> 00:59:41
the set of all sets that aren't elements of themselves can't be a set, because if it

00:59:40 --> 00:59:44
were, then it would be an element of itself if and only if it's not a member of

00:59:43 --> 00:59:46
itself, and that's a contradiction.

00:59:46 --> 00:59:51
And so Russell wrote this letter to Frege, and it was just at the moment

00:59:50 --> 00:59:54
when Frege was finishing his work. It was already at the publishers

00:59:54 --> 00:59:58
and, you know, in press basically. But it's

00:59:57 --> 01:00:02
completely devastating. I mean, it must have been such a

01:00:01 --> 01:00:05
horrible situation for Frege to be placed in

01:00:04 --> 01:00:10
because he's finished this monumental work, you know,

01:00:10 --> 01:00:15
years of his life dedicated to this, and Russell finds this

01:00:15 --> 01:00:21
basically one-line proof of a contradiction in the fundamental principles of

01:00:20 --> 01:00:27
the thesis that completely destroys the whole system. And

01:00:27 --> 01:00:32
Frege had put in the appendix of his work a

01:00:31 --> 01:00:36
response to Russell's letter in which he explained what happened, and he wrote

01:00:35 --> 01:00:39
very gracefully, "Hardly anything more unwelcome can befall a

01:00:38 --> 01:00:43
scientific writer than to have one of the foundations of his edifice shaken after the

01:00:42 --> 01:00:47
work is finished. This is the position into which I was put by a letter from

01:00:46 --> 01:00:50
Mr. Bertrand Russell as the printing of this volume was nearing

01:00:49 --> 01:00:54
completion." And then he goes on to explain the matter, it concerns his basic law

01:00:53 --> 01:00:55
five, and so on, and...

01:00:54 --> 01:00:58
- It's heartbreaking. I mean, there's nothing more traumatic to

01:00:58 --> 01:01:02
a person who dreams of constructing mathematics all from logic.

01:01:02 --> 01:01:09
to get a very clean, simple contradiction. I mean, that's just...

01:01:08 --> 01:01:14
- You devote your life to this work, and then it's shown to be contradictory, and

01:01:13 --> 01:01:16
that must have been heartbreaking.

01:01:16 --> 01:01:21
- What do you think about the Frege project, the philosophy of logic,

01:01:20 --> 01:01:23
the dream of the power of logic-

01:01:22 --> 01:01:23
Right

01:01:22 --> 01:01:25
- ... to construct a mathematical universe?

01:01:24 --> 01:01:28
- So, of course, the project of logicism did not die with

01:01:28 --> 01:01:32
Frege, and it was continued, and, you know, there's a whole

01:01:31 --> 01:01:36
movement, the neologicists and so on, in contemporary times

01:01:35 --> 01:01:40
even. But my view of the matter is that, really, we

01:01:39 --> 01:01:45
should view the main goals of logicism are basically completely fulfilled

01:01:45 --> 01:01:50
in the rise of set-theoretic foundationalism. I mean,

01:01:49 --> 01:01:57
When you view ZFC as the foundation of mathematics, and in my view, the principles of

01:01:56 --> 01:02:01
ZFC are fundamentally illogical in character including the axiom of

01:02:00 --> 01:02:05
choice, as I mentioned, as a principle of logic. This is a

01:02:04 --> 01:02:09
highly disputed point of view, though, cause a lot of people take even the axiom of

01:02:08 --> 01:02:13
infinity as inherently mathematical and

01:02:12 --> 01:02:17
not logical and so on. But I think if you adopt the view

01:02:17 --> 01:02:21
that the principles of ZFC have to do with the principles of

01:02:20 --> 01:02:25
abstract, you know, set formation, which is fundamentally

01:02:24 --> 01:02:29
logical in character, then it's complete success for

01:02:28 --> 01:02:33
logicism. So the fact that set theory is able to serve as a foundation

01:02:32 --> 01:02:36
means that mathematics can be founded on logic.

01:02:35 --> 01:02:40
- I think this is a good moment to talk about Gödel's incompleteness

01:02:39 --> 01:02:44
theorems. So, can you explain them and what do they teach

01:02:43 --> 01:02:47
us about the nature of mathematical truth?

01:02:47 --> 01:02:52
- Absolutely. It's one of the most profound developments in mathematical

01:02:51 --> 01:02:54
ogic. I mean, the incompleteness theorems is when

01:02:54 --> 01:02:59
mathematical logic, in my view, first became sophisticated.

01:02:59 --> 01:03:04
