It's a kind of birth of the subject of mathematical

01:03:03 --> 01:03:08
ogic. But to understand the theorems, you really have to start a little

01:03:07 --> 01:03:10
bit earlier with Hilbert's program.

01:03:10 --> 01:03:15
because at that time, you know, with the Russell Paradox and so on, there were these various contradictions

01:03:14 --> 01:03:19
popping up in various parts of set theory and the Burali-Forti

01:03:18 --> 01:03:26
paradox and so on. And, and Hilbert was famously supportive of set theory. I mean,

01:03:25 --> 01:03:30
there's this quote of him saying, "No one

01:03:29 --> 01:03:34
shall cast us from the paradise that Cantor has created for

01:03:33 --> 01:03:37
us." And what I take him to mean by that is he was

01:03:37 --> 01:03:41
o captured by the idea of using set theory as a foundation

01:03:40 --> 01:03:45
of mathematics and it was so powerful and convenient and unifying in a

01:03:44 --> 01:03:49
way that was extremely important. And he wasn't, he didn't wanna give that

01:03:48 --> 01:03:53
up, despite the danger of these

01:03:53 --> 01:03:59
paradoxes, these contradictions, basically, is how some people viewed them. And so,

01:03:58 --> 01:04:01
- this minefield of paradoxes. Yeah.

01:04:00 --> 01:04:04
- Right. A minefield. That's a really good way of describing the situation. And

01:04:03 --> 01:04:08
so Hilbert said, "Well, look, we have to fix this

01:04:07 --> 01:04:12
problem, you know. We wanna use the set theory foundations, but we want to

01:04:11 --> 01:04:16
do it in a way that is trustworthy and reliable. We can't

01:04:15 --> 01:04:19
allow that the foundations of mathematics are in

01:04:18 --> 01:04:24
question. You know, this is a kind of attitude, I think, that underlies Hilbert

01:04:24 --> 01:04:29
and the Hilbert program. And so he proposed, "Look,

01:04:28 --> 01:04:33
we're going to have this strong theory, this set theory that we want to be proving

01:04:32 --> 01:04:37
our theorems in but I

01:04:36 --> 01:04:41
mean, on the one hand, we want it to be as strong as possible. We would

01:04:40 --> 01:04:45
like it to answer all the questions." There's another

01:04:44 --> 01:04:49
famous quote of Hilbert in his retirement address where,

01:04:48 --> 01:04:52
he proclaims, "Wir müssen wissen, wir

01:04:51 --> 01:04:56
werden wissen." So, "We must know, we will know," in

01:04:55 --> 01:05:00
which he's very optimistic about the ability of

01:04:59 --> 01:05:05
mathematics to answer all of the questions of mathematics that

01:05:05 --> 01:05:10
we have posed. We have all these problems we want to solve and he is saying,

01:05:09 --> 01:05:14
"We're going to do it. We're going to solve all these problems." So we want to propose this strong theory

01:05:13 --> 01:05:17
and one has the sense that he had in mind set

01:05:16 --> 01:05:20
heory in which all the questions are going to be

01:05:19 --> 01:05:28
answered. Okay? But secondly, we want to combine that with, in a very

01:05:27 --> 01:05:32
weak arithmetic, purely finitistic theory, we want to prove

01:05:32 --> 01:05:37
that the reasoning process of the strong theory is

01:05:36 --> 01:05:40
afe. Okay? So in order to make sense of that point of view, you

01:05:39 --> 01:05:47
basically have to invent the philosophy of formalism, where we can look at what is a

01:05:46 --> 01:05:50
proof, what is the nature of mathematical

01:05:49 --> 01:05:54
reasoning. And on Hilbert's way of thinking about

01:05:53 --> 01:06:01
his, a proof is basically itself a finitistic kind of object. It's a sequence

01:06:00 --> 01:06:05
of... If you think about the nature of what a proof is, it's a sequence of

01:06:04 --> 01:06:09
assertions which can be viewed as sort of sequences of symbols

01:06:08 --> 01:06:13
that conform with certain rules of logical reasoning.

01:06:12 --> 01:06:17
And this is a formalist way of understanding the nature of

01:06:16 --> 01:06:20
proof. So we think about a proof in a kind of syntactic, formal

01:06:19 --> 01:06:24
way. Even though the contents of those statements might be

01:06:23 --> 01:06:27
referring to infinite uncountable objects,

01:06:27 --> 01:06:32
the statements themselves are not infinite uncountable objects. The statements themselves are

01:06:31 --> 01:06:34
just finite sequences of symbols.

01:06:33 --> 01:06:37
- So when you think of proof as, maybe it's fair to say, almost like a

01:06:36 --> 01:06:41
outside of math. It's like, tools operating on math. And then for

01:06:40 --> 01:06:46
Hilbert, he thought proof is inside the axiomatic system. Something like this.

01:06:45 --> 01:06:47
- Yeah, that's helpful.

01:06:46 --> 01:06:48
- That's wild.

01:06:48 --> 01:06:53
- The main thing about formalism is that you

01:06:52 --> 01:06:56
think of the process of doing mathematics. You divorce it

01:06:56 --> 01:07:01
from the meaning of the mathematical assertions, right? So the

01:07:00 --> 01:07:05
meaning of the mathematical assertions that you make in this infinitary theory

01:07:04 --> 01:07:09
has to do with these huge uncountable infinities and so on possibly.

01:07:08 --> 01:07:16
And that's a very sort of uncertain realm maybe and the source of the paradoxes

01:07:15 --> 01:07:20
and so on in some people's minds. But the reasoning

01:07:19 --> 01:07:24
process itself consists of writing down sequences of symbols

01:07:23 --> 01:07:28
on your page and, you know, undertaking or,

01:07:27 --> 01:07:34
an argument with them which is following these finitary rules. And so,

01:07:33 --> 01:07:38
if we divorce the meaning of the symbols from just the process of

01:07:37 --> 01:07:41
manipulating the symbols, it's a way of looking at the nature of mathematics

01:07:40 --> 01:07:44
as a kind of formal game in which-

01:07:43 --> 01:07:48
the meaning may be totally absent. I don't think it's necessarily

01:07:47 --> 01:07:52
part of the formalist view that there is no meaning behind, but rather it's

01:07:51 --> 01:07:56
emphasizing that we can divorce the meaning of

01:07:55 --> 01:07:59
the sentences from the process of manipulating those sentences.

01:07:59 --> 01:08:03
And then Hilbert wanted to prove in this purely finitary

01:08:02 --> 01:08:07
theory that if we follow the rules of that game, we're

01:08:06 --> 01:08:10
never going to get a contradiction.

01:08:09 --> 01:08:14
So those were the two aims of the Hilbert program, is to

01:08:13 --> 01:08:17
found the strong infinitary theory, probably set

01:08:16 --> 01:08:21
heory, which is going to answer all the questions. And then

01:08:20 --> 01:08:29
secondly, prove in the finitary theory that the strong theory is

01:08:28 --> 01:08:32
afe. In other words, consistent, yeah?

01:08:31 --> 01:08:35
- What does the word "finitary" in finitary theory mean?

01:08:34 --> 01:08:38
- Yeah. Well, this is, of course, philosophically contentious, and people

01:08:38 --> 01:08:43
have different ideas about what exactly it should mean. So there's hundreds

01:08:42 --> 01:08:46
of papers on exactly that question. But I like to take it just kind of

01:08:45 --> 01:08:50
informally. I mean, it means that we're talking about finite sequences of

01:08:49 --> 01:08:53
symbols, and we're going to have a theory, you know, finite

01:08:52 --> 01:08:57
strings of symbols. A finitary theory would be

01:08:56 --> 01:09:01
one whose subject matter is about those kinds of things so that we

01:09:00 --> 01:09:05
can conceivably argue about the nature

01:09:04 --> 01:09:09
of these finite strings. A proof is just a finite sequence of statements,

01:09:08 --> 01:09:13
so that every statement is either one of the axioms or follows

01:09:12 --> 01:09:16
by the laws of logic from the earlier statements in some specified

01:09:15 --> 01:09:21
manner, like using modus ponens or some other law of logic like that.

01:09:21 --> 01:09:26
And such that the last line on the list is, you know, the theorem

01:09:25 --> 01:09:30
that you're proving. So that's what a proof is in this kind of way of thinking.

01:09:29 --> 01:09:34
To take a specific example, I mean, I always conceive of the, perhaps the

01:09:33 --> 01:09:38
most natural finitary theory that one would be called upon to

01:09:37 --> 01:09:42
exhibit would be Peano arithmetic, the theory of Peano arithmetic, which-

01:09:41 --> 01:09:45
which is a first order theory of the nature of

01:09:44 --> 01:09:49
arithmetic. But okay, so some people say, "Well, Peano

01:09:48 --> 01:09:53
arithmetic has these strong first order induction axioms, and there's much,

01:09:52 --> 01:09:57
much weaker versions of arithmetic, like I-sigma-naught or I-sigma-1 and

01:09:56 --> 01:10:00
so on, which are even more finitary than Peano

01:09:59 --> 01:10:04
arithmetic." So different philosophical positions take different

01:10:03 --> 01:10:08
attitudes about what is, what does it take to be finitary? How finitary do you

01:10:07 --> 01:10:11
have to be to be truly finitary?

01:10:10 --> 01:10:14
- So according to Perplexity, Peano arithmetic is a foundational system for

01:10:13 --> 01:10:18
malizing the properties and operations of natural numbers using a set of

01:10:17 --> 01:10:22
axioms called the Peano axioms. Peano arithmetic provides a

01:10:21 --> 01:10:26
formal language and axioms for arithmetic operations, such as

01:10:25 --> 01:10:30
addition and multiplication over the natural numbers. The axioms define the

01:10:29 --> 01:10:33
xistence of a first natural number, usually zero or

01:10:32 --> 01:10:37
one, the concept of a successor function, which generates

01:10:36 --> 01:10:41
the next natural number, rules for addition and multiplication built from these

01:10:40 --> 01:10:45
concepts, the principle of induction allowing proofs around all

01:10:44 --> 01:10:49
natural numbers, and it goes on. So it's a very particular kind of arithmetic

01:10:48 --> 01:10:52
that is finitary.

01:10:51 --> 01:10:56
- You know, in my... I view it as finitary, but this is a contentious view. Not everyone agrees with

01:10:55 --> 01:10:59
at. That's what I was trying to hint at.

01:10:58 --> 01:10:59
- Okay. I got it. All right.

01:10:58 --> 01:11:05
- Peano arithmetic is one of the hugely successful theories

01:11:04 --> 01:11:08
of the natural numbers and elementary number theory.

01:11:07 --> 01:11:12
Essentially, all of classical number theory, so

01:11:11 --> 01:11:16
whatever kind of theorems you want to be proving about the prime numbers or

01:11:15 --> 01:11:19
factorization or any kind of finitary reasoning about

01:11:19 --> 01:11:23
finite combinatorial objects, all of it can be formalized in

01:11:22 --> 01:11:27
Peano arithmetic. I mean, that's the basic situation. Of

01:11:26 --> 01:11:31
course, one has to qualify those statements in light of the Gödel incompleteness

01:11:30 --> 01:11:38
theorem, but for the most part, the classical number theoretic analysis of

01:11:37 --> 01:11:45
the finite numbers is almost entirely developable inside Peano arithmetic.

01:11:45 --> 01:11:50
So if we go back to the Hilbert program, so Hilbert has these two goals:

01:11:50 --> 01:11:55
produce the strong theory which is going to answer all the questions, and then

01:11:54 --> 01:11:57
prove by purely finitary means

01:11:57 --> 01:12:02
that that theory will never lead into contradiction. And one

01:12:01 --> 01:12:06
can think about, well, the incompleteness theorem should be viewed as a

01:12:05 --> 01:12:10
decisive refutation of the Hilbert program. It defeats

01:12:09 --> 01:12:16
both of those goals decisively, completely. But before explaining

01:12:15 --> 01:12:20
that, maybe one should think about, you know, what if Hilbert had been right? What would

01:12:19 --> 01:12:25
be the nature of mathematics in the world that Hilbert is telling us to search for?

01:12:24 --> 01:12:29
- And if I may, going to Perplexity's definition of Hilbert's

01:12:28 --> 01:12:33
program, it was David Hilbert's early 20th century project to give all

01:12:32 --> 01:12:37
of classical mathematics a completely secure finitary

01:12:36 --> 01:12:41
foundation. In essence, the goal was to formalize all of

01:12:40 --> 01:12:44
mathematics in precise axiomatic systems and then

01:12:44 --> 01:12:48
prove using only very elementary finitary reasoning

01:12:48 --> 01:12:52
about symbols that these systems are free of contradiction.

01:12:51 --> 01:12:56
- Right. Exactly right. Let's imagine what it would be like if he had been

01:12:55 --> 01:12:59
right. So we would have this finitary theory,

01:12:59 --> 01:13:04
and it would prove that the strong theory was free of contradiction.

01:13:04 --> 01:13:11
So we could start enumerating proofs from the strong theory. Right now, we can

01:13:11 --> 01:13:17
write a computer program that would systematically generate all possible proofs

01:13:17 --> 01:13:27
from a given theory. And so we could have, like, this theorem

01:13:27 --> 01:13:32
enumeration machine that would just spit out theorems all day long in such

01:13:31 --> 01:13:36
a manner that every single theorem would eventually be produced by this device.

01:13:37 --> 01:13:45
And so if you had a mathematical question of any kind, you could answer it by just

01:13:45 --> 01:13:49
waiting for either the answer to come out yes

01:13:48 --> 01:13:53
or from the machine or the answer to come out no. So

01:13:52 --> 01:13:56
the nature of mathematical investigation in Hilbert's world

01:13:55 --> 01:14:00
is one of just turning the crank of the theorem enumeration

01:13:59 --> 01:14:04
machine, devoid of creative thinking or

01:14:03 --> 01:14:08
imagination, it's just getting the answer from this by rote.

01:14:07 --> 01:14:16
procedure. So Hilbert, in effect, is telling us, I mean, with his program, that the

01:14:15 --> 01:14:20
fundamental nature of mathematics is rote computation. I mean, the way I

01:14:19 --> 01:14:23
think about the Hilbert program seems extremely attractive in the

01:14:23 --> 01:14:27
historical context of being worried about the antinomies,

01:14:26 --> 01:14:32
the inconsistencies, and so how can we kind of block them?

01:14:31 --> 01:14:36
It seems natural, first of all, to have a strong theory that's going to answer

01:14:35 --> 01:14:40
all the questions, because the idea of logical independence and

01:14:39 --> 01:14:47
pervasiveness that we now know exists just wasn't, you know, there was

01:14:46 --> 01:14:51
no known. They didn't know anything like that happening ever.

01:14:50 --> 01:14:55
And so it's natural to think that it wouldn't happen, and also

01:14:54 --> 01:14:59
that they would be able to guard against this inconsistency. So it seems like the

01:14:58 --> 01:15:04
goals of the Hilbert program are quite natural in that historical context.

01:15:03 --> 01:15:08
But, you know, when you think a little more about what the nature of it would be like,

01:15:07 --> 01:15:12
it shows you this kind of rote procedure. And now you're saying, well, that

01:15:11 --> 01:15:16
doesn't seem so unlikely maybe, I mean, in the light of the

01:15:15 --> 01:15:20
increasing computer power and so on, it's actually maybe turning into

01:15:19 --> 01:15:24
ur everyday experience, where the machines are calculating more and more for

01:15:23 --> 01:15:31
us in a way that could be alarming. Okay. But, okay, so to talk about the

01:15:30 --> 01:15:34
alternative to the Hilbert point of view, I mean, if he's

01:15:33 --> 01:15:38
wrong, then what is the nature of mathematical reality? Well, it would mean

01:15:37 --> 01:15:42
that we couldn't ever maybe, for the first goal, we

01:15:41 --> 01:15:45
couldn't ever write down a theory that answered all the

01:15:44 --> 01:15:49
questions. So we would always be in

01:15:48 --> 01:15:54
a situation where our best theory, even the infinitary theories,

01:15:55 --> 01:16:00
would have questions that they stumble with and are unable to answer.

01:15:59 --> 01:16:04
Independence would occur. But then also, because of

01:16:03 --> 01:16:08
the failure of the second goal, we would also have to be

01:16:07 --> 01:16:12
constantly worrying about whether our theories were consistent or not, and we wouldn't

01:16:11 --> 01:16:15
have any truly convincing means of

01:16:14 --> 01:16:19
saying that they were free from contradiction. And

01:16:18 --> 01:16:27
the fact of Gödel's Incompleteness Theorem shows that that is exactly the nature of

01:16:26 --> 01:16:31
mathematical reality, actually. Those are the two incompleteness theorems.

01:16:30 --> 01:16:34
So the first incompleteness theorem says you cannot write

01:16:33 --> 01:16:38
down a computably axiomatizable theory that answers all the

01:16:37 --> 01:16:41
questions. Every such theory will be incomplete,

01:16:40 --> 01:16:45
assuming it includes a certain amount of arithmetic. And

01:16:44 --> 01:16:49
secondly, no such theory can ever prove its own consistency. So not only is

01:16:48 --> 01:16:52
it the case that the finitary theory can't prove the consistency of the

01:16:51 --> 01:16:56
strong infinitary theory, but even the infinitary theory can't prove

01:16:55 --> 01:17:00
its own consistency, right? That's the second incompleteness

01:16:59 --> 01:17:06
theorem. And so it's, in that sense, a decisive takedown of the Hilbert

01:17:05 --> 01:17:10
program, which is really quite remarkable, the

01:17:09 --> 01:17:14
xtent to which his theorem just really answered that

01:17:13 --> 01:17:18
whole puzzle. It's quite amazing.

01:17:17 --> 01:17:22
I mean, there's another aspect, kind of easy to think about.

01:17:21 --> 01:17:26
I mean, if you're wondering about theories that prove their

01:17:25 --> 01:17:30
own consistency, then, I mean, would you trust a theory

01:17:29 --> 01:17:34
that proves of itself that it's consistent? I

01:17:33 --> 01:17:37
mean, that's like... it's like the used car salesman

01:17:37 --> 01:17:42
telling you, "Oh, I'm trustworthy." I mean, it's not a reason

01:17:41 --> 01:17:45
to trust the used car salesman, is it? Just because he says that.

01:17:45 --> 01:17:50
So similarly, if you have a theory that proves its own consistency, well, even an

01:17:49 --> 01:17:53
inconsistent theory would prove its own consistency. And so it doesn't

01:17:53 --> 01:17:57
seem to be a logical reason to believe in the consistency, if you have a

01:17:57 --> 01:17:59
theory that proves itself consistent.

01:17:59 --> 01:18:04
- Just for clarification, you used the word theory.

01:18:03 --> 01:18:08
Is it, in this context, synonymous with axiomatic system?

01:18:07 --> 01:18:12
- Right. So in mathematical logic, "theory" is a technical term.

01:18:11 --> 01:18:16
And it means any set of sentences in a formal language. And so

01:18:15 --> 01:18:20
if you say axiomatic system, it's basically synonymous to my

01:18:19 --> 01:18:23
usage with theory. So a theory means, you know, the

01:18:22 --> 01:18:27
consequences of a set of axioms or... People are sometimes unclear on whether they just mean

01:18:26 --> 01:18:30
the axioms or the consequences of the axioms, but...

01:18:29 --> 01:18:33
- So theory includes both the axioms and the consequences of the

01:18:32 --> 01:18:37
axioms, and you use it interchangeably and the context is supposed to

01:18:36 --> 01:18:41
help you figure out which of the two you're talking about? The axioms

01:18:40 --> 01:18:45
or the consequences? Or maybe to you, they're basically the same?

01:18:44 --> 01:18:48
- Yeah, well, they're so closely connected, although all the features aren't the same.

01:18:47 --> 01:18:56
So if you have a computable list of axioms for a theory, then you

01:18:55 --> 01:19:00
can start enumerating the consequences of the

01:18:59 --> 01:19:03
axioms, but you won't be able to computably decide

01:19:02 --> 01:19:07
whether a given statement is a consequence or not. You can enumerate the

01:19:06 --> 01:19:11
consequences, so you can semi-decide the

01:19:10 --> 01:19:15
consequences, but you won't be able to decide yes or no whether a given statement

01:19:14 --> 01:19:17
is a consequence or not.

01:19:17 --> 01:19:22
So it's the distinction between a problem being computably decidable and a

01:19:21 --> 01:19:25
problem being computably enumerable, which,

01:19:24 --> 01:19:29
was made clear following the work of Turing and others

01:19:28 --> 01:19:33
that came from that. I mean, so that's one difference between the list of

01:19:32 --> 01:19:37
axioms of the theory and the theory itself. The axioms could

01:19:36 --> 01:19:41
be... You can decide, maybe computably, whether something is an axiom or not, but

01:19:40 --> 01:19:45
hat doesn't mean that you can decide computably whether or not something is a theorem

01:19:44 --> 01:19:48
or not. Usually, you only get to decide the positive

01:19:47 --> 01:19:52
instances. If something is a theorem, you will eventually come to recognize that, but if something isn't

01:19:51 --> 01:19:57
a theorem, maybe at no point will you be able to say, "No, that's not a theorem."

01:19:56 --> 01:19:59
- And that's of course connected to the halting problem.

01:19:58 --> 01:20:03
..and all of these contradictions and paradoxes are

01:20:02 --> 01:20:07
all nicely, beautifully interconnected.

01:20:06 --> 01:20:10
So can we just linger on Gödel's incompleteness theorem?

01:20:09 --> 01:20:12
You mentioned the two components there.

01:20:12 --> 01:20:16
You know, there's so many questions to ask, like what is the difference between

01:20:15 --> 01:20:20
provability and truth? What is true and what

01:20:19 --> 01:20:22
is provable? Maybe that's a good line to draw.

01:20:21 --> 01:20:26
- Yeah, this is a really core distinction that

01:20:27 --> 01:20:32
it's fascinating to me to go back and read

01:20:32 --> 01:20:38
even the early 20th century people before Gödel and Tarski,

01:20:38 --> 01:20:42
and they were totally sloppy about this distinction

01:20:41 --> 01:20:46
between truth and proof. It wasn't clear at all

01:20:45 --> 01:20:49
until Gödel, basically. Although even as late as

01:20:49 --> 01:20:53
Bourbaki has the kind of confusion in this

01:20:52 --> 01:20:57
foundational work. So, this standard graduate-level

01:20:56 --> 01:21:04
textbook used in France in the presentation of logic, they are conflating truth and

01:21:03 --> 01:21:08
proof. To be true for them means to be provable. So, in

01:21:07 --> 01:21:12
the early days, maybe it wasn't clear enough that the concept of truth

01:21:11 --> 01:21:16
needed a mathematical investigation or

01:21:15 --> 01:21:20
analysis. Maybe it was already taken to be fully clear.

01:21:19 --> 01:21:23
But because of the incompleteness theorem, we realized that actually there's quite

01:21:23 --> 01:21:27
subtle things happening, right? And so, why don't we talk about

01:21:26 --> 01:21:31
his distinction a bit? To me, it's absolutely core and fundamental

01:21:30 --> 01:21:34
to our understanding of mathematical logic now.

01:21:34 --> 01:21:41
This distinction between truth and proof. So, truth is

01:21:41 --> 01:21:45
on the semantic side of the syntax-semantics dichotomy.

01:21:44 --> 01:21:49
Truth has to do with the nature of reality. I

01:21:48 --> 01:21:53
mean, okay, when I talk about reality, I'm not talking about physical reality.

01:21:52 --> 01:21:57
I'm talking about mathematical reality. So, we have a concept of something being

01:21:56 --> 01:22:01
true in a structure, a statement being true in a mathematical structure.

01:22:00 --> 01:22:05
Like maybe you have the real field or something, and you want to know, does it satisfy this

01:22:04 --> 01:22:09
tatement or that statement? Or you have a group of some kind, or maybe

01:22:08 --> 01:22:12
you have a graph. This is a particular kind of mathematical structure

01:22:12 --> 01:22:16
that has a bunch of vertices and edges, and you want to know

01:22:16 --> 01:22:20
does this graph satisfy that statement? And

01:22:19 --> 01:22:24
Tarski gave this absolutely wonderful account of the

01:22:23 --> 01:22:28
nature of truth in what's now known as the disquotational

01:22:27 --> 01:22:35
theory of truth. And what Tarski says is the sentence, "Snow is white,"

01:22:35 --> 01:22:46
is true if and only if snow is white. And what he means by that is, look, to say

01:22:46 --> 01:22:51
truth is a property of an assertion, so we can think of the assertion as it

01:22:51 --> 01:22:57
syntactically. So, the sentence is true

01:22:57 --> 01:23:01
if and only if the content of the sentence is the case. You

01:23:00 --> 01:23:09
know? So, the sentence, "Snow is white," you know, in quotations is true, that

01:23:08 --> 01:23:13
just means that snow is white. And

01:23:12 --> 01:23:17
that's why it's called the disquotational theory because we remove the quotation marks.

01:23:16 --> 01:23:21
from the assertion, right? And you can use this idea of

01:23:20 --> 01:23:25
disquotation to give a formal definition of truth in a

01:23:24 --> 01:23:29
mathematical structure of a statement in a formal language. So, for example, if I

01:23:28 --> 01:23:32
have a formal language that allows me to make atomic statements

01:23:31 --> 01:23:35
about the objects and relations of the

01:23:35 --> 01:23:39
structure, and I can build up a formal language with, you know,

01:23:38 --> 01:23:42
with the logical connectives of "and" and "or" and

01:23:41 --> 01:23:46
"implies" and "not" and so on, and maybe I have quantifiers, right? Then,

01:23:46 --> 01:23:50
for example, to say that the structure satisfies phi

01:23:50 --> 01:23:54
and psi, that that single statement, phi

01:23:53 --> 01:24:01
and psi, I'm thinking of that as one statement, just means that it satisfies phi

01:24:00 --> 01:24:06
and it satisfies psi. And if you notice what happened there,

01:24:06 --> 01:24:11
I... At first, the 'and' was part of the sentence inside the

01:24:10 --> 01:24:15
sentence, but then in the second part, I was using the word

01:24:14 --> 01:24:18
'and' to refer to the conjunction of the two conditions. So-

01:24:17 --> 01:24:19
Yeah, it has the disquotation.

01:24:20 --> 01:24:25
And so this idea can be done for all the logical connectors and quantifiers and

01:24:24 --> 01:24:28
everything. You're applying Tarski's idea of disquotation,

01:24:28 --> 01:24:33
and it allows you to define by induction the truth of any

01:24:32 --> 01:24:37
assertion in a formal language inside any mathematical structure. And

01:24:36 --> 01:24:41
so to say that a sentence is true, first of all, it's ambiguous

01:24:40 --> 01:24:45
unless you tell me which structure you're talking about it being true in.

01:24:45 --> 01:24:50
And so maybe we have in mind the standard model of arithmetic

01:24:49 --> 01:24:54
or something with the natural numbers and the arithmetic structure, and I want to know is a given statement

01:24:53 --> 01:24:57
rue in that structure. Then we have a formal definition of what that means

01:24:56 --> 01:25:05
according to the Tarski recursive definition of truth. Okay, that's

01:25:04 --> 01:25:09
truth. Proof, on the other hand, is, you know, in

01:25:08 --> 01:25:13
this Hilbert way of thinking, we can develop proof theory. What is

01:25:12 --> 01:25:16
a proof for a mathematician, for a mathematical logician? A

01:25:15 --> 01:25:23
proof is a certain sequence or arrangement of sentences in the formal language that

01:25:22 --> 01:25:27
accord with the logical rules of a proof

01:25:26 --> 01:25:30
system. So there are certain modes of reasoning that are

01:25:29 --> 01:25:34
allowed. So if you know A and you know A implies B in

01:25:33 --> 01:25:38
the proof, then at a later step you're allowed to

01:25:37 --> 01:25:42
write B as a consequence. So if you know A

01:25:41 --> 01:25:45
and you know A implies B, those are both two statements that are known, then

01:25:45 --> 01:25:50
you can deduce B as a consequence according to the rule of modus

01:25:49 --> 01:25:54
ponens. This is the rule of modus ponens. And, you know, there are a lot of other

01:25:53 --> 01:25:58
ules. Some people would call this implication elimination. There are

01:25:57 --> 01:26:01
different kinds of proof systems. There are a lot of different formal

01:26:01 --> 01:26:06
proof systems that exist that are studied by the proof theorists, and all of them have

01:26:05 --> 01:26:10
the property that they're sound, which means

01:26:09 --> 01:26:14
that if the premises of the argument are all

01:26:13 --> 01:26:17
true in a structure and you have a proof to get a

01:26:16 --> 01:26:21
conclusion, then the conclusion is also true in that structure. So

01:26:20 --> 01:26:27
that's what it means to be sound. Proofs preserve truth. They're truth-

01:26:26 --> 01:26:34
preserving arguments. Okay? But also the proof systems are

01:26:33 --> 01:26:38
also generally complete. They're both sound and complete, and complete

01:26:37 --> 01:26:45
means that whenever a statement is a consequence, a logical consequence of some

01:26:44 --> 01:26:49
other statements, which means that whenever the assumptions are true, then

01:26:48 --> 01:26:52
the consequence is also true in the structure.

01:26:52 --> 01:26:56
So whenever you have a logical consequence, then there is a proof of

01:26:55 --> 01:27:00
it. Okay? And the proof systems generally

01:26:59 --> 01:27:04
have both of those properties; they're sound and complete. There's a third property a lot of logicians

01:27:03 --> 01:27:05
talk about

01:27:04 --> 01:27:09
sound and complete, sound and complete this, sound and complete that. But actually, there's a hidden

01:27:08 --> 01:27:14
third adjective that they should always be talking about in any such case, which is

01:27:13 --> 01:27:18
that you should be able to recognize whether or

01:27:17 --> 01:27:22
not something is a proof or not. So there's a computable aspect

01:27:22 --> 01:27:26
o the proof systems. We want to be able to recognize whether something is a proof.

01:27:25 --> 01:27:30
It should be computably decidable whether a given sequence of statements is a proof

01:27:29 --> 01:27:34
or not. So we don't want a proof system in which

01:27:33 --> 01:27:38
someone claims to have a proof, but we can't check that fact, whether

01:27:37 --> 01:27:44
it's a proof or not. We want to be able to correctly adjudicate

01:27:43 --> 01:27:47
all claims to having a proof.

01:27:46 --> 01:27:51
- Yeah. A mathematician comes to mind that said he has a proof, but the margins are

01:27:50 --> 01:27:52
too small

01:27:51 --> 01:27:52
- That's right.

01:27:51 --> 01:27:53
- to continue.

01:27:52 --> 01:27:54
- Exactly. So

01:27:53 --> 01:27:55
- So that doesn't count as a proof.

01:27:55 --> 01:27:59
- Yeah. So generally, all the classical proof systems that are used are sound

01:27:59 --> 01:28:03
and complete and also computably decidable in the sense that we can decide whether

01:28:02 --> 01:28:05
something is a proof or not.

01:28:04 --> 01:28:09
- So what is, again, the tension between truth and proof?

01:28:08 --> 01:28:13
Which is more powerful, and how do the two interplay

01:28:12 --> 01:28:16
with the contradictions that we've been discussing?

01:28:15 --> 01:28:20
- Right. So the incompleteness theorem is the question whether we

01:28:19 --> 01:28:22
could, say, write down a theory

01:28:22 --> 01:28:27
for arithmetic. Say, for the standard model of arithmetic where we have the natural

01:28:26 --> 01:28:31
numbers and plus and times and zero, one, and less than, and so on.

01:28:30 --> 01:28:35
In that formal language, we can express an enormous number of

01:28:34 --> 01:28:38
statements about the nature not only of arithmetic, but actually by

01:28:38 --> 01:28:42
various coding methods, we can express essentially all of finite

01:28:41 --> 01:28:46
mathematics in that structure. So the question would be, can

01:28:45 --> 01:28:50
we write down a computable list of axioms that will answer

01:28:49 --> 01:28:54
all those questions by proof? In other words, we want to

01:28:53 --> 01:29:00
have a complete theory, a theory of arithmetic that proves all and only the true

01:29:00 --> 01:29:04
statements. That would be the goal. Hilbert would love that. I mean, that would

01:29:04 --> 01:29:08
be supportive of Hilbert's program to have such a complete

01:29:07 --> 01:29:12
theory of arithmetic, and Godel proved that this is

01:29:11 --> 01:29:16
impossible. You cannot write down a computable list of axioms that is complete

01:29:15 --> 01:29:20
in that sense. There will always be statements... If the theory is

01:29:19 --> 01:29:24
consistent, there will always be statements that you cannot prove and you cannot

01:29:23 --> 01:29:27
refute. So they are independent of that theory.

01:29:26 --> 01:29:31
- How traumatic is that, that there are statements that are independent from the theory?

01:29:30 --> 01:29:37
- I mean, my view is that this isn't traumatic at all. This is rather

01:29:38 --> 01:29:43
completely eye-opening in terms of our

01:29:42 --> 01:29:50
understanding of the nature of mathematical reality. I mean, we're not...

01:29:49 --> 01:29:54
We understand this profound fact about our

01:29:53 --> 01:29:58
situation with regard to mathematical truth.

01:29:57 --> 01:30:02
The incompleteness theorem tells us, look, we just can't write down a list of

01:30:01 --> 01:30:06
axioms that is going to be consistent and it's going to answer all the questions.

01:30:05 --> 01:30:10
It's impossible. And so I don't think of it as trauma.

01:30:09 --> 01:30:13
I just think, look, this is the nature of mathematical reality and it's good that

01:30:12 --> 01:30:17
we know it, and so now we need to move on from that. And, you know,

01:30:16 --> 01:30:20
do what we can in light of that.

01:30:19 --> 01:30:23
- Is it fair to say that in general it means if I give you a

01:30:22 --> 01:30:32
statement, you can't know if your axiomatic system would be able to prove it?

01:30:31 --> 01:30:36
- That's right. In general, you cannot. The provability problem,

01:30:35 --> 01:30:39
we can formulate it as a decision problem. Given a theory and given a

01:30:38 --> 01:30:43
statement, is that statement a consequence of that theory? Yeah.

01:30:42 --> 01:30:47
This is one of the most famous decision problems. In fact, the very first one,

01:30:46 --> 01:30:50
because it's equivalent to the Hilbert-Ackermann

01:30:49 --> 01:30:54
Entscheidungsproblem, which is also appearing in the title of

01:30:53 --> 01:31:02
Turing's 1936 paper that was so important for computability theory. So, it's a

01:31:01 --> 01:31:06
formulation of the Entscheidungsproblem. Does a given theory

01:31:05 --> 01:31:10
have a given statement as a logical consequence? Which, because

01:31:09 --> 01:31:14
of Gödel's completeness theorem, not his incompleteness theorem, but his earlier

01:31:13 --> 01:31:18
completeness theorem, Gödel had proved that the proof systems that they

01:31:17 --> 01:31:22
studied did have this completeness property that I mentioned. So provability

01:31:21 --> 01:31:26
is the same as logical consequence. And this is an undecidable decision

01:31:25 --> 01:31:31
problem. Turing proved and we now know it's equivalent to the Halting Problem.

01:31:30 --> 01:31:35
- Can you describe the Halting Problem? Because it's a thing that shows up in a

01:31:34 --> 01:31:39
very useful and, again, traumatic way through a lot of computer

01:31:38 --> 01:31:40
science, through a lot of mathematics.

01:31:39 --> 01:31:44
- Yeah. The Halting Problem is expressing a fundamental

01:31:43 --> 01:31:47
property of computational processes. So,

01:31:47 --> 01:31:52
given a program, or maybe we think of it as a program together with its input,

01:31:51 --> 01:31:55
but let me just call it a program. So given a program, we could run that

01:31:54 --> 01:31:59
program, but I want to pose it as a decision

01:31:58 --> 01:32:02
problem. Will this program ever complete its

01:32:01 --> 01:32:06
task? Will it ever halt? And the Halting Problem is the question, given

01:32:05 --> 01:32:14
a program, will it halt? Yes or no? And, of course, for

01:32:13 --> 01:32:17
any one instance, the answer's either yes or no. That's not what we're talking

01:32:16 --> 01:32:21
about. We're talking about whether there's a computable procedure to answer

01:32:20 --> 01:32:25
all instances of this question. So, it's a decision problem is

01:32:24 --> 01:32:29
given as a scheme of instances for all possible programs that you could ask

01:32:28 --> 01:32:33
about. What I want to know is, is there a computable procedure that will answer

01:32:32 --> 01:32:34
those questions?

01:32:34 --> 01:32:38
And it turns out the answer's no. The Halting Problem is computably

01:32:37 --> 01:32:42
undecidable. There is no computable procedure that will correctly

01:32:41 --> 01:32:49
answer all instances of whether a given program will halt. And of course, we can

01:32:48 --> 01:32:52
get half the answers in the sense that

01:32:52 --> 01:32:57
you give me a program and you say, "Will this halt?" And I could take that

01:32:56 --> 01:33:00
program and I could run it.

01:32:59 --> 01:33:03
And I could keep running it, and maybe in a week, it would

01:33:02 --> 01:33:06
halt. And at that time, I could say, "Yes, it

01:33:05 --> 01:33:12
halted." So I can get the yes answers correctly for halting, all the yes answers.

01:33:13 --> 01:33:15
But the problem is

01:33:17 --> 01:33:21
if it didn't halt yet, like maybe I waited, you know, a thousand years

01:33:21 --> 01:33:23
and it still hasn't halted,

01:33:23 --> 01:33:28
I don't seem entitled to say, "No, it's not going to halt." Yet, because

01:33:27 --> 01:33:32
maybe in a thousand and one years, it'll halt. And so at no point can I

01:33:31 --> 01:33:35
seem to say, "No." In order to say, "No, it won't ever halt," it

01:33:35 --> 01:33:40
seems like I would have to really understand how the program

01:33:39 --> 01:33:41
worked and what it was doing.

01:33:41 --> 01:33:46
So giving the "yes" answers was sort of trivial. You didn't have to understand it. You just needed

01:33:45 --> 01:33:50
to run it, which is a kind of rote task. But to give the "no" answers,

01:33:49 --> 01:33:54
you need to have a kind of deep insight into the nature of the program and what it's

01:33:53 --> 01:33:59
doing in such a way that you would understand it and be able to see, "Oh, no, I can

01:33:58 --> 01:34:03
see this program is never gonna halt." Because, you know, it's a much more

01:34:02 --> 01:34:07
difficult task to say, "No, it won't halt," than it is to say, "Yes,

01:34:06 --> 01:34:11
it halted because I ran it and it halted." And it turns

01:34:10 --> 01:34:15
out to be impossible to have a computable procedure that gives

01:34:14 --> 01:34:19
the "no" answers, you know? And the argument is not very difficult. Should we do it?

01:34:18 --> 01:34:20
- Yes, let's do it.

01:34:19 --> 01:34:24
- Okay. Suppose toward contradiction. I mean, all these proofs are by

01:34:23 --> 01:34:28
contradiction, and this argument is going to be a diagonal argument in the same

01:34:27 --> 01:34:31
style as the Russell argument and the Cantor argument and

01:34:30 --> 01:34:35
Gödel's argument that we haven't talked about yet. So many diagonal

01:34:34 --> 01:34:37
arguments come in. So

01:34:37 --> 01:34:42
suppose towards contradiction that we had a procedure for determining whether a given program

01:34:41 --> 01:34:49
halted on a given input. Now, let me describe. I'm gonna use that procedure

01:34:48 --> 01:34:53
as a subroutine in the following process. And

01:34:52 --> 01:34:57
my process, let's call it Q, process Q, and it takes as input

01:34:57 --> 01:35:05
a program P, okay? And the first thing it does is it asks that subroutine,

01:35:04 --> 01:35:09
"Hey, would P halt if I ran it on

01:35:08 --> 01:35:15
P itself?" Okay, that's the diagonal part because we're applying P to P, right?

01:35:14 --> 01:35:19
Okay, so I'm describing program Q, and program Q takes as input

01:35:18 --> 01:35:23
P, which is itself a program. And the first thing it does is it asks

01:35:22 --> 01:35:26
the halting subroutine program, "Would P halt on

01:35:25 --> 01:35:30
P?" And if the answer comes back from the subroutine,

01:35:29 --> 01:35:34
"Yeah, that would halt," then what I do in program Q

01:35:34 --> 01:35:38
is I immediately jump into an infinite loop. So I don't

01:35:37 --> 01:35:42
halt. If P halts on P, I don't halt.

01:35:41 --> 01:35:46
But if the answer came back, "No, P is never gonna halt on P," then

01:35:45 --> 01:35:54
I halt immediately. Okay, so and that's it. I've described what Q does.

01:35:53 --> 01:36:01
And the thing about Q is that Q's behavior on P was the opposite of P's

01:36:00 --> 01:36:09
behavior on P. I mean, that's how we designed Q specifically so that Q on P

01:36:09 --> 01:36:14
had the opposite behavior as P on P. Okay, so

01:36:13 --> 01:36:17
now, of course, what do we do? Well, the same thing that

01:36:16 --> 01:36:24
Russell did and so forth, and Cantor, we ask, "Well, what would Q do on

01:36:23 --> 01:36:28
Q?" And because of this opposite behavior,

01:36:28 --> 01:36:32
Q would halt on Q if and only if Q does not halt on

01:36:31 --> 01:36:36
Q, which is a contradiction, because Q has to have the opposite behavior on

01:36:35 --> 01:36:39
Q than Q does, but that's just contradictory.

01:36:38 --> 01:36:40
- What a beautiful proof. Simple.

01:36:39 --> 01:36:44
- It's absolutely beautiful. Yeah, I agree. And it's following the

01:36:43 --> 01:36:48
same logic of Russell and Cantor. I mean, going back to

01:36:47 --> 01:36:51
Cantor basically, because Russell is also quoting Cantor in

01:36:50 --> 01:36:55
his letter to Frege. So, therefore, the

01:36:54 --> 01:36:59
conclusion is that the halting problem is not computably

01:36:58 --> 01:37:03
decidable. And now we can immediately prove Godel's

01:37:02 --> 01:37:07
decidable. And now we can immediately prove Gödel's theorem using this, actually. It's an immediate consequence. So why don't we just do that?

01:37:06 --> 01:37:08
I view this as the simplest proof

01:37:08 --> 01:37:13
of Gödel's theorem. You don't need the Gödel sentence to

01:37:12 --> 01:37:19
prove Gödel's theorem. You can do it with the halting problem. So, suppose

01:37:19 --> 01:37:24
that we could write down a computable axiomatization of all

01:37:23 --> 01:37:30
of the true facts of elementary mathematics, meaning arithmetic

01:37:29 --> 01:37:34
and finite combinatorial things such as Turing

01:37:33 --> 01:37:37
machine computations and so on. So in fact, all those

01:37:37 --> 01:37:41
finite combinatorial processes are formalizable inside arithmetic with the

01:37:40 --> 01:37:45
standard arithmetization coding process. But let

01:37:44 --> 01:37:49
me just be a little bit informal and say suppose we could write down a complete

01:37:48 --> 01:37:51
me just be a little bit informal and say, suppose we could write down a complete theory of elementary finite mathematics.

01:37:52 --> 01:37:56
So we have a, an axiomatization of that theory.

01:37:56 --> 01:38:00
So we have an axiomatization of that theory. Then we could produce all possible theorems from those

01:38:00 --> 01:38:04
axioms in the way that I was describing earlier with

01:38:03 --> 01:38:08
Hilbert's program. I mean, if we had a complete theory of elementary

01:38:07 --> 01:38:12
mathematics, we could construct a theorem enumeration machine that

01:38:11 --> 01:38:15
produced all the theorems and only the theorems from that

01:38:15 --> 01:38:23
heory.... so now, I have this theorem enumeration device on my desk, and I announce

01:38:22 --> 01:38:27
theory. So now I have this theorem enumeration device on my desk, and I announce that I'm open for business to solve the halting problem. So you give me a program

01:38:26 --> 01:38:30
and input that you wanna run that program on,

01:38:29 --> 01:38:34
and input that you want to run that program on, and I'm going to answer the halting problem. And the way I'm going to do it is I'm just

01:38:33 --> 01:38:35
gonna wait for the

01:38:35 --> 01:38:40
going to wait for the statement coming out of the theorem enumeration device that asserts either that

01:38:39 --> 01:38:44
P does halt on that input or I wait for the statement that P does not

01:38:43 --> 01:38:48
halt on that input. But one of them's going to happen because it was a

01:38:47 --> 01:38:51
complete theory that was enumerating all the true statements of elementary

01:38:50 --> 01:38:55
mathematics. So therefore, if I had such a system, I could solve the halting

01:38:54 --> 01:38:59
problem, but we already proved that you cannot solve the halting problem,

01:38:58 --> 01:39:03
so therefore you cannot have such a complete theory of

01:39:02 --> 01:39:05
arithmetic. So that proves Gödel's theorem.

01:39:05 --> 01:39:10
- Maybe to take a little bit of a tangent, can you speak... You've written a wonderful book about proofs and the art of mathematics.

01:39:09 --> 01:39:14
So what can you say about proving stuff in mathematics? What is the process of

01:39:17 --> 01:39:21
proof? What are the tools? What is the art? What is the science of

01:39:20 --> 01:39:23
proving things in mathematics?

01:39:22 --> 01:39:27
- proving things in mathematics? So this is something that I find so wonderful to teach young

01:39:26 --> 01:39:31
mathematicians who are learning how to become mathematicians and

01:39:30 --> 01:39:35
learning about proof, and I wrote that book when I was teaching such a

01:39:34 --> 01:39:38
proofwriting class in New York.

01:39:37 --> 01:39:42
So many universities have such a course, the proofreading course, which is usually

01:39:41 --> 01:39:46
taken by students who have learned some mathematics.

01:39:45 --> 01:39:49
Usually, they've completed maybe the calculus sequence and are making the kind of

01:39:48 --> 01:39:53
transition to higher mathematics, which tends to involve

01:39:52 --> 01:39:56
much more proof, and it's a kind of challenging step for them.

01:39:56 --> 01:40:01
Many math departments have this kind of course on proofwriting where the

01:40:00 --> 01:40:05
students would get exposed to how to write proofs. I wasn't

01:40:04 --> 01:40:09
happy with most of the other books that exist for those

01:40:08 --> 01:40:16
kind of courses, and the reason was that they were so often so dull because

01:40:15 --> 01:40:20
they would concentrate on, like, these-

01:40:20 --> 01:40:24
totally uninteresting parts of what it's like to write a

01:40:23 --> 01:40:26
proof, these kind of mechanistic

01:40:26 --> 01:40:31
procedures about how to write a proof. You know, if you're going to prove an

01:40:30 --> 01:40:35
implication, then you assume the hypothesis and argue for the conclusion, and so on.

01:40:34 --> 01:40:39
And all of that is true and fine and that's good to know, except if that's all that you're

01:40:38 --> 01:40:43
saying about the nature of proof, then I don't think you're really

01:40:42 --> 01:40:47
learning very much. So I felt that it was possible to have a

01:40:46 --> 01:40:50
much better kind of book, one that was much more

01:40:49 --> 01:40:54
interesting and that had interesting theorems in

01:40:53 --> 01:40:58
it that still admitted of elementary proof. So I wrote this

01:40:57 --> 01:41:02
book and tried to fill it with all of the

01:41:01 --> 01:41:06
compelling mathematical statements with very elementary

01:41:05 --> 01:41:10
proofs that exhibited lots of different proof styles in

01:41:09 --> 01:41:13
it. And so, I found that the students appreciated it a lot.

01:41:12 --> 01:41:17
- We should say, we dedicate the book to my students, may all their

01:41:16 --> 01:41:21
theorems be true, proved by elegant arguments that flow effortlessly from

01:41:20 --> 01:41:27
hypothesis to conclusion while revealing fantastical mathematical beauty.

01:41:27 --> 01:41:32
Are there some interesting proofs that maybe illustrate,

01:41:33 --> 01:41:38
for people outside of mathematics or for people who just take math classes-

01:41:37 --> 01:41:38
Right

01:41:37 --> 01:41:40
- ...in high school and so on?

01:41:39 --> 01:41:42
- Yeah, let's do a proof. There's one in the book.

01:41:41 --> 01:41:45
We can talk about it. I think it's a nice problem. It's in

01:41:44 --> 01:41:49
the discrete math, yeah, the 5.1, that one,

01:41:48 --> 01:41:53
more pointed at than pointing. Okay. So this is the

01:41:52 --> 01:41:56
following problem. Suppose you're gathered with some

01:41:55 --> 01:42:00
friends, you know, in a circle, and you can point

01:41:59 --> 01:42:03
at each other however you want, or yourself, whatever, it doesn't

01:42:02 --> 01:42:07
matter, and you can point at more than one person, you know, use all your fingers or

01:42:06 --> 01:42:11
your feet or whatever you want. So maybe you point at three of your friends or

01:42:10 --> 01:42:15
something and they point at two or three of their friends or whatever, and one person

01:42:14 --> 01:42:19
is pointing at 10 people and somebody isn't pointing at anybody maybe, or and various

01:42:18 --> 01:42:25
people are pointed at also, right? So the question is, could we arrange a pattern of

01:42:24 --> 01:42:29
pointing so that everyone was more pointed at

01:42:30 --> 01:42:34
han they are pointing at others? So in other words, maybe

01:42:33 --> 01:42:38
there's seven people pointing at me, but I'm only pointing at five people and

01:42:37 --> 01:42:42
maybe there's, you know, 20 people pointing at you,

01:42:41 --> 01:42:46
but you're only pointing at 15 people or something like that, right? So I want to

01:42:45 --> 01:42:49
know. There's a similar question on Twitter.

01:42:49 --> 01:42:54
For a group of people on Twitter, could you arrange

01:42:54 --> 01:43:01
that everyone has more followers than following? Yeah, it's the same question.

01:43:00 --> 01:43:03
Mathematically, it's identical.

01:43:02 --> 01:43:07
Although, I don't know, it's not identical, because I said you could point at yourself,

01:43:06 --> 01:43:10
and I think that's not... Can you follow yourself?

01:43:09 --> 01:43:11
- No, I don't think so, no.

01:43:10 --> 01:43:14
- I don't think you can. Okay. So can you arrange it

01:43:13 --> 01:43:18
so that everyone is more pointed at than pointing? And in my book,

01:43:17 --> 01:43:22
I give a couple of different proofs of this. I think I give an induction proof

01:43:21 --> 01:43:26
and then there's another proof. I think there's three different proofs in there.

01:43:25 --> 01:43:28
But why don't we just talk about my favorite proof?

01:43:28 --> 01:43:33
Suppose it were possible to arrange that we're all more pointed at than pointing,

01:43:32 --> 01:43:36
okay? Now what we're going to do, we're going to agree,

01:43:36 --> 01:43:41
we're going to give a dollar to everyone that we're pointing at.

01:43:40 --> 01:43:45
Okay? And so what happens? Everybody made money,

01:43:46 --> 01:43:51
because I was pointed at by more people than I'm pointing, so I got

01:43:50 --> 01:43:55
$10, but I only paid out $7. And similarly, you

01:43:54 --> 01:43:58
got paid $20, but you only paid out $15. So,

01:43:57 --> 01:44:02
if everyone is more pointed at than pointing, then everyone makes money.

01:44:01 --> 01:44:06
But it's obviously impossible for us to make money as a group by just trading

01:44:05 --> 01:44:08
money with ourselves.

01:44:07 --> 01:44:13
And therefore, it can't be possible that we're all more pointed at than pointing.

01:44:13 --> 01:44:17
And this proof illustrates something. It's one of my habits that I

01:44:16 --> 01:44:22
suggest in the book: to anthropomorphize your mathematical ideas.

01:44:22 --> 01:44:27
You should imagine that the mathematical objects

01:44:26 --> 01:44:29
that are playing a role in your question are

01:44:29 --> 01:44:33
people, or active, somehow, animals, or

01:44:32 --> 01:44:37
something that maybe have a will and a goal and so on.

01:44:36 --> 01:44:41
This is this process of anthropomorphizing.

01:44:40 --> 01:44:45
And it often makes the problems easier to understand because we all

01:44:44 --> 01:44:49
are familiar with the fact that it's difficult to make money,

01:44:48 --> 01:44:53
and the proof is totally convincing because of our knowledge that

01:44:52 --> 01:44:56
we can't make money as a group by trading

01:44:56 --> 01:45:02
dollars between us, you know, without any new money coming into the group.

01:45:01 --> 01:45:05
But that by itself is actually a difficult

01:45:04 --> 01:45:08
mathematical claim. I mean, if someone had to

01:45:07 --> 01:45:12
prove that you can't make money by trading within a

01:45:11 --> 01:45:16
group, you know, it can't be that everyone in the group

01:45:15 --> 01:45:20
makes money just by shifting money around in the group. Maybe you think that's

01:45:19 --> 01:45:23
obvious, and it is obvious if you think about money. But if you had asked the

01:45:22 --> 01:45:27
question, you know, about mathematical functions of a certain kind and so on,

01:45:26 --> 01:45:31
then maybe it wouldn't be as clear as it is when you're talking about this

01:45:30 --> 01:45:33
money thing, because of

01:45:33 --> 01:45:38
we can build on our human experience about the difficulty of getting

01:45:37 --> 01:45:42
money and, you know, or other resources. It doesn't have to be money, it could be candy,

01:45:41 --> 01:45:46
whatever. You know, we just know that you can't easily get

01:45:45 --> 01:45:49
more things in that kind just by trading within a group.

01:45:48 --> 01:45:53
- And we should say that sometimes the power of proof is such that the

01:45:52 --> 01:45:57
non-obvious can be shown, and then over time that becomes obvious.

01:45:56 --> 01:45:59
So in the context of money,

01:45:58 --> 01:46:02
or social systems, there's a bunch of things that are non-obvious.

01:46:01 --> 01:46:06
And the whole point is that proof can guide us

01:46:05 --> 01:46:10
to the truth, to the accurate description

01:46:09 --> 01:46:13
of reality. We just proved a property of money.

01:46:14 --> 01:46:19
- It's interesting to think about, well, what if there were infinitely many

01:46:18 --> 01:46:23
people in your, in your group? Then it's not true anymore.

01:46:22 --> 01:46:27
The theorem fails. In fact, you can arrange that everyone is strictly more

01:46:26 --> 01:46:35
pointed than pointing. And also, you can if everyone has even just one dollar bill—

01:46:35 --> 01:46:39
...then you can arrange that afterwards everyone has infinitely many dollar

01:46:39 --> 01:46:43
bills. Cause in terms of cardinality, that's the same. It's just, say, countable infinity in

01:46:42 --> 01:46:47
each case. If you had countably many friends and everyone has one dollar bill, then you can arrange

01:46:46 --> 01:46:51
a pattern of passing those dollar bills amongst each other so that afterwards

01:46:50 --> 01:46:55
everyone has infinitely many dollar bills. What you need is for each person

01:46:54 --> 01:46:59
to be attached to, you know, one of the train cars or

01:46:58 --> 01:47:03
something. So, think of everyone as coming from Hilbert's train,

01:47:03 --> 01:47:07
but also think of them as fitting into Hilbert's Hotel. So just have

01:47:06 --> 01:47:11
veryone on the Nth car give all their

01:47:10 --> 01:47:15
money to the person who ends up in the Nth room. So they each

01:47:14 --> 01:47:19
give one dollar to that person. So afterwards, that person has infinitely many dollars, but

01:47:18 --> 01:47:24
everyone only paid out one dollar. So it's a way of making it happen.

01:47:23 --> 01:47:28
- To what degree, sticking on the topic of infinity,

01:47:27 --> 01:47:32
should we think of infinity as something real?

01:47:33 --> 01:47:38
- That's an excellent question. I mean, a huge part of the philosophy of

01:47:37 --> 01:47:41
mathematics is about this kind of question: what is the nature of

01:47:41 --> 01:47:46
the existence of mathematical objects, including infinity?

01:47:45 --> 01:47:50
But I think asking about infinity specifically is

01:47:49 --> 01:47:54
not that different than asking about the number five. What is—

01:47:53 --> 01:47:58
...what does it mean for the number five to exist? What are the numbers

01:47:57 --> 01:48:03
really, right? This is maybe one of the fundamental questions of mathematical

01:48:02 --> 01:48:07
ontology. I mean, there's many different positions to take on the question of the nature of

01:48:06 --> 01:48:11
the existence of mathematical objects or abstract objects in

01:48:10 --> 01:48:15
general. And there's a certain kind of conversation that sometimes happens

01:48:14 --> 01:48:22
when you do that, and it goes something like this. Sometimes people find it

01:48:21 --> 01:48:26
problematic to talk about the existence of abstract objects

01:48:25 --> 01:48:29
uch as numbers, and there seems to be a kind of wish that we

01:48:29 --> 01:48:33
could give an account of the existence of numbers or other

01:48:32 --> 01:48:36
mathematical objects or abstract objects that was more

01:48:35 --> 01:48:42
like, you know, the existence of tables and chairs and rocks and so on.

01:48:42 --> 01:48:50
And so there seems to be this desire to reduce mathematical existence to

01:48:49 --> 01:48:54
something, you know, that we can experience physically in the real

01:48:53 --> 01:48:59
world. But my attitude about this attempt

01:49:00 --> 01:49:05
is that it's very backward, I think, because

01:49:06 --> 01:49:11
I don't think we have such a clear understanding of the nature of

01:49:10 --> 01:49:15
physical objects, actually. I mean, we all have experience

01:49:14 --> 01:49:19
about existing in the physical world, as we must, because we do

01:49:18 --> 01:49:23
exist in the physical world, but I don't know of any

01:49:24 --> 01:49:28
satisfactory account of what it means to exist

01:49:27 --> 01:49:35
physically. I mean, if I ask you, say, "Imagine a certain kind of

01:49:35 --> 01:49:40
steam locomotive," you know, and I describe the engineering of

01:49:39 --> 01:49:45
it and the weight of it and the nature of the gear linkages and

01:49:45 --> 01:49:50
you know, and I show you schematic drawings of the whole design and so

01:49:49 --> 01:49:53
n and, you know, we talk in detail about every single detailed

01:49:52 --> 01:49:57
aspect of this steam locomotive. But then suppose

01:49:56 --> 01:50:01
after all that conversation, I say, "Okay, now I would like

01:50:00 --> 01:50:05
you to tell me what would it mean for it to exist physically, I mean, as

01:50:04 --> 01:50:08
opposed to just being an imaginary steam locomotive?" Then

01:50:08 --> 01:50:13
what, what could you possibly say about it? I

01:50:12 --> 01:50:17
mean, except by saying, "Oh, I just mean that it exists in the physical world." But what does that mean?

01:50:16 --> 01:50:20
That's the question, right? It's not an answer to the question. That is the question.

01:50:20 --> 01:50:24
So I don't think that there's anything sensible that

01:50:23 --> 01:50:28
we can say about the nature of physical existence. It is a profound

01:50:27 --> 01:50:32
mystery. In fact, it becomes more and more mysterious

01:50:31 --> 01:50:36
the more physics we know. I mean, back in, say,

01:50:35 --> 01:50:40
Newtonian physics, one had a picture of the nature of physical objects

01:50:39 --> 01:50:44
as, you know, little billiard balls or something, or maybe they're infinitely divisible or something

01:50:43 --> 01:50:48
like that. Okay, but then this picture is upset with the

01:50:47 --> 01:50:52
atomic theory of matter. But then that picture's upset

01:50:51 --> 01:50:55
when we realize that the atoms actually can be split and consist of electrons and

01:50:55 --> 01:50:59
protons and neutrons and so on. But then that picture's upset

01:50:58 --> 01:51:03
when we realize that those things themselves are built out of quarks and leptons and so on, and

01:51:02 --> 01:51:07
who knows what's coming. Furthermore, all of those things, the nature of their

01:51:06 --> 01:51:11
existence is actually as wave functions in

01:51:10 --> 01:51:15
you know, some cloud of probability and so on. So it just becomes

01:51:14 --> 01:51:19
more and more and more mysterious the more we learn, and not at all

01:51:18 --> 01:51:23
clarifying. So the nature of what it means to

01:51:22 --> 01:51:26
say that, you know, there's an apple on my

01:51:25 --> 01:51:29
desk, and to give an account of what that physical existence

01:51:29 --> 01:51:34
really is at bottom, I think, is totally absent. Whereas we

01:51:33 --> 01:51:37
do seem to have a much more

01:51:36 --> 01:51:41
satisfactory account of the nature of abstract existence. I mean, I

01:51:40 --> 01:51:45
can talk about the nature of the empty set. You know, this is the

01:51:44 --> 01:51:49
predicate which is never true or something like that. I can talk

01:51:48 --> 01:51:52
about those kind of logical properties or the

01:51:51 --> 01:51:56
singleton of the empty set and so on. I mean, of course, it's very difficult if you go very

01:51:56 --> 01:52:01
far with it, but the point is that it doesn't get more and more mysterious. The more that you

01:52:00 --> 01:52:08
say, it becomes only more and more clear. So it seems to me that,

01:52:07 --> 01:52:12
we don't really have any understanding of what the

01:52:11 --> 01:52:16
physical world is as opposed to the abstract world, and it's the

01:52:15 --> 01:52:20
abstract world where existence is much more clear.

01:52:19 --> 01:52:21
- It is very true

01:52:21 --> 01:52:26
that we don't know anything about the soda bottle or the steam locomotive just because we can

01:52:25 --> 01:52:30
poke at it. Again, we anthropomorphize, and that actually gets us into

01:52:29 --> 01:52:34
trouble sometimes because I'm not feeling the quantum mechanics when

01:52:33 --> 01:52:35
I'm touching it.

01:52:34 --> 01:52:35
- That's right.

01:52:34 --> 01:52:40
- And therefore, it's easy to forget and feel like this is real and

01:52:41 --> 01:52:45
mathematical objects are not, but you're making the opposite argument.

01:52:44 --> 01:52:49
When you draw a distinction between numerals and numbers, which numerals are

01:52:48 --> 01:52:52
the representation of the number on the page, and so on,

01:52:52 --> 01:52:58
but could you say that a number is real? Do numbers exist?

01:52:57 --> 01:53:02
- I happen to think so. I mean, I'm on the side of realism in

01:53:01 --> 01:53:06
mathematics, and I think that these abstract objects do have a real

01:53:05 --> 01:53:11
existence in a way that we can give an account of, in a way I just tried to describe.

01:53:10 --> 01:53:15
- So, you would describe it as the size of a set with four elements in it?

01:53:14 --> 01:53:19
- Well, there are different ways to understand the nature of four. I mean, actually, this gets into

01:53:18 --> 01:53:25
the question of structuralism, which is maybe a good place to talk about it.

01:53:24 --> 01:53:26
- What is structuralism?

01:53:25 --> 01:53:30
- Structuralism is a philosophical position in mathematics,

01:53:29 --> 01:53:34
or the philosophy of mathematics, by which one emphasizes that what's

01:53:33 --> 01:53:38
important about mathematical objects is not what they're made out of or

01:53:37 --> 01:53:41
what their substance or essence is, but rather how they function in a

01:53:40 --> 01:53:45
mathematical structure. And so, what I call the

01:53:44 --> 01:53:49
structuralist attitude in mathematics is that we should

01:53:48 --> 01:53:53
only care about our mathematical structures up

01:53:52 --> 01:53:57
to isomorphism. If I have a mathematical structure of a certain kind,

01:53:56 --> 01:54:01
and I make an exact copy of it using different individuals

01:54:00 --> 01:54:03
to form the elements of that structure,

01:54:03 --> 01:54:08
then the isomorphic copy is just as good mathematically, and there's no

01:54:07 --> 01:54:12
important mathematical difference that would ever arise

01:54:11 --> 01:54:15
from working with this isomorphic copy instead of the original

01:54:14 --> 01:54:19
structure. And so, therefore, that's another way of saying

01:54:18 --> 01:54:23
that the substance of individuals, you know, in a mathematical

01:54:22 --> 01:54:29
structure is irrelevant with regard to any mathematical property of that

01:54:28 --> 01:54:38
structure. And so, to ask a question like, "What is the number four really?" is an

01:54:37 --> 01:54:42
ti-structuralist thing because, you know, if you have

01:54:41 --> 01:54:46
a structure, say, the natural numbers, you know, with all the numbers in it: 0,

01:54:45 --> 01:54:48
1, 2, 3, 4, and so on, then

01:54:48 --> 01:54:53
I could replace the number four with something else, like, you

01:54:52 --> 01:54:55
know, this bottle of water

01:54:55 --> 01:55:00
could play the role of the number four in that structure, and it would be

01:54:59 --> 01:55:04
isomorphic. And it wouldn't matter at all for any mathematical

01:55:03 --> 01:55:08
purpose to use this alternative mathematical system, you know?

01:55:07 --> 01:55:12
That's to say that we don't care what the number four is

01:55:11 --> 01:55:16
really. That is irrelevant. The only thing that matters is what are the

01:55:15 --> 01:55:19
properties of the number four in a given mathematical system, you

01:55:18 --> 01:55:23
know, and recognizing that there are other isomorphic copies of that

01:55:22 --> 01:55:27
system, and the properties of that other system's number four are

01:55:26 --> 01:55:31
going to be identical to the properties of this system's number

01:55:30 --> 01:55:35
four with regard to any question that's important about the number four. But those

01:55:34 --> 01:55:39
questions won't be about essence. So, in a sense,

01:55:38 --> 01:55:43
structuralism is an anti-essential in mathematics.

01:55:42 --> 01:55:47
- So, is it fair to think of numbers as a kind of pointer to

01:55:46 --> 01:55:49
a deep underlying structure?

01:55:48 --> 01:55:52
- Yeah, I think so, because I guess part of the point of

01:55:51 --> 01:55:56
structuralism is that it doesn't make sense to consider mathematical

01:55:56 --> 01:56:00
objects or individuals in isolation.

01:55:59 --> 01:56:04
What's interesting and important about mathematical objects is how they interact with

01:56:03 --> 01:56:08
each other and how they behave in a system, and so maybe one wants to

01:56:07 --> 01:56:12
think about the structural role that the objects play, you know, in a larger

01:56:11 --> 01:56:16
system, a larger structure. There's a famous question

01:56:15 --> 01:56:20
that Frege had asked actually when he was looking into the nature of

01:56:19 --> 01:56:24
numbers, because in his logicist program, right, he was trying to

01:56:23 --> 01:56:27
reduce all of mathematics to logic. And in that

01:56:26 --> 01:56:31
process, he was referring to the Cantor-Hume

01:56:30 --> 01:56:35
principle that, you know, whenever two sets are

01:56:34 --> 01:56:38
quinumerous, then they have the same number of elements, I mean, if and only

01:56:37 --> 01:56:42
if. And he founded his theory of number on this

01:56:41 --> 01:56:46
principle, but he recognized that there was something that

01:56:45 --> 01:56:50
dissatisfied him about that situation, which is that the

01:56:49 --> 01:56:54
Cantor-Hume principle does not seem to give

01:56:53 --> 01:56:57
you criteria for which things are numbers. It only tells

01:56:56 --> 01:57:01
you a kind of identity criteria for when two numbers are equal to

01:57:00 --> 01:57:05
each other. Well, two numbers are equal just in case the sets of

01:57:04 --> 01:57:08
those sizes are equinumerous, so that's the criteria for number

01:57:07 --> 01:57:11
identity. But it is not a criteria for what is a

01:57:11 --> 01:57:15
number. And so this problem has become known as the Julius

01:57:14 --> 01:57:20
Caesar problem because Frege said we don't seem to have any way of telling

01:57:20 --> 01:57:25
from the Hume principle whether Julius Caesar is a

01:57:24 --> 01:57:29
number or not. So he's asking about the essence of number and

01:57:28 --> 01:57:36
whether... Of course, one has a sense that he picked maybe what he was trying to

01:57:35 --> 01:57:37
present as a ridiculous example,

01:57:36 --> 01:57:41
because maybe you have the idea that well, obviously Julius Caesar is not a

01:57:40 --> 01:57:44
number, and there's a lot of philosophical writing that seems to take that line

01:57:43 --> 01:57:48
also, that obviously the answer is that Julius Caesar is not a

01:57:47 --> 01:57:52
number. But the structuralists disagree with that

01:57:51 --> 01:57:55
position. The structuralist attitude is,

01:57:54 --> 01:58:00
"Look, you give me a number system. If Julius Caesar isn't a number, then

01:58:00 --> 01:58:05
I can just... let's take the number 17 out of that system and plug

01:58:04 --> 01:58:09
in Julius Caesar for that role, and now I've got a new number system, and

01:58:08 --> 01:58:11
now Julius Caesar happens to be the number 17."

01:58:10 --> 01:58:15
And that's totally fine, you know. So the point of

01:58:14 --> 01:58:19
structuralism is is that the question of

01:58:18 --> 01:58:22
whether Julius Caesar is a number or not is irrelevant to

01:58:21 --> 01:58:26
mathematics. It is irrelevant because it is not about

01:58:25 --> 01:58:31
structure, it's about this essence of the mathematical objects.

01:58:30 --> 01:58:36
So that's the structuralist criticism of Frege's point.

01:58:35 --> 01:58:38
- You've kind of made the case that you can

01:58:38 --> 01:58:42
say more concrete things about the existence of objects in mathematics

01:58:42 --> 01:58:47
than you can in our physical reality, about which to us human brains,

01:58:47 --> 01:58:52
things are obvious or not. So what's more real? The

01:58:51 --> 01:59:00
reality we see with our eyes or the reality we can express in mathematical theorems?

01:58:59 --> 01:59:09
- I'm not quite sure. I mean, I live entirely in the Platonic realm, and I don't

01:59:08 --> 01:59:14
really understand the physical universe at all. So I don't have strong views.

01:59:13 --> 01:59:20
- Let's talk about the Platonic realm. Is it- because you live there, is it real? Or-

01:59:19 --> 01:59:24
Oh yeah, totally, yeah. This is the realist position in mathematics is that

01:59:23 --> 01:59:28
abstract objects have a real existence. And okay, what's meant by that

01:59:27 --> 01:59:33
is that there's some sense of existence in which those objects can be regarded as real.

01:59:32 --> 01:59:37
- How should we think about that? How should we try to visualize that? What does it mean to

01:59:36 --> 01:59:41
live amongst abstract objects?

01:59:41 --> 01:59:42
- Right.

01:59:41 --> 01:59:47
- Because life is finite. We're all afraid of death. We f-

01:59:46 --> 01:59:52
fall in love with other physical manifestations of objects.

01:59:52 --> 01:59:57
And you're telling me that maybe reality actually exists elsewhere,

01:59:56 --> 02:00:00
and this is all just a projection-

01:59:59 --> 02:00:00
Well, I mean-

01:59:59 --> 02:00:03
... from the abstract realm.

02:00:02 --> 02:00:08
- Do abstract objects exist in a place and at a time? That's very debatable, I think.

02:00:07 --> 02:00:09
- Right. And what does place and time mean?

02:00:08 --> 02:00:11
- Uh, all time, yeah, so...

02:00:10 --> 02:00:16
- So what's more real, physics or the mathematical Platonic space?

02:00:16 --> 02:00:19
- Well, the mathematical Platonic realm

02:00:19 --> 02:00:24
is... I'm not sure I would say it's more real, but I'm saying we

02:00:23 --> 02:00:28
understand the reality of it in a much deeper and more-

02:00:27 --> 02:00:32
...a more convincing way. I don't think we understand the nature of physical

02:00:31 --> 02:00:34
reality very well at all. And I think

02:00:34 --> 02:00:38
most people aren't even scratching the surface of the

02:00:38 --> 02:00:43
question as I intend to be asking it. So, you know, obviously we

02:00:42 --> 02:00:45
understand physical reality. I mean, I knock on the table-

02:00:44 --> 02:00:49
...and so on, and we know all about what it's like to, you know, have a birthday

02:00:48 --> 02:00:53
party or to drink a martini or whatever. And so we, we, we

02:00:52 --> 02:00:57
have a deep understanding of existing in the physical world.

02:00:56 --> 02:01:01
But maybe understanding is the wrong word. We have an experience of

02:01:00 --> 02:01:02
living in the world-

02:01:01 --> 02:01:02
Yeah, experience.

02:01:01 --> 02:01:06
- ...and riding bicycles and all those things, but I don't think we actually

02:01:05 --> 02:01:10
have an understanding at all. I mean, very, very little

02:01:09 --> 02:01:13
of the nature of physical existence. I think it's a profound

02:01:12 --> 02:01:20
mystery. Whereas I think that we do have something a little better of an

02:01:19 --> 02:01:24
understanding of the nature of mathematical existence and abstract existence.

02:01:23 --> 02:01:27
So that's how I would describe the point.

02:01:26 --> 02:01:34
- Somehow it feels like we're approaching some deep truth from different directions,

02:01:34 --> 02:01:39
and we just haven't traveled as far in the physics

02:01:38 --> 02:01:42
world as we have in the mathematical world.

02:01:41 --> 02:01:46
- Maybe I could hope that someone will give, you know, the

02:01:45 --> 02:01:50
convincing account, but it seems to be a profound mystery to me. I can't even

02:01:49 --> 02:01:53
imagine what it would be like to give an account of physical existence.

02:01:52 --> 02:01:57
- Yeah, I wonder, like a thousand years from now as physics progresses...

02:01:56 --> 02:01:57
- Right

02:01:56 --> 02:02:00
- ...what this same conversation would look like.

02:01:59 --> 02:02:01
- Right. That would be quite interesting.

02:02:00 --> 02:02:05
- Do you think there's breakthroughs a thousand years from now on the mathematics side?

02:02:04 --> 02:02:12
Because we've just discussed, and we'll return to, a lot of turmoil a century ago.

02:02:11 --> 02:02:12
- Right.

02:02:12 --> 02:02:15
- Do you think there's more turmoil to be had?

02:02:14 --> 02:02:19
- It's interesting to me because I have my feet in two worlds

02:02:18 --> 02:02:23
of mathematics and philosophy, and to compare the

02:02:22 --> 02:02:27
differences between these subjects. And one of the, one of the

02:02:26 --> 02:02:31
big... There's many cultural differences, but one of the big cultural differences is

02:02:30 --> 02:02:35
towards the idea of progress in the

02:02:34 --> 02:02:39
subject. Because mathematics has huge progress. We

02:02:38 --> 02:02:42
simply understand the mathematical ideas much, much

02:02:41 --> 02:02:46
better, you're continually improving our understanding and

02:02:45 --> 02:02:50
there's growth in knowledge. We understand the nature of infinity now better

02:02:49 --> 02:02:53
than they did 100 years ago. I mean, definitely better. And they

02:02:52 --> 02:02:57
understood it better 100 years ago than they did, you know, for the previous thousands of years

02:02:56 --> 02:03:01
and so on. So, in almost every part of mathematics, there's

02:03:00 --> 02:03:05
improved understanding of the core issues so much

02:03:04 --> 02:03:09
so that, you know, the questions at hand become

02:03:08 --> 02:03:12
totally different and the field sort of moves on to more

02:03:11 --> 02:03:16
difficult, interesting questions. Whereas in philosophy,

02:03:15 --> 02:03:20
there's... That's a little bit true that there's

02:03:19 --> 02:03:24
progress. But meanwhile, it's also true that there are these

02:03:23 --> 02:03:27
ternal questions that have been with us for thousands of years

02:03:27 --> 02:03:31
and in fact, so much so that you can find a lot of philosophers

02:03:30 --> 02:03:35
arguing the important contribution of philosophy is in

02:03:34 --> 02:03:39
asking the questions rather than answering them because it's hopeless

02:03:38 --> 02:03:43
to answer them. I mean, the nature of these deep philosophical

02:03:42 --> 02:03:47
questions is so difficult. Less of a sense of

02:03:46 --> 02:03:51
progress is what I'm trying to say. I don't see any reason to

02:03:50 --> 02:03:55
think that the progress in mathematics, in the growth in our mathematical

02:03:54 --> 02:03:59
understanding and knowledge won't simply continue. And

02:03:58 --> 02:04:05
so, a thousand years from now, maybe the mathematics that they will be doing at that time

02:04:04 --> 02:04:09
would probably be completely unrecognizable to me. I maybe

02:04:08 --> 02:04:13
wouldn't even begin to understand what they're talking about, even without sort of

02:04:12 --> 02:04:16
witnessing, you know, the intervening

02:04:16 --> 02:04:20
developments. So if you bring someone from ancient

02:04:19 --> 02:04:24
imes to today, they maybe wouldn't even understand what we're talking about

02:04:23 --> 02:04:28
with some of the questions. But I feel that,

02:04:27 --> 02:04:31
you know, if Archimedes came and we were able to

02:04:30 --> 02:04:35
communicate, I think I would be able to tell him, you

02:04:34 --> 02:04:38
know, about some of the things that are going on in

02:04:37 --> 02:04:43
mathematics now and, and maybe, you know...

02:04:43 --> 02:04:49
Or, or anyone from that time, I mean. So I think it is possible to

02:04:49 --> 02:04:54
have this kind of progress even when the subject kind of shifts

02:04:53 --> 02:04:58
away from the earlier concerns as a result of the progress, basically.

02:04:57 --> 02:05:02
- To take a tangent on a tangent since you mentioned

02:05:01 --> 02:05:06
philosophy, maybe potentially more about the questions and maybe

02:05:05 --> 02:05:09
mathematics is about the answers, I have to say you are a

02:05:08 --> 02:05:13
legend on MathOverflow, which is like Stack Overflow

02:05:12 --> 02:05:16
but for math. You're ranked number one all time on there with currently

02:05:15 --> 02:05:24
over 246,000 reputation points. How do you approach answering difficult questions on

02:05:23 --> 02:05:24
there?

02:05:24 --> 02:05:28
- Well, MathOverflow has really been one of the great pleasures of

02:05:28 --> 02:05:33
my, of my life. I've really enjoyed it. I mean... And I've

02:05:32 --> 02:05:40
learned so much from interacting on MathOverflow. I've been on there since

02:05:39 --> 02:05:44
2009, which was shortly after it started. I mean, it wasn't exactly at the start, but a little

02:05:43 --> 02:05:48
bit later. And and

02:05:48 --> 02:05:53
I think it gives you the stats for how many characters I typed and I don't

02:05:53 --> 02:06:01
know how many million it is, but uh, this enormous amount of um,

02:06:01 --> 02:06:06
time that I've spent thinking about those questions and it has really just been

02:06:05 --> 02:06:07
amazing to me. Uh-

02:06:06 --> 02:06:10
How do you find the questions that grab you and how do you go about-

02:06:10 --> 02:06:11
Right

02:06:10 --> 02:06:12
- ... answering them?

02:06:11 --> 02:06:16
- So, I'm interested in any question that I find interesting.

02:06:15 --> 02:06:19
