So... And it's not all questions. Sometimes certain kinds of

02:06:18 --> 02:06:22
questions just don't appeal to me that much.

02:06:21 --> 02:06:24
- So you go outside of set theory as well?

02:06:23 --> 02:06:28
- So, I think when I first joined MathOverflow, I

02:06:27 --> 02:06:32
was, I was basically one of the only, one of the few

02:06:31 --> 02:06:36
people in logic who was answering. I mean, there were other people who know

02:06:35 --> 02:06:39
some logic, particularly from category theory and other parts of

02:06:38 --> 02:06:43
mathematics that aren't in the most traditional parts of

02:06:42 --> 02:06:47
logic, but they were answering some of the logic questions. So I really found myself

02:06:46 --> 02:06:50
able to make a contribution in those very early days by

02:06:49 --> 02:06:54
engaging with the logic-related questions. But

02:06:54 --> 02:06:58
here weren't many logic people asking questions either. But what I found was

02:06:57 --> 02:07:01
that there was an enormous amount of interest

02:07:01 --> 02:07:06
in topics that were logic-adjacent. So a

02:07:05 --> 02:07:10
question would arise, you know, in group theory, but it had a logic aspect or an

02:07:09 --> 02:07:13
alysis or whatever, and there would be some logic

02:07:12 --> 02:07:16
angle on it. And what I found was that I was often able

02:07:16 --> 02:07:21
to figure out an answer by learning enough about

02:07:20 --> 02:07:25
hat other subject matter. This is what was so rewarding for me, because basically I had to learn enough.

02:07:25 --> 02:07:29
My expertise, my main expertise was

02:07:28 --> 02:07:32
logic, but someone would ask a question, you know, that, that

02:07:32 --> 02:07:36
was about, say, the axiom of choice in this other subject

02:07:35 --> 02:07:40
matter or the continuum hypothesis or something like that in an, in the other

02:07:39 --> 02:07:44
subject matter. And I would have to learn enough about that other subject and

02:07:43 --> 02:07:48
the context of the question in order to answer and I was often able to do

02:07:47 --> 02:07:52
that. And so I was quite happy to do that. And, and also

02:07:51 --> 02:07:54
I learned a lot by doing that because

02:07:54 --> 02:07:58
I had to learn about these other problem areas. And so it really

02:07:57 --> 02:08:02
allowed me to grow enormously as a mathematician.

02:08:01 --> 02:08:06
- To give some examples of questions you've answered, what are some reasonable

02:08:05 --> 02:08:10
sounding statements that are independent of ZFC? What are the most

02:08:09 --> 02:08:14
misleading alternate definitions in taught mathematics? Is

02:08:13 --> 02:08:17
the analysis as taught in universities in fact the analysis of definable

02:08:16 --> 02:08:21
numbers? Solutions to the continuum hypothesis?

02:08:20 --> 02:08:25
Most unintuitive application of the axiom of choice? Non-trivial

02:08:24 --> 02:08:30
theorems with trivial proofs? Reductio ad absurdum or the

02:08:29 --> 02:08:34
contrapositive? What is a chess piece

02:08:33 --> 02:08:38
mathematically? We should say you worked quite a bit on infinite chess, which we

02:08:37 --> 02:08:41
should definitely talk about. It's awesome. You've worked on so many fascinating

02:08:40 --> 02:08:45
things. Has philosophy ever clarified mathematics?...

02:08:44 --> 02:08:49
why do we have two theorems when one implies the other?

02:08:48 --> 02:08:53
And, of course, just as an example you've given a really, a

02:08:52 --> 02:08:57
great, almost historical answer on the topic of the

02:08:56 --> 02:09:01
continuum hypothesis. Maybe that's a good place to go. We've touched on it a little

02:09:00 --> 02:09:04
bit, but it would be nice to lay out what is the continuum

02:09:03 --> 02:09:08
hypothesis that Cantor struggled with. And I would love to also speak to the

02:09:07 --> 02:09:12
psychology of his- his own life story, his own struggle with it.

02:09:11 --> 02:09:15
It's the human side of mathematics is also fascinating.

02:09:14 --> 02:09:17
So what is the continuum hypothesis?

02:09:16 --> 02:09:21
- So the continuum hypothesis is the question that arises

02:09:20 --> 02:09:24
o naturally whenever you prove that there's more than one size of

02:09:23 --> 02:09:30
infinity. So Cantor proved that the infinity of the real numbers is strictly larger

02:09:30 --> 02:09:35
than the infinity of the natural numbers. But immediately when you prove that,

02:09:36 --> 02:09:41
one wants to know, well, is there anything in between? I mean, what could be a

02:09:40 --> 02:09:42
more natural question to ask

02:09:42 --> 02:09:47
immediately after that? And so Cantor did ask it, and he spent his

02:09:46 --> 02:09:51
whole life thinking about this question. And so the continuum hypothesis

02:09:50 --> 02:09:55
the assertion that there is no infinity in between the natural numbers

02:09:54 --> 02:09:59
and the real numbers. And, of course, Cantor knew many sets

02:09:58 --> 02:10:03
of real numbers. Everything in between... I mean, everything that's in that

02:10:02 --> 02:10:06
interval would be equinumerous with some set of real

02:10:05 --> 02:10:10
numbers. But we know lots of sets of real numbers. I mean, there's all these various

02:10:09 --> 02:10:14
closed sets, Cantor sets, and so on. There's Vitali sets. We have all kinds of

02:10:13 --> 02:10:18
sets of real numbers. And so you might think, well, if the continuum

02:10:17 --> 02:10:22
hypothesis is false, then we've probably seen the- the set already. We just have

02:10:21 --> 02:10:25
to prove, you know, that it's strictly in between. But it turned

02:10:24 --> 02:10:29
out that for all the sets that anyone ever could define or pick

02:10:28 --> 02:10:33
out or observe, for all the sets of real numbers, it was always the case

02:10:32 --> 02:10:37
ither that they were countable, in which case they're equinumerous

02:10:36 --> 02:10:41
with the natural numbers or else finite. Or they were

02:10:40 --> 02:10:45
fully equinumerous with the whole real line. And so

02:10:44 --> 02:10:49
they were never strictly in between. I mean, you're in this situation and you

02:10:48 --> 02:10:53
have 100, thousands of sets that are

02:10:52 --> 02:10:57
candidates to be in between, but in every single case,

02:10:56 --> 02:10:59
you can prove it's on one side or the other

02:10:59 --> 02:11:03
and not strictly in between. And so in every situation

02:11:03 --> 02:11:07
where you're able to figure out whether it's in between or not, it's

02:11:06 --> 02:11:09
always never strictly in between.

02:11:09 --> 02:11:12
- Now, Cantor was obsessed with this.

02:11:11 --> 02:11:16
- I think he was. Yeah, I'm not a historian, so I don't know the exact history.

02:11:15 --> 02:11:19
- Well, everything I've seen, it seems to be the question that broke him, huh? Um,

02:11:19 --> 02:11:24
I mean, just struggling with different opinions on the hypothesis

02:11:23 --> 02:11:30
within himself and... ...Desperately chasing, trying to prove it.

02:11:29 --> 02:11:33
- So he had a program for proving it, which has been

02:11:33 --> 02:11:38
affirmed in a certain respect. Of course, the continuum hypothesis holds

02:11:37 --> 02:11:41
for open sets. That's easy to see. If you have an open

02:11:40 --> 02:11:45
interval, then this is fully equinumerous with the whole

02:11:44 --> 02:11:47
real line. Any interval is equinumerous with the whole line

02:11:47 --> 02:11:52
because all you would need is a function, you know, like the arctangent function

02:11:51 --> 02:11:56
or something that maps the whole real line into an interval. And

02:11:55 --> 02:12:00
that's a one-to-one function. So we know the open sets

02:11:59 --> 02:12:04
have the property that their non-trivial open sets are all

02:12:03 --> 02:12:07
fully equinumerous with the whole real line. So, never strictly in between.

02:12:07 --> 02:12:12
But remarkably, Cantor proved it also for the closed sets,

02:12:11 --> 02:12:16
and that is using what's called the Cantor-Bendixson theorem. So,

02:12:15 --> 02:12:20
it's quite a remarkable result. It's definitely not obvious.

02:12:20 --> 02:12:25
And in this theorem actually was the origin of the ordinals. Cantor

02:12:24 --> 02:12:32
had to invent the ordinals in order to make sense of his Cantor-Bendixson process.

02:12:31 --> 02:12:35
- Can you define the open and the closed set in this context?

02:12:34 --> 02:12:39
- Oh, yeah. Sure. So a set of reals is open if every

02:12:38 --> 02:12:43
point that it contains is surrounded by a little interval of

02:12:42 --> 02:12:47
points, the whole tiny little interval. But that

02:12:46 --> 02:12:50
iny little interval is already just by itself equinumerous with the whole line.

02:12:49 --> 02:12:54
So that's why that question is sort of easy for open sets. A closed

02:12:53 --> 02:12:57
set is a complement of an open set, and there's a lot of

02:12:57 --> 02:13:01
closed sets that are really complicated of varying

02:13:00 --> 02:13:05
sizes. So of course, any closed interval is a closed set, but it's not only those.

02:13:04 --> 02:13:09
There's also things like the Cantor set, which you get by omitting middle

02:13:08 --> 02:13:13
thirds. Maybe some people have seen this construction. Or you can

02:13:12 --> 02:13:16
imagine sort of randomly taking a lot of little tiny

02:13:16 --> 02:13:20
open intervals, you know, all over the line and so on. So that altogether would be an open

02:13:20 --> 02:13:25
set, and the complement of it would be a closed set. So you can imagine just

02:13:24 --> 02:13:28
kind of tossing down these open intervals, and what's left over

02:13:27 --> 02:13:32
is the closed set. Those sets can be quite complicated, and they can have

02:13:31 --> 02:13:36
isolated points, for example, if the two open intervals were just

02:13:35 --> 02:13:40
kissing and leaving only the one point between them. But also you could

02:13:39 --> 02:13:44
have sequences that are converging to a point, that would

02:13:43 --> 02:13:48
also be a closed set, or convergent sequences of convergent

02:13:47 --> 02:13:51
sequences and so on. That would be a closed set also.

02:13:50 --> 02:13:55
- The Cantor set is constructed by iteratively removing open intervals, middle thirds,

02:13:54 --> 02:13:56
like you mentioned, from the interval,

02:13:56 --> 02:14:01
and trying to see, can we do a thing that that goes in between?

02:14:00 --> 02:14:05
- Right. So the question would be, can you produce a set

02:14:05 --> 02:14:10
hat has an intermediate size? an intermediate cardinality, right?

02:14:09 --> 02:14:14
And Cantor proved, with the closed set, "No, it's impossible." Every closed

02:14:13 --> 02:14:19
set is either countable or equinumerous with the whole real line.

02:14:18 --> 02:14:24
And the Cantor program for solving the Continuum Hypothesis was,

02:14:25 --> 02:14:30
a sort of working up. So you did it for open sets and for closed sets,

02:14:29 --> 02:14:33
and you sort of work up. Maybe he wants to go into what are called the Borel sets,

02:14:32 --> 02:14:37
which are sort of combinations of open and closed sets. And there's a vast

02:14:36 --> 02:14:41
hierarchy of Borel complexity. And it

02:14:40 --> 02:14:45
urns out that the Continuum Hypothesis has been proved also

02:14:44 --> 02:14:49
for the Borel sets in this hierarchy. But then one wants to go beyond.

02:14:48 --> 02:14:53
What about more complicated sets? So there's this hierarchy of complexity

02:14:52 --> 02:14:57
for sets of real numbers. And Cantor's idea was to sort of work your way up the

02:14:56 --> 02:15:01
hierarchy by proving that the Continuum Hypothesis

02:15:00 --> 02:15:05
was more and more true for those more and more complicated sets, based on our

02:15:04 --> 02:15:08
understanding of the earlier cases. And that has

02:15:07 --> 02:15:16
been carried out to a remarkable degree. It turns out that one begins to need

02:15:15 --> 02:15:20
large cardinal assumptions, though, in order to get to

02:15:19 --> 02:15:23
the higher realms, even at the level of projective hierarchy,

02:15:23 --> 02:15:28
which are sets that you can define by using quantifiers over the real numbers

02:15:27 --> 02:15:32
themselves. So you get this hierarchy on top of the Borel hierarchy,

02:15:32 --> 02:15:36
the hierarchy of projectively definable sets. And it

02:15:35 --> 02:15:42
urns out that if you have enough large cardinals, then the projective sets

02:15:42 --> 02:15:46
also are always either countable or equinumerous with the whole real

02:15:45 --> 02:15:50
ine. And then one can try to go beyond this and so

02:15:49 --> 02:15:54
n. So I view all of those results which came, you know, in the past 50

02:15:53 --> 02:16:02
years, the later ones, as fulfilling this Cantor idea that goes back,

02:16:01 --> 02:16:06
you know, 120 years to his idea that we would prove the Continuum

02:16:05 --> 02:16:10
Hypothesis by establishing more and more instances for greater and

02:16:09 --> 02:16:13
greater complexity of sets. But of course,

02:16:13 --> 02:16:18
even with what we know now, it hasn't fully succeeded and it

02:16:17 --> 02:16:22
can't because the hierarchy of complexity doesn't include

02:16:21 --> 02:16:26
all sets of real numbers. Some of them are, sort of, transcending this hierarchy

02:16:25 --> 02:16:29
completely, in a way. And

02:16:29 --> 02:16:34
so the program can't ever fully be successful, especially in light

02:16:33 --> 02:16:35
of the independence result.

02:16:34 --> 02:16:39
- Yeah. Well, spoiler alert, can you go to the independence result?

02:16:38 --> 02:16:40
- Sure.

02:16:39 --> 02:16:44
- So what does that mean? So the Continuum

02:16:43 --> 02:16:47
Hypothesis was shown to be independent from the ZFC axioms of mathematics?

02:16:46 --> 02:16:51
- Right. So the ZFC axioms were the axioms that were

02:16:50 --> 02:16:55
put forth first by Zermelo in 1908 in regard to his

02:16:54 --> 02:16:59
proof of the well-order theorem using the axiom of choice. That

02:16:58 --> 02:17:03
wasn't fully ZFC. At that time, it was just Zermelo theory because he

02:17:02 --> 02:17:07
sort of... There was a kind of missing axiom, the replacement axiom, and the

02:17:06 --> 02:17:10
foundation axiom were added later, and that's what makes the Zermelo-Fraenkel

02:17:10 --> 02:17:13
axiomatization, which became, sort of, standard.

02:17:13 --> 02:17:18
Actually, there's another aspect, which is Zermelo's original theory allowed

02:17:17 --> 02:17:21
for the existence of ur-elements, or these

02:17:20 --> 02:17:25
atoms, mathematical objects that are not sets

02:17:25 --> 02:17:29
but out of which we build the set theoretic universe,

02:17:28 --> 02:17:36
whereas set theorists today generally don't use ur-elements at all. I,

02:17:36 --> 02:17:41
I argue that it's really the philosophy of structuralism that

02:17:40 --> 02:17:45
leads them to omit the ur-elements because it turns out that

02:17:44 --> 02:17:53
if you adopt ZFC axioms with ur-elements, ZFCU it's called, or ZFA, then any

02:17:52 --> 02:17:57
structure that exists, any mathematical structure that exists in that set theoretic universe

02:17:56 --> 02:18:02
with the atoms is isomorphic to a structure that doesn't use the atoms at all.

02:18:02 --> 02:18:07
And you don't need the atoms if you're a structuralist because you only care about

02:18:06 --> 02:18:11
he structures up to isomorphism anyways, and the theory is simply more

02:18:10 --> 02:18:14
legant and clear without the atoms. They're just not

02:18:13 --> 02:18:18
needed. And so that's why today when we talk about set theory, generally we

02:18:17 --> 02:18:21
talk about the atom-free version, and ZFC has no

02:18:20 --> 02:18:25
ur-elements. Okay. So we formulate the ZFC

02:18:25 --> 02:18:29
axioms of set theory. These are expressing the main

02:18:28 --> 02:18:36
principle ideas that we have about the nature of sets and set existence. And

02:18:38 --> 02:18:41
Canter had asked about the continuum hypothesis

02:18:41 --> 02:18:51
in the late 19th century, and it remained open, totally open until 1938.

02:18:50 --> 02:18:54
- We should mention, I apologize, that it was the number one

02:18:54 --> 02:18:59
problem in the Hilbert's 23 set of problems formulated at the

02:18:58 --> 02:19:00
beginning of the century.

02:18:59 --> 02:19:00
- That's right.

02:18:59 --> 02:19:03
- Maybe you can comment on why did he put that as number one.

02:19:02 --> 02:19:07
- So... Right. So Hilbert had introduced at his famous address at the

02:19:06 --> 02:19:11
turn of the century this list of problems that he thought

02:19:11 --> 02:19:15
could guide or were important to consider in the

02:19:15 --> 02:19:20
coming century of mathematics. I mean, that's how people talk about it now, although I'm not sure at

02:19:19 --> 02:19:23
all... Of course, I can't really speak for Hilbert at all, but if

02:19:23 --> 02:19:26
you were a very prominent mathematician,

02:19:27 --> 02:19:32
I find it a little hard to believe that Hilbert would have conceived of his list

02:19:31 --> 02:19:36
in the same way that we now take his lists. I mean,

02:19:35 --> 02:19:40
having observed the century unfold, we know that that list of

02:19:39 --> 02:19:44
23 problems did in fact guide whole research programs, and

02:19:43 --> 02:19:48
it was extremely important and influential. But at the time, Hilbert

02:19:47 --> 02:19:52
would have no reason to think that that would be true, and he was

02:19:51 --> 02:19:56
just giving a lecture and had a list of problems that

02:19:55 --> 02:20:00
he thought were very important. And so I tend to I would find

02:19:59 --> 02:20:04
it more reasonable to think that he was just making a list of problems that he thought were

02:20:03 --> 02:20:08
xtremely interesting and important and fundamental in a way without

02:20:07 --> 02:20:12
he kind of heavy burden of

02:20:11 --> 02:20:16
guiding this 20th century research. Although it turns out

02:20:15 --> 02:20:17
hat in fact that's exactly what they did.

02:20:18 --> 02:20:22
And we already discussed how Hilbert's views on the nature of set

02:20:22 --> 02:20:25
heory and the fundamental character, that quote

02:20:25 --> 02:20:30
where he said, "No one will cast us from the paradise that

02:20:29 --> 02:20:34
Canter has created for us." So, so I think

02:20:33 --> 02:20:38
Hilbert was convinced by Canter on the importance and the

02:20:37 --> 02:20:42
fundamental nature of the continuum hypothesis for the foundations of mathematics,

02:20:41 --> 02:20:45
which was a critically important development for the unity of

02:20:44 --> 02:20:49
mathematics. I mean, before set theory emerged as a foundation of mathematics,

02:20:48 --> 02:20:53
you know, there are different subjects in mathematics.

02:20:52 --> 02:20:57
There's algebra and there's analysis, real analysis, and topology and

02:20:56 --> 02:20:59
geometry, and so on. There are all these disparate subjects

02:21:00 --> 02:21:04
with their own axioms, separate axioms, right?

02:21:03 --> 02:21:08
And sometimes it happens, like when you're proving, say, the fundamental

02:21:07 --> 02:21:12
theorem of algebra, you know, that the complex numbers are an algebraically closed

02:21:11 --> 02:21:16
field that you can solve any polynomial equation in.

02:21:16 --> 02:21:20
But the proof methods for that theorem come from

02:21:19 --> 02:21:24
other parts of mathematics. You know, those topological proofs and so

02:21:23 --> 02:21:28
n. And so how does that

02:21:27 --> 02:21:32
work? I mean, if you have totally different axiom systems, but you're using results from

02:21:31 --> 02:21:36
one subject in another subject, it's somehow incoherent

02:21:35 --> 02:21:38
unless there's one underlying

02:21:38 --> 02:21:43
subject. So the unity of mathematics was provided by the

02:21:42 --> 02:21:47
xistence of a mathematical foundation like set theory. And at the time, it was set theory.

02:21:47 --> 02:21:52
And so it's critically important to be able to have a

02:21:51 --> 02:21:56
single theory in which one views all of mathematics as taking

02:21:55 --> 02:22:00
place to resolve that kind of transfer and borrowing

02:21:59 --> 02:22:03
phenomenon that was definitely happening. So that must

02:22:03 --> 02:22:07
have been part of Hilbert's thinking about why it's so important to have a uniform

02:22:07 --> 02:22:11
foundation, and set theory was playing that role at the time. Now, of course, we have

02:22:11 --> 02:22:15
other possible foundations coming from category theory or type

02:22:14 --> 02:22:18
theory, and there's univalent

02:22:18 --> 02:22:22
foundations now. So there are sort of competing foundations now. There's no

02:22:21 --> 02:22:26
need to just use one foundation, one set theoretic foundation. Although

02:22:25 --> 02:22:30
set theory continues to, in my view, have an extremely successful

02:22:29 --> 02:22:34
meta-mathematical analysis as a foundation, I think is much more

02:22:33 --> 02:22:38
successful than set theory for any of those other foundations, but

02:22:37 --> 02:22:42
it's much less amenable though to things like computer proof and so on, which is part of

02:22:41 --> 02:22:46
the motivation to find these alternative foundations. So, yeah, okay, so

02:22:45 --> 02:22:50
just to talk about Hilbert though, I think he was motivated

02:22:49 --> 02:22:53
by the need for a unifying foundation of

02:22:52 --> 02:22:57
mathematics, and set theory was playing that role, and the continuum hypothesis

02:22:56 --> 02:23:01
such a core, fundamental question to ask, so it

02:23:00 --> 02:23:05
seems quite natural that he would put it on the list. There were other

02:23:04 --> 02:23:09
logic-related questions though, like Hilbert's tenth problem is also related to logic.

02:23:08 --> 02:23:13
This is the question about Diophantine equations, and

02:23:12 --> 02:23:16
he asked to provide an algorithm to decide whether a given

02:23:16 --> 02:23:20
Diophantine equation has a solution in the integers. So a Diophantine

02:23:19 --> 02:23:24
quation is just, I mean, it's maybe a fancy way of talking about

02:23:23 --> 02:23:28
something that's easy to understand, a polynomial equation, except it's

02:23:27 --> 02:23:32
not just one variable, many variables.

02:23:31 --> 02:23:36
So you have polynomials in several variables over the

02:23:35 --> 02:23:39
integers, and you want to know, can you solve it? So the problem is,

02:23:38 --> 02:23:43
as stated by Hilbert, to provide an

02:23:42 --> 02:23:46
algorithm for answering the question whether a given

02:23:45 --> 02:23:49
polynomial equation has a solution in the integers.

02:23:49 --> 02:23:54
So he's sort of presuming that there is an algorithm, but he wants to know what it

02:23:53 --> 02:24:02
is. What is the algorithm? But the problem was solved by proving that there is no

02:24:01 --> 02:24:05
algorithm. It's an undecidable problem, like the halting problem.

02:24:05 --> 02:24:10
There is no computable procedure that will correctly decide whether a given

02:24:09 --> 02:24:14
polynomial equation has a solution in the integers. So that's quite

02:24:13 --> 02:24:17
a remarkable development, I think. There were also a few other

02:24:17 --> 02:24:21
logic-related questions on the list.

02:24:20 --> 02:24:25
- And so eventually, continuum hypothesis was shown to be independent from ZFC

02:24:24 --> 02:24:29
axioms, as we've mentioned. So, how does that make you feel?

02:24:29 --> 02:24:31
What is independence, and what does that mean?

02:24:30 --> 02:24:33
- But once you tell the story, the historical story...

02:24:32 --> 02:24:33
- Yes

02:24:32 --> 02:24:35
- ...is really quite dramatic.

02:24:34 --> 02:24:35
- Yeah, that's great

02:24:34 --> 02:24:40
- I think, because Cantor poses the question, you know, late 19th century.

02:24:39 --> 02:24:44
And then it's totally open. Hilbert asks about it, you know, at the turn of the

02:24:43 --> 02:24:48
20th century. Nobody has any clue. There's no answer

02:24:47 --> 02:24:54
coming until 1938. This is four decades later, right? So a long

02:24:53 --> 02:25:02
time, and Gödel, Kurt Gödel, proved half of it. What he proved is that

02:25:03 --> 02:25:10
if the axioms of set theory are consistent, then there is a set theoretic world

02:25:10 --> 02:25:16
where both the axiom of choice and the continuum hypothesis are true.

02:25:17 --> 02:25:23
So what he's doing is showing this is called the constructible universe, Gödel's L.

02:25:24 --> 02:25:29
So he solved this. This is the same result

02:25:28 --> 02:25:32
where he answers the safety question of the axiom of choice, but

02:25:31 --> 02:25:37
also for the continuum hypothesis. They're true in the same set theoretic universe

02:25:37 --> 02:25:42
we get. So if ZF, without the axiom of choice, is consistent, then so is

02:25:41 --> 02:25:48
ZFC plus the continuum hypothesis is the result. 1938. It's really such a beautiful

02:25:47 --> 02:25:52
argument. It's just incredible, I think, because

02:25:51 --> 02:25:55
he's building an alternative mathematical

02:25:54 --> 02:25:59
reality. That's the structure of the proof, is that, okay,

02:25:58 --> 02:26:03
if there's any mathematical reality, if there's any set theoretic

02:26:02 --> 02:26:06
world, then we're going to build another one, a separate one, a

02:26:05 --> 02:26:10
different one, maybe different. Maybe it's the same as the original one, it could be.

02:26:09 --> 02:26:13
If we started already in the one that he built, then it would be the same. But

02:26:13 --> 02:26:17
here's no reason to assume it was the same. So he has this kind of

02:26:17 --> 02:26:21
model construction method to build this

02:26:20 --> 02:26:25
alternative set theoretic reality, the constructible universe. And then

02:26:24 --> 02:26:29
he proves that the axiom of choice is true there, and also the continuum hypothesis is

02:26:28 --> 02:26:33
true there, and it's just amazing. Really beautiful argument.

02:26:32 --> 02:26:37
Okay, so then for the other part of the independence, that's only half of it,

02:26:36 --> 02:26:41
because Gödel shows basically

02:26:40 --> 02:26:45
that you can't refute the continuum hypothesis, but that's not

02:26:44 --> 02:26:49
he same thing as proving that it's true. He showed

02:26:48 --> 02:26:53
that if set theory is consistent...

02:26:52 --> 02:26:56
without the continuum hypothesis, then it's consistent with the continuum hypothesis.

02:26:55 --> 02:27:00
So that's not the same thing as proving that it's true. Yeah.

02:26:59 --> 02:27:07
And then it didn't come until 1963, when Paul Cohen invented the method of forcing.

02:27:06 --> 02:27:11
And proved that if there's a model of set

02:27:10 --> 02:27:14
heory, then there's a model of set theory in which the continuum

02:27:13 --> 02:27:19
hypothesis is false. So Cohen also is

02:27:19 --> 02:27:24
giving us this extremely powerful tool for building

02:27:23 --> 02:27:27
alternative mathematical realities, is how I think about

02:27:26 --> 02:27:31
it. He's explained to us how to take any set theoretic

02:27:30 --> 02:27:35
world and build another different one in which the continuum hypothesis is

02:27:34 --> 02:27:38
false. The forcing extension.

02:27:37 --> 02:27:41
- It's just such a fascinating technique,

02:27:40 --> 02:27:45
tool of forcing. Maybe I'm anthropomorphizing it,

02:27:44 --> 02:27:53
but it seems like a way to escape one mathematical universe into another, or to

02:27:52 --> 02:27:56
expand it or to alter it. So you travel between mathematical

02:27:55 --> 02:27:58
universes. Can you explain the technique of forcing?

02:27:57 --> 02:28:02
- Yeah, exactly. It's all those things. It's so wonderful. I mean, that's exactly how

02:28:01 --> 02:28:04
I think about it. I mean...

02:28:03 --> 02:28:08
- And we should mention, maybe this is a good place to even give a bigger

02:28:07 --> 02:28:12
picture. One of your more controversial ideas in mathematics as

02:28:11 --> 02:28:15
laid out in the paper, The Set-Theoretic Multiverse, you

02:28:15 --> 02:28:19
describe that there may not be one true

02:28:18 --> 02:28:22
mathematics, but rather multiple mathematical universes, and

02:28:21 --> 02:28:26
forcing is one of the techniques that gets you from one to the other, so...

02:28:25 --> 02:28:26
- The-

02:28:25 --> 02:28:28
Can you explain the whole shebang? The whole...

02:28:27 --> 02:28:35
- Yeah, sure. Let's get into it. So the lesson of Cohen's result and Gödel's

02:28:34 --> 02:28:39
result and so on, these producing these alternative set theoretic

02:28:38 --> 02:28:43
universes. We've observed that the continuum hypothesis is

02:28:42 --> 02:28:47
independent and the axiom of choice is independent of the other axioms,

02:28:46 --> 02:28:51
but it's not just those two. We have thousands of independence results.

02:28:50 --> 02:28:54
Practically every non-trivial statement of infinite

02:28:53 --> 02:28:57
combinatorics is independent of ZFC. I mean, this is the

02:28:56 --> 02:29:01
fact. It's not universally true. There are

02:29:00 --> 02:29:04
some extremely difficult prominent results where

02:29:03 --> 02:29:08
people proved things in ZFC, but for the most

02:29:07 --> 02:29:12
part, if you ask a non-trivial question about infinite

02:29:11 --> 02:29:15
cardinalities, then it's very likely to be independent of

02:29:14 --> 02:29:19
ZFC. And we have these thousands of arguments, these

02:29:18 --> 02:29:23
forcing arguments that are used to establish that. And

02:29:22 --> 02:29:27
so how should we take that? I mean, on the one hand, if you have

02:29:26 --> 02:29:31
a theory and it doesn't answer any of the questions that you're

02:29:30 --> 02:29:35
interested in... Okay, so what does that mean? If you're following what I

02:29:34 --> 02:29:38
call the universe view or the monist view,

02:29:38 --> 02:29:46
you might naturally say, "Well, look, ZFC is a weak theory, and there's the true

02:29:46 --> 02:29:51
set theoretic reality out there, and we need a better theory

02:29:50 --> 02:29:54
'cause the current theory isn't answering the questions. Everything's

02:29:53 --> 02:29:58
independent." And so that seems like a quite reasonable thing

02:29:57 --> 02:30:02
to take. If you think that there is... that every set theoretic question has a

02:30:01 --> 02:30:06
definite answer and there's a unique set theoretic truth or a unique

02:30:05 --> 02:30:09
fact of the matter, right, this is the universe view.

02:30:09 --> 02:30:11
- And by the way, to reiterate,

02:30:11 --> 02:30:15
independent means it cannot be proved or disproved within this

02:30:14 --> 02:30:17
axiomatic system within this theory.

02:30:16 --> 02:30:21
- Right. Exactly. So to be independent means you can't prove it and also you can't prove that

02:30:20 --> 02:30:23
it's false. You can't refute it.

02:30:22 --> 02:30:27
- And you're saying that's why the statement is so traumatic or sad, that

02:30:26 --> 02:30:31
most of the interesting stuff, as you said, has been shown to be independent.

02:30:31 --> 02:30:32
of ZFC.

02:30:31 --> 02:30:36
- But, but that's an interesting way to put it, I think, because it reminds me of this,

02:30:35 --> 02:30:40
uh... when I was a graduate student in Berkeley, there was

02:30:39 --> 02:30:44
another graduate student who was working with a non-logic

02:30:43 --> 02:30:48
professor in C-star algebras or something

02:30:47 --> 02:30:52
like this. So it's a part of analysis or functional analysis,

02:30:51 --> 02:30:56
and they were looking at a question, and it turned out to be

02:30:55 --> 02:31:00
independent of ZFC, right? And the attitude of this

02:30:59 --> 02:31:03
other professor was that, "Oh, I guess I asked the wrong

02:31:02 --> 02:31:07
question." But my attitude and the

02:31:06 --> 02:31:11
attitude of all the set theorists was when you ask a question that

02:31:10 --> 02:31:15
urns out to be independent, then you asked exactly the right question because this is the

02:31:14 --> 02:31:19
one... You know, it's carving nature at its joints.

02:31:18 --> 02:31:23
You're adjudicating the nature of set theoretic reality

02:31:22 --> 02:31:26
by finding these two realms. You find one of these

02:31:25 --> 02:31:29
dichotomies. You know, there's the worlds where it's true and the worlds where it's false.

02:31:28 --> 02:31:33
And so when you ask that question, that's to be celebrated. It means you asked exactly

02:31:32 --> 02:31:37
the right, interesting, fascinating question. So it's not a kind of bleak

02:31:36 --> 02:31:41
thing that you can't prove it and you can't refute it, and that's such a disaster.

02:31:40 --> 02:31:49
Rather, it means that you found this... this... this cleavage in reality, in

02:31:48 --> 02:31:53
mathematical reality, and it's good to know about those when they happen, you know?

02:31:52 --> 02:31:56
- Carving nature at its joints. So what can you do about the

02:31:56 --> 02:32:00
things that are shown to be independent from ZFC?

02:32:00 --> 02:32:01
- Right. So...

02:32:00 --> 02:32:02
- What are the techniques?

02:32:01 --> 02:32:04
- So one thing is that because of the incompleteness theorem,

02:32:04 --> 02:32:09
we know that there's going to be... For any

02:32:08 --> 02:32:13
theory that we can write down, there's going to be things that we can't

02:32:12 --> 02:32:17
prove, true things we can't prove in it. So there's those things are gonna be

02:32:16 --> 02:32:24
independent. And so we're already aware of the fact that there will always be these

02:32:23 --> 02:32:28
independent phenomenon for any theory that we write. And

02:32:27 --> 02:32:32
furthermore, some of those theories we won't even be able to prove

02:32:31 --> 02:32:35
that they're consistent, you know, like the consistency of their own theory. So that's called the

02:32:34 --> 02:32:37
consistency-strength hierarchy.

02:32:37 --> 02:32:42
So it's a direct consequence of Gödel's second incompleteness theorem that

02:32:42 --> 02:32:47
for any theory we can write down, then towering over it is this

02:32:46 --> 02:32:50
incredibly tall tower of consistency strength,

02:32:49 --> 02:32:54
where the strength in theories aren't just adding another

02:32:53 --> 02:32:58
axiom, but they're adding another axiom even whose consistency was not

02:32:57 --> 02:33:01
provable in the previous layers of the hierarchy.

02:33:01 --> 02:33:05
So, and so how lucky we are to find the large

02:33:04 --> 02:33:13
cardinal axioms that instantiate exactly this feature of increasing

02:33:12 --> 02:33:20
consistency strength, this unending and extremely tall hierarchy of consistency

02:33:19 --> 02:33:24
strength of axioms. And it exactly fulfills the prediction

02:33:23 --> 02:33:28
that Gödel's theorem makes about that kind of

02:33:27 --> 02:33:32
thing. Except, it's the axioms in the large cardinal

02:33:31 --> 02:33:35
hierarchy aren't, you know, metalogical

02:33:35 --> 02:33:39
self-referential statements of the form that sometimes arise in the Gödel

02:33:38 --> 02:33:42
analysis, but rather they're professing

02:33:42 --> 02:33:47
existence of big infinities, these large cardinal axioms. And so

02:33:47 --> 02:33:52
it's such a welcome development, and yet it's

02:33:51 --> 02:33:55
also known that the continuum hypothesis is

02:33:54 --> 02:34:00
independent of all of the known large cardinal axioms. So

02:34:00 --> 02:34:05
none of the large cardinal axioms we can prove, none

02:34:04 --> 02:34:09
of them can settle the continuum hypothesis. So the independence

02:34:08 --> 02:34:12
phenomenon is still there for things like the continuum

02:34:11 --> 02:34:18
hypothesis and the cardinal combinatorics that I mentioned.

02:34:17 --> 02:34:20
- So you're building this incredible hierarchy

02:34:21 --> 02:34:25
of axiomatic systems that are more powerful than the ZFC.

02:34:24 --> 02:34:29
- More powerful than ZFC and then more powerful than that, more powerful than that, and so on.

02:34:28 --> 02:34:33
It keeps going forever, and it will never be finished.

02:34:32 --> 02:34:37
- And still, to this day, the continuum hypothesis does not...

02:34:36 --> 02:34:40
- It's not settled by any of the large cardinal axioms.

02:34:39 --> 02:34:44
- Wow. Wow.

02:34:44 --> 02:34:48
What does that mean? How does that make you feel? Will it ever be settled?

02:34:47 --> 02:34:52
- Yeah, well, it's part of my multiverse view, I guess. So which

02:34:51 --> 02:34:56
we started by, I was describing the universe view, which

02:34:55 --> 02:35:00
is the view that, look, there are facts of the matter about all

02:34:59 --> 02:35:03
of these questions and that it will turn out, if you're a universe view

02:35:02 --> 02:35:07
person, which I'm not, but if you are, then you will hold

02:35:06 --> 02:35:11
that there is a right answer to the continuum hypothesis question, and there's a

02:35:10 --> 02:35:15
right answer to the large cardinal questions

02:35:14 --> 02:35:19
and so on. And that what we should be aiming to do is figure out this

02:35:18 --> 02:35:25
one true set theory, okay? In contrast I take the developments

02:35:24 --> 02:35:32
of set theory over the past half century or more as evidence that there isn't such a

02:35:31 --> 02:35:35
unique set theoretic reality. Rather,

02:35:35 --> 02:35:40
what we've been doing for decades now is producing more and

02:35:39 --> 02:35:44
more alternative set theoretic universes in which the

02:35:43 --> 02:35:48
fundamental truths are differing from one to the other. And that

02:35:47 --> 02:35:53
is the answer to the continuum hypothesis question. The fact that

02:35:53 --> 02:35:58
given any model of set theory, there's a forcing extension where the continuum

02:35:57 --> 02:36:02
hypothesis is true, and another one where it's false. You can sort of

02:36:01 --> 02:36:06
turn it on and off like a light switch. And that's the fundamental nature of the

02:36:05 --> 02:36:10
continuum hypothesis is that you can have it or you can have the negation as you like

02:36:10 --> 02:36:15
within a very closely related set theoretic world.

02:36:14 --> 02:36:19
Wherever you happen to be living, there's a closely related one where

02:36:18 --> 02:36:22
CH is true, where the continuum hypothesis is true, and one

02:36:22 --> 02:36:26
where it's false. And that itself is a kind of answer.

02:36:25 --> 02:36:30
It's not a singularist answer, a universe view

02:36:29 --> 02:36:34
answer. It's a pluralist answer. And this

02:36:33 --> 02:36:38
led me to my views on the multiverse view of set theory and

02:36:37 --> 02:36:42
pluralist truth, namely the fundamental nature of set theoretic truth

02:36:41 --> 02:36:45
as this plural character in that

02:36:45 --> 02:36:50
here isn't a singular meaning to the fundamental terms, but

02:36:49 --> 02:36:54
rather, there's this choice of alternative set-theoretic universes

02:36:53 --> 02:36:55
that have different truths.

02:36:55 --> 02:36:59
- So, what does the multiverse view of mathematics enable you to

02:36:58 --> 02:37:03
do? What does it empower you to do and what are the limitations?

02:37:02 --> 02:37:07
What are the things it breaks about mathematics as a field, as a

02:37:06 --> 02:37:11
space of knowledge, and what does it enable?

02:37:10 --> 02:37:15
- First of all, I guess one should say that these different philosophical

02:37:14 --> 02:37:18
positions that you might take in the philosophy of set theory, like the multiverse

02:37:18 --> 02:37:22
view or the universe view, we don't ever disagree about

02:37:21 --> 02:37:26
he mathematics. We're all agreeing on what the theorems are.

02:37:25 --> 02:37:30
It's a question of philosophical perspective on the underlying meaning

02:37:29 --> 02:37:36
or the context, or really what is a philosophy of mathematics for, right?

02:37:36 --> 02:37:41
And I mean, if you look back in history, for example, like

02:37:40 --> 02:37:44
to the time of calculus with Newton and Leibniz, right?

02:37:44 --> 02:37:52
They famously developed the ideas of calculus using their concepts of infinitesimals,

02:37:51 --> 02:37:56
and those foundations were roundly mocked by

02:37:55 --> 02:38:00
Bishop Berkeley and so on who talked about, you know, what are these

02:37:59 --> 02:38:03
same evanescent increments, and shall we not call them the

02:38:02 --> 02:38:07
ghosts of departed quantities? But the

02:38:06 --> 02:38:11
foundations really were kind of completely suspect, I think,

02:38:10 --> 02:38:15
at the time. And that foundation

02:38:14 --> 02:38:18
of infinitesimal calculus really only became rigorous in the

02:38:17 --> 02:38:22
1950s or so with the development of non-standard analysis and

02:38:21 --> 02:38:25
Robinson's work. Okay, so the point I'm trying to make is that, do you

02:38:24 --> 02:38:33
need a robust, rigorous foundation of mathematics to make enduring insights in

02:38:32 --> 02:38:40
mathematics? And the answer, regrettably, is apparently not because

02:38:39 --> 02:38:47
in calculus, even with that lousy, creaky foundation of infinitesimals not even

02:38:47 --> 02:38:52
well understood that Newton and Leibniz had, they proved all the

02:38:51 --> 02:38:57
fundamental theorems of calculus and, you know, they had all the main insights

02:38:57 --> 02:39:01
in those early days with that extremely bad

02:39:00 --> 02:39:08
foundation. And so that shows you something about the relevance of the kind of

02:39:07 --> 02:39:12
oundational views on mathematics and how important they are for

02:39:11 --> 02:39:16
mathematical developments and progress and insight. I mean, because I

02:39:15 --> 02:39:19
view those early mathematical developments in calculus as

02:39:18 --> 02:39:23
genuinely mathematical and extremely important

02:39:23 --> 02:39:27
and insightful, even though the foundations weren't any good,

02:39:27 --> 02:39:31
from, you know, by contemporary perspectives. Okay.

02:39:30 --> 02:39:35
So, rather... So when it comes to the philosophy of set theory and

02:39:34 --> 02:39:41
the dispute between the universe view and the pluralism, my view is that the choice

02:39:41 --> 02:39:45
of the philosophical perspective doesn't actually have to do with the

02:39:44 --> 02:39:49
mathematical developments directly at all. Rather, it

02:39:48 --> 02:39:53
ells us, "Where should set theory go? What

02:39:52 --> 02:39:57
kind of set theory should we be looking at? What kind of

02:39:56 --> 02:40:01
questions should we be asking?" So if you have a

02:40:00 --> 02:40:05
universe mentality, the universe view, then you're

02:40:04 --> 02:40:09
gonna be pushed to try to find and articulate the

02:40:08 --> 02:40:12
nature of the one true set-theoretic universe.

02:40:12 --> 02:40:17
And I think that remark is really well borne out by the developments with

02:40:16 --> 02:40:21
Hugh Woodin, who's one of the most prominent mathematicians

02:40:20 --> 02:40:25
and philosophers with the universe view and his theory of ultimate

02:40:24 --> 02:40:27
L and so on. And he's really striving.

02:40:26 --> 02:40:28
- Who was also your advisor.

02:40:28 --> 02:40:31
- He was also my supervisor. My graduate supervisor.

02:40:30 --> 02:40:33
- Which is a, a personal story as well.

02:40:32 --> 02:40:37
- This, this fundamental dispute, yeah, on this question.

02:40:37 --> 02:40:42
he is uh, has a very strong and successful

02:40:41 --> 02:40:45
research program, sort of trying to give legs

02:40:45 --> 02:40:49
to finding the nature of the one true set theoretic

02:40:48 --> 02:40:53
universe. And it's driving the questions that he's asking and the

02:40:52 --> 02:40:56
mathematical programs that he's pursuing. Whereas if you have a

02:40:56 --> 02:41:00
pluralist view, as I do, then you're gonna be led and

02:40:59 --> 02:41:04
attracted to questions that have to do with the interaction of

02:41:03 --> 02:41:07
different set theoretic universes. Or maybe you wanna

02:41:06 --> 02:41:11
understand the nature of how are the models of set theory related to their

02:41:10 --> 02:41:15
forcing extensions and so on. And so this led to things,

02:41:14 --> 02:41:19
um that I call, say, set theoretic potentialism, where you think about a set theoretic

02:41:18 --> 02:41:23
universe in a potentialist way. Not in

02:41:22 --> 02:41:27
the sense of potential infinity directly, because all of these universes have

02:41:26 --> 02:41:30
infinite sets inside them already. But they're potentialist in the sense

02:41:29 --> 02:41:34
that we could have more sets. The universe could be wider

02:41:33 --> 02:41:38
and taller and so on, you know, by forcing or by

02:41:37 --> 02:41:42
extending upward. And so we wanna understand the nature of

02:41:41 --> 02:41:46
this realm of set theoretic universes.

02:41:45 --> 02:41:49
And, and that's quite some exciting work. And so with

02:41:48 --> 02:41:53
Benedikt Loewe and I, we proved some theorems on the modal logic of

02:41:52 --> 02:41:57
orcing and set theoretic potentialism under end extension. I've done a bunch

02:41:56 --> 02:42:01
of work on this topic. And, and also I

02:42:00 --> 02:42:05
mounted together with Gunter Fuchs and Jonas Riets, who was one of my own PhD students, the topic of set theoretic

02:42:08 --> 02:42:13
geology, which is studying... It's taking the metaphor of

02:42:12 --> 02:42:16
orcing. I mean, in forcing, you have the ground model and the forcing

02:42:15 --> 02:42:19
extension. And when I was first working with Jonas

02:42:20 --> 02:42:24
he said, "I wanna undo forcing. I wanna go

02:42:23 --> 02:42:28
backwards." And I at first said, "But Jonas, it doesn't work

02:42:27 --> 02:42:32
that way. You start in the model, in the ground model, and you go out, you go to the bigger one.

02:42:31 --> 02:42:36
You know, that's how forcing works." And he said, "No, no, I wanna go

02:42:35 --> 02:42:39
backwards." And, and so he was quite persistent, actually.

02:42:38 --> 02:42:43
And um, and so finally, I said, "Okay, let's, let's do it. Let's

02:42:42 --> 02:42:47
take it seriously." And so we sat down and, and s- and started

02:42:46 --> 02:42:51
thinking, you know, more precisely and carefully and deeply about the nature

02:42:50 --> 02:42:54
of taking a set theoretic universe and seeing where did it come

02:42:53 --> 02:42:59
from by forcing, which was a new way of thinking about forcing at the time.

02:42:58 --> 02:43:01
- Like reverse engineering the forcing?

02:43:00 --> 02:43:04
- Yeah, something like that. Forcing is a way of producing a new

02:43:04 --> 02:43:09
universe. And so you could start somewhere and go to that new universe, or you could look

02:43:08 --> 02:43:13
where you are and say, "Well, look, I got here by doing that already in the past."

02:43:12 --> 02:43:17
So we defined models of the bedrock model

02:43:16 --> 02:43:21
and ground, you know, sort of undoing the forcing. And, and

02:43:20 --> 02:43:25
really, it was quite fruitful. And I view this as part of the sort of pluralist

02:43:24 --> 02:43:29
perspective, except the difference is that set theoretic geology is

02:43:28 --> 02:43:33
amenable to the universe view. So even though the work

02:43:32 --> 02:43:40
was inspired by this philosophical view on the multiverse view, nevertheless, the

02:43:39 --> 02:43:44
central ideas of geology have now been picked up

02:43:43 --> 02:43:48
by the people with the research program in the universe view. Because it turns

02:43:47 --> 02:43:52
out that set theoretic geology is helping them or us to

02:43:51 --> 02:43:56
discover the nature of the one true universe relates to its

02:43:55 --> 02:44:00
mantle. There's this concept of the set theoretic mantle that I had introduced in

02:43:59 --> 02:44:04
a way that is extremely interesting. And so it's

02:44:03 --> 02:44:07
historically quite funny, I think, because this research program

02:44:07 --> 02:44:11
that grew entirely out of the pluralist point of

02:44:10 --> 02:44:15
view ended up being picked up by the

02:44:14 --> 02:44:21
universe point of view research program in a, in a way that is quite important.

02:44:20 --> 02:44:28
- Can you prove something in the world that you arrived at through forcing and

02:44:27 --> 02:44:31
then take some of that back to the ground model?

02:44:30 --> 02:44:35
- Yeah, absolutely. And that's a really powerful argument method, actually. People often

02:44:34 --> 02:44:39
want to do that. Suppose you're in some set theoretic

02:44:38 --> 02:44:42
ontext. You know, you could think about as living in a set theoretic

02:44:41 --> 02:44:46
universe, and you want to prove something in that universe only.

02:44:45 --> 02:44:50
But maybe one way to do it is to first

02:44:49 --> 02:44:54
construct this forcing extension and then use the features

02:44:53 --> 02:44:58
about this forcing extension to realize that certain things must have already been

02:44:57 --> 02:45:02
true in the ground model. And then you throw the forcing extensions away and you...

02:45:01 --> 02:45:02
- Oh, cool

02:45:01 --> 02:45:06
- ...yeah. So this can happen. To pick a more elementary

02:45:05 --> 02:45:09
example, if you think about the early days of people

02:45:08 --> 02:45:15
reasoning with the complex numbers before they really understood them.

02:45:14 --> 02:45:17
So they would have these algebraic equations that they're trying to

02:45:16 --> 02:45:21
solve, you know, and they would have the tools and methods of

02:45:20 --> 02:45:25
doing it, but then in the course of, you know, so they would have to do things to the polynomial

02:45:24 --> 02:45:29
and change the factors and so on, and produce other polynomials and solve them and

02:45:28 --> 02:45:33
so on. And sometimes, they could produce solutions.

02:45:33 --> 02:45:38
in the middle of their construction, they were led to, like, the

02:45:37 --> 02:45:41
square root of minus five or something, you know, in the

02:45:40 --> 02:45:45
construction. And they didn't have any meaning for that, but they

02:45:44 --> 02:45:49
would just do it symbolically, you know. And, and

02:45:48 --> 02:45:53
eventually, it would turn in, you know, because of the methods that they had, they

02:45:52 --> 02:45:57
would combine and they would cancel and so on, and all the complex parts would cancel out and they'd end

02:45:56 --> 02:46:01
up with this, you know, actual answer, you know, three plus square root of

02:46:00 --> 02:46:05
17 or whatever. And, and they could check it and it worked. It was

02:46:04 --> 02:46:08
a solution of the original equation. And so it must have been

02:46:08 --> 02:46:12
bewildering to them because they would start with this question purely

02:46:12 --> 02:46:16
in the real numbers, an algebraic question, and they would

02:46:15 --> 02:46:20
march on their method and proceed through the land of nonsense, you

02:46:19 --> 02:46:24
know, with these square roots of negative numbers and then end up with

02:46:23 --> 02:46:30
an answer that was real again that they could verify was correct.

02:46:29 --> 02:46:34
And so, I view this kind of forcing argument that I was just describing

02:46:33 --> 02:46:38
in a similar way. You start in set theory, and you go to

02:46:37 --> 02:46:42
this land of nonsense in the forcing extension, this imaginary world.

02:46:41 --> 02:46:46
And you argue and you come back. I mean, you make a consequence in the ground model,

02:46:45 --> 02:46:48
and it's such a beautiful way of arguing.

02:46:47 --> 02:46:52
- So, speaking of the land of nonsense, I have to ask you about surreal numbers, but

02:46:51 --> 02:46:54
first, I need another bathroom break.

02:46:54 --> 02:46:58
All right, we're back, and there's this aforementioned

02:46:58 --> 02:47:03
wonderful blog post on the surreal numbers, and

02:47:04 --> 02:47:08
that there's quite a simple surreal number generation

02:47:08 --> 02:47:11
process that can basically construct all numbers.

02:47:11 --> 02:47:16
So, maybe this is a good spot to ask,

02:47:15 --> 02:47:21
what are surreal numbers and what is the way we can generate all numbers?

02:47:20 --> 02:47:25
- So, the surreal number system is an amazing,

02:47:24 --> 02:47:31
amazingly beautiful mathematical system that was introduced by John Conway.

02:47:30 --> 02:47:34
- Rest in peace, one of the great mathematicians ever on this earth.

02:47:33 --> 02:47:38
- Yes, absolutely. And I really admire his

02:47:37 --> 02:47:42
tyle of mathematical thinking and working in mathematics, and the

02:47:41 --> 02:47:45
surreal number system is a good instance of this. So, the way I think about the

02:47:44 --> 02:47:49
surreal numbers system is what it's doing is providing us

02:47:48 --> 02:47:53
a number system that unifies all the other number systems. So, it

02:47:52 --> 02:47:57
extends the real numbers. Well, not only, it extends the

02:47:56 --> 02:48:00
integers, the natural numbers and the integers and the rational numbers, and the real

02:47:59 --> 02:48:04
numbers, but also the ordinals and the

02:48:03 --> 02:48:10
infinitesimals. So, they're all sitting there inside the surreal numbers, and

02:48:10 --> 02:48:14
it's this colossal system of numbers. It's not a

02:48:14 --> 02:48:18
set even. It's a proper class, it turns out, because it contains all

02:48:17 --> 02:48:24
the ordinal numbers. But it's generated from nothing

02:48:23 --> 02:48:28
by a single rule, and the rule is, so we're

02:48:27 --> 02:48:32
gonna generate the numbers in stages, in transfinite sequence of

02:48:31 --> 02:48:36
stages. And at every stage, we take the numbers that we

02:48:35 --> 02:48:40
have so far and in all possible ways, we divide them into

02:48:39 --> 02:48:44
two sets, a lower set and an upper set, or a left set and

02:48:43 --> 02:48:48
a right set. So we divide them into these two sets, so that

02:48:47 --> 02:48:52
everything in the left set is less than everything in the right set, and then at that

02:48:51 --> 02:48:57
moment, we create a new number that fits in the gap

02:48:56 --> 02:49:04
between L and R. Okay? That's it. That's all we do. So, let me say it again.

02:49:05 --> 02:49:09
The rule is, we proceed in stages, and at any

02:49:08 --> 02:49:13
stage, then in all possible ways, we divide the

02:49:12 --> 02:49:15
numbers we have into two collections,

02:49:16 --> 02:49:20
the left set and the right set, so that everything in the left set is less than everything in the right

02:49:19 --> 02:49:28
set. And we create a new number, a new surreal number that will fit in that gap.

02:49:27 --> 02:49:32
Okay. So, for example, we could start... At the beginning, we don't have any numbers.

02:49:31 --> 02:49:36
We haven't created anything yet, and so, well,

02:49:35 --> 02:49:40
we could take nothing, and we could divide it into two sets, the empty lower

02:49:39 --> 02:49:44
set and the empty upper set. I mean, the two empty sets.

02:49:43 --> 02:49:48
And everything in the empty set is less than everything in the empty set because that's a vacuous

02:49:47 --> 02:49:52
tatement. So we satisfy the conditions,

02:49:51 --> 02:49:56
and we apply the number generation rule, which says we should create

02:49:55 --> 02:50:00
a new number. And this is what I call the Big Bang of

02:49:59 --> 02:50:03
numbers, the surreal genesis when the number zero is

02:50:03 --> 02:50:07
born. Zero is the firstborn number that is bigger than

02:50:07 --> 02:50:10
everything in the empty set and less than everything in the empty

02:50:09 --> 02:50:14
set. Okay, but now we have this number zero, and so

02:50:13 --> 02:50:18
therefore, we now can define new gaps.

02:50:17 --> 02:50:22
Because if we put zero into the left set and have an

02:50:21 --> 02:50:26
empty right set, then we should create a new number that's bigger than

02:50:25 --> 02:50:30
zero and less than everything in the empty set, and that number is called the number

02:50:29 --> 02:50:36
one. And similarly, at that same stage, we could have put zero into the right set,

02:50:36 --> 02:50:41
and so that would be the firstborn number that's less than zero, which is called

02:50:40 --> 02:50:44
minus one. So now we have three numbers, minus one,

02:50:43 --> 02:50:48
zero and one, and they have four gaps because there could be a number below

02:50:47 --> 02:50:52
minus one or between minus one and zero or between zero and one

02:50:51 --> 02:50:56
or above one, and so we create those four new

02:50:55 --> 02:51:00
numbers. The first number above one is called two. The first number between zero and one

02:50:59 --> 02:51:04
is called 1/2, and then on the negative side, we have minus 1/2 and minus

02:51:03 --> 02:51:08
two and so on. So now we have, what is that, seven numbers?

02:51:07 --> 02:51:12
seven numbers. So there's eight gaps between them. So at the next

02:51:11 --> 02:51:16
birthday, they call them, the next stage will be born all the numbers

02:51:15 --> 02:51:19
between those gaps, and then between those and between those and so

02:51:18 --> 02:51:22
n. And as the days progress, we get more and more

02:51:21 --> 02:51:26
numbers, but those are just the finite birthdays, because as I said, it's a

02:51:25 --> 02:51:30
transfinite process. So at day omega, that's the

02:51:29 --> 02:51:34
first infinite day, we're going to create a lot of

02:51:33 --> 02:51:38
new surreal numbers. So every real number will

02:51:37 --> 02:51:42
be born at that stage because every real number fills a

02:51:41 --> 02:51:45
gap in the previously born rational numbers that we had just talked

02:51:44 --> 02:51:49
about. It's not all the rationals, because actually the rational numbers that are born at the

02:51:48 --> 02:51:53
finite stages are just the rationals whose denominator is a power of

02:51:52 --> 02:51:57
two, it turns out. Those are called the dyadic rationals.

02:51:56 --> 02:52:00
So the real numbers are all born on day omega,

02:51:59 --> 02:52:04
but also some other numbers are born on day omega, namely,

02:52:04 --> 02:52:09
the ordinal omega itself is the firstborn number that's bigger than all those

02:52:08 --> 02:52:13
finite numbers, and minus omega is the firstborn number

02:52:12 --> 02:52:17
that's less than all those finite numbers. But also, we have

02:52:16 --> 02:52:21
the number epsilon, which is the firstborn number that's strictly bigger than

02:52:20 --> 02:52:24
zero and strictly less than all the positive rational

02:52:24 --> 02:52:28
numbers. So that's going to be an infinitesimal number in that

02:52:27 --> 02:52:32
gap, and so on. On day omega plus one, we

02:52:31 --> 02:52:36
get more numbers, and then omega plus two and so on. And the numbers just keep coming

02:52:35 --> 02:52:39
forever. So, this is how you build the surreal number

02:52:38 --> 02:52:43
system. And then it turns out you can define the arithmetic

02:52:42 --> 02:52:47
operations of addition and multiplication in a natural way that

02:52:46 --> 02:52:50
is engaging with this recursive definition. So we have sort of

02:52:50 --> 02:52:54
recursive definitions of plus and times for the

02:52:53 --> 02:52:58
surreal numbers. And it turns out you can prove

02:52:57 --> 02:53:02
that they make the surreal numbers into what's called an ordered

02:53:01 --> 02:53:06
field. So they satisfy the field axioms, which means that you have distributivity

02:53:05 --> 02:53:10
and commutativity of addition and multiplication.

02:53:09 --> 02:53:13
multiplication, and also you have reciprocals for

02:53:12 --> 02:53:17
every non-zero number. You can divide by the number. So you can add

02:53:16 --> 02:53:21
and multiply and divide and subtract. And furthermore, you can take

02:53:20 --> 02:53:27
square roots. And furthermore, every odd degree polynomial has a root,

02:53:27 --> 02:53:31
which is true in the real numbers, because if you think about, say, a cubic or a

02:53:31 --> 02:53:36
fifth degree polynomial, then you know it's going to cross the

02:53:35 --> 02:53:39
axis, because it has opposite behaviors on the two

02:53:38 --> 02:53:43
infinities, because it's an odd degree polynomial. So on the positive side,

02:53:42 --> 02:53:47
it's going to the positive infinity. On the negative side, it would be going to minus infinity.

02:53:46 --> 02:53:50
So it has to cross. So we know in the real numbers,

02:53:49 --> 02:53:54
every odd degree polynomial has a root. And

02:53:53 --> 02:53:58
that's also true in the surreal numbers. So that makes it what's called a

02:53:57 --> 02:54:02
real closed field, which is a very nice mathematical theory.

02:54:02 --> 02:54:07
So it's really quite interesting how we can find copies

02:54:06 --> 02:54:10
of all these other number systems inside the surreal numbers.

02:54:09 --> 02:54:13
- But the surreal numbers are fundamentally discontinuous as you're worried about.

02:54:12 --> 02:54:15
What are the consequences of this?

02:54:14 --> 02:54:19
- Right. So the surreal numbers have a property that they form

02:54:18 --> 02:54:24
a non-standard model of the real field, which means that

02:54:24 --> 02:54:29
hey provide a notion of infinitesimality that one can use to develop

02:54:28 --> 02:54:32
calculus on the grounds of Robinson's non-standard theory that I had

02:54:31 --> 02:54:36
mentioned earlier. But they don't have

02:54:35 --> 02:54:40
the least upper bound property for subcollections. There's

02:54:39 --> 02:54:44
no set of surreal numbers, no non-trivial set of surreal numbers

02:54:43 --> 02:54:48
has at least upper bound, and there are no convergent sequences in

02:54:47 --> 02:54:52
the surreal numbers. And so for the sort of ordinary

02:54:51 --> 02:54:55
use in calculus based on limits and

02:54:54 --> 02:54:59
convergence, that method does not work in the surreal numbers

02:54:58 --> 02:55:03
at all. So that's what I mean when I say the surreal numbers are fundamentally

02:55:02 --> 02:55:06
discontinuous. They have a fundamental discontinuity going

02:55:05 --> 02:55:10
on. But you can still do calculus with them, because you have

02:55:09 --> 02:55:13
infinitesimals if you use these non-standard

02:55:12 --> 02:55:17
methods, the infinitesimal-based methods to calculus. And

02:55:16 --> 02:55:21
people do that. I once organized a

02:55:20 --> 02:55:25
conference in New York, and we had John Conway as a speaker at that conference. And there

02:55:24 --> 02:55:29
was a question session, and someone asked him, I mean,

02:55:28 --> 02:55:33
it's a bit rude, I think, but they

02:55:32 --> 02:55:37
asked it and the question was, "What is your greatest

02:55:36 --> 02:55:41
disappointment in life?" I mean, I would never ask a question like that at a conference

02:55:40 --> 02:55:44
in a very public setting. But Conway was extremely graceful,

02:55:44 --> 02:55:47
and he answered by saying that,

02:55:47 --> 02:55:52
"The surreal numbers..." Not the numbers themselves, but the reception

02:55:51 --> 02:55:56
of the surreal numbers, because he had ambition that the

02:55:55 --> 02:56:00
surreal numbers would become a fundamental number

02:55:59 --> 02:56:05
system used throughout mathematics and science, because it was able to do

02:56:05 --> 02:56:10
non-set analysis, it was able to do calculus, it unified the ordinals and so on. And it's

02:56:09 --> 02:56:14
uch a unifying, amazing structure, beautiful structure

02:56:13 --> 02:56:18
with elegant proofs and sophisticated ideas all around

02:56:17 --> 02:56:26
it. And he was disappointed that it never really achieved that unifying

02:56:25 --> 02:56:30
status that he had the ambition for. And this,

02:56:29 --> 02:56:32
he mentioned as his greatest disappointment.

02:56:32 --> 02:56:37
- Yeah, Donald Knuth tried to celebrate it. It never quite took hold.

02:56:36 --> 02:56:41
- So I don't want to give the impression though that the surreal numbers are not widely studied,

02:56:40 --> 02:56:42
because there are thousands of people who are...

02:56:41 --> 02:56:42
- Sure

02:56:42 --> 02:56:46
- ...studying it. In fact, Philip Ehrlich, who is one of the world experts on

02:56:45 --> 02:56:50
the surreal numbers, mentioned to me once that Conway was his own

02:56:50 --> 02:56:54
worst enemy with regard to that very issue, because

02:56:54 --> 02:56:58
in the Conway style, everything is a game. And he

02:56:57 --> 02:57:01
treated the surreal numbers as a kind of plaything, a

02:57:00 --> 02:57:05
toy, and maybe that makes people

02:57:04 --> 02:57:08
not take it seriously. Although my view is that it is

02:57:07 --> 02:57:12
extremely serious, useful, and profound,

02:57:11 --> 02:57:16
and I've been writing a whole series of essays on the surreal numbers for

02:57:15 --> 02:57:21
my Substack at Infinitely More. And I just find the whole subject so

02:57:20 --> 02:57:25
fascinating and beautiful. I mean, it's true. I'm not

02:57:24 --> 02:57:31
applying it in engineering, which maybe was part of this Conway ambition.

02:57:30 --> 02:57:33
- And I just wanted to, before I forget, mention

02:57:34 --> 02:57:39
the Conway, turning everything into a game. It is a fascinating

02:57:38 --> 02:57:42
point that I didn't quite think about, which I think the Game of Life is just an

02:57:41 --> 02:57:46
example of exploration of cellular automata. I think cellular automata is one of the

02:57:45 --> 02:57:50
most incredible, complicated, fascinating... It feels like an

02:57:49 --> 02:57:54
open door into a world we have not quite yet explored. And it's such

02:57:53 --> 02:57:58
a beautiful illustration of that world, the Game of Life, but calling it a

02:57:57 --> 02:58:02
game, maybe life balances it, 'cause that's your powerful word,

02:58:01 --> 02:58:05
but it's not quite a game. It's a fascinating

02:58:04 --> 02:58:09
invitation to an incredibly complicated and fascinating mathematical

02:58:08 --> 02:58:13
world. I think every time I see cellular automata and the fact that

02:58:12 --> 02:58:17
we don't quite have mathematical tools to make sense

02:58:16 --> 02:58:21
of that world, it fills me with awe. Speaking of a thousand years from

02:58:20 --> 02:58:24
now, it feels like that is a world we might make some progress on.

02:58:23 --> 02:58:28
- The Game of Life is a sort of playground for computably undecidable

02:58:27 --> 02:58:32
questions because in fact, you can prove that the

02:58:31 --> 02:58:36
question of whether a given cell will ever become alive

02:58:36 --> 02:58:40
is computably undecidable. In other words,

02:58:39 --> 02:58:44
given a configuration and you ask, "Will this particular cell ever

02:58:43 --> 02:58:50
you know, be alive?" "...in the evolution?" And you can prove that that

02:58:49 --> 02:58:54
question is equivalent to the Halting Problem. It's computably

02:58:53 --> 02:58:58
undecidable. It's semi-decidable in the sense that if it will

02:58:57 --> 02:59:02
become alive, then you will know it at a finite stage because you could just run the

02:59:01 --> 02:59:06
Game of Life algorithm and let it run. And if it ever did come

02:59:05 --> 02:59:10
alive, you could say, "Yeah, it was alive." But if you've run it for a thousand

02:59:09 --> 02:59:14
years and it hasn't come alive yet, then you don't necessarily seem to

02:59:13 --> 02:59:17
have any basis for saying, "No, it won't ever come alive" if the behavior was very

02:59:17 --> 02:59:19
complicated.

02:59:18 --> 02:59:22
Maybe if you have a complete understanding of the evolution of the behavior, then you can say

02:59:22 --> 02:59:26
no, but you can prove you won't always have that understanding-

02:59:25 --> 02:59:29
... precisely because the problem is equivalent to the halting problem.

02:59:28 --> 02:59:32
- And nevertheless, when you sit back and look and visualize the thing, some

02:59:31 --> 02:59:36
little mini cellular automata civilizations are born and die quickly,

02:59:35 --> 02:59:40
and some are very predictable and boring, but some have this

02:59:39 --> 02:59:44
rich incredible complexity. And maybe that

02:59:43 --> 02:59:49
speaks to a thing I wanted to ask on the halting problem and decidability.

02:59:50 --> 02:59:54
You've mentioned this thing where if you understand the program deeply, you

02:59:54 --> 02:59:58
might be able to say something. So can we say something interesting

02:59:57 --> 03:00:02
about maybe once statistically how many programs

03:00:02 --> 03:00:07
we know something about in terms of whether they halt or not? Or what does it mean to

03:00:06 --> 03:00:12
understand a program deeply enough to be able to make a prediction?

03:00:11 --> 03:00:16
- The main lesson of computability theory in my view

03:00:16 --> 03:00:23
is that it's never the case that you can have a thorough

03:00:22 --> 03:00:26
understanding of the behavior of a program by looking at the

03:00:25 --> 03:00:30
program, and that the content of what you learn from

03:00:29 --> 03:00:34
a program, I mean, in the most general case, is always obtained just by

03:00:33 --> 03:00:38
running it and looking at the behavior. And the

03:00:37 --> 03:00:42
proof of that is there's a theorem called Rice's Theorem, which

03:00:41 --> 03:00:49
makes that idea completely robust. But I want to just take a little detour towards

03:00:48 --> 03:00:52
another question riffing on something that you just

03:00:51 --> 03:00:56
said. Namely, one can ask the question,

03:00:55 --> 03:01:00
What is the behavior of a random program? So you

03:00:59 --> 03:01:04
have some formal computing language, you know, and you want to, you know, look

03:01:03 --> 03:01:08
at the collection of all programs of a certain size. Maybe there's only finitely

03:01:07 --> 03:01:10
many. And

03:01:10 --> 03:01:14
can you say something about the behavior of a randomly chosen one, like with a

03:01:14 --> 03:01:18
certain likelihood it will have a certain behavior? And the answer turns out

03:01:18 --> 03:01:22
o be extremely interesting. Once years ago,

03:01:21 --> 03:01:26
Alexey Myasnikov asked me a question. He had this concept of a

03:01:25 --> 03:01:29
decision problem with a black hole, and what that means is

03:01:29 --> 03:01:33
it's a decision problem which is possibly difficult in the worst

03:01:33 --> 03:01:37
case, but the difficulty was concentrated in a very

03:01:36 --> 03:01:43
tiny region called the black hole. And outside of that black hole, it was very easy.

03:01:42 --> 03:01:47
And so, for example, this kind of problem is a terrible problem to use if

03:01:46 --> 03:01:51
you're basing your encryption scheme, you know, you don't

03:01:50 --> 03:01:54
want to use a black hole problem because if someone can rob the bank

03:01:54 --> 03:01:58
95% of the time, you know, then that's not what you want,

03:01:58 --> 03:02:02
or even any non-trivial percent of the time is too dangerous.

03:02:01 --> 03:02:09
So you don't want to use problems that are, you know, almost every case is easily

03:02:08 --> 03:02:13
solved as the basis of your encryption. And the question Alexey

03:02:12 --> 03:02:16
asked me was, "Does the halting problem have a black

03:02:15 --> 03:02:20
hole?" You know? And so if we take, say, the standard model of

03:02:19 --> 03:02:24
Turing machines, it's one-way infinite tape with zeros

03:02:23 --> 03:02:28
and ones on the tape and so on, the head moving back and forth, and you know, it

03:02:27 --> 03:02:32
stops when it gets into the halt state, then it turns

03:02:31 --> 03:02:36
out we proved that there is a black hole. And what that

03:02:35 --> 03:02:39
means is there's a computer procedure that decides correctly

03:02:39 --> 03:02:44
almost every instance of the halting problem. Even though the halting problem is not

03:02:43 --> 03:02:48
decidable, we can decide almost every instance. So, more

03:02:47 --> 03:02:51
precisely, there's a collection of Turing machine programs

03:02:51 --> 03:02:56
uch that we can easily decide whether a program's in that

03:02:55 --> 03:03:00
collection or not. And for the programs in the collection,

03:02:59 --> 03:03:03
we can decide the halting problem for those programs

03:03:02 --> 03:03:07
easily. And furthermore, almost every program is

03:03:06 --> 03:03:11
in the collection in the sense that as the number of states goes to, you know,

03:03:10 --> 03:03:18
becomes large, the proportion of programs in the collection goes to 100%.

03:03:18 --> 03:03:24
So the asymptotic density of the programs is one.

03:03:23 --> 03:03:27
And the proof was quite fascinating because it's one of these situations where

03:03:26 --> 03:03:31
the theorem sounds really surprising, I think, to many people when I first tell it,

03:03:30 --> 03:03:35
I mean, to computability experts. Then it's sort of

03:03:34 --> 03:03:39
intriguing to think that you can solve almost every instance of a halting

03:03:38 --> 03:03:43
problem. But then when they hear the proof, it's

03:03:42 --> 03:03:48
completely a letdown. Unfortunately, nobody likes the theorem after the proof.

03:03:47 --> 03:03:51
And so the proof is so simple,

03:03:50 --> 03:03:55
though. If you know how a Turing machine operates, there's this infinite

03:03:54 --> 03:03:58
paper tape on which the machine writes zeros and ones, and the head moves

03:03:58 --> 03:04:02
back and forth according to rigid instructions. And the instructions are

03:04:01 --> 03:04:06
all of the form,

03:04:05 --> 03:04:10
if the machine is in such and such a state and it's reading such and such symbol

03:04:09 --> 03:04:14
on the tape, then it should write this symbol on the tape and it should

03:04:13 --> 03:04:18
change to this new state specified, and it should either move left or right

03:04:17 --> 03:04:25
as specified. So a program consists of instructions like that. If you look at,

03:04:25 --> 03:04:30
If you look at a program, you know, one of the states is the halt state and that's

03:04:29 --> 03:04:35
when the program halts. But you can calculate how many programs

03:04:35 --> 03:04:39
don't have any instruction that transitions to the halt

03:04:38 --> 03:04:43
state. You can easily calculate the proportion. And

03:04:42 --> 03:04:47
in the limit, it goes to 1 over E squared, 13 and a half

03:04:46 --> 03:04:51
percent. If you calculate the limit, the

03:04:50 --> 03:04:55
proportion of programs with end states that don't

03:04:54 --> 03:04:59
ever halt because they don't have any instruction saying halt.

03:04:58 --> 03:05:03
Those programs obviously never halt because they can't halt. They don't have any

03:05:02 --> 03:05:04
instruction that says halt.

03:05:04 --> 03:05:07
- So 13% of programs, you could say,

03:05:06 --> 03:05:11
- 13%, you can say they don't halt, because you just look at them and you can understand them.

03:05:10 --> 03:05:12
- There's no halt state.

03:05:11 --> 03:05:14
- There's no... They never change to the halt state, so they can't halt.

03:05:13 --> 03:05:18
- I mean, that nevertheless is beautiful to know.

03:05:17 --> 03:05:22
- So that's a kind of trivial reason for non-halting, you know.

03:05:21 --> 03:05:25
And when I first made that observation, I thought, "Okay, this is the proof

03:05:24 --> 03:05:29
strategy." Because we wanted... I wanted to say at first the

03:05:28 --> 03:05:33
goal was, look, that's a stupid reason for a program not to

03:05:32 --> 03:05:38
halt. And I just want to pile up as many stupid reasons as I can think of...

03:05:37 --> 03:05:44
until it gets more than 50%, and then I can say most.

03:05:43 --> 03:05:44
- That was brilliant.

03:05:43 --> 03:05:45
- Yeah. That was my goal.

03:05:44 --> 03:05:46
- I love this.

03:05:45 --> 03:05:49
- Yeah. So we thought more about it, though, and we hit the

03:05:48 --> 03:05:53
jackpot because we found one gigantic stupid reason that

03:05:52 --> 03:06:01
converged to 100%. I mean, in the limit. And so, the stupid reason for a

03:06:00 --> 03:06:05
program not to halt is that, well, if you think about

03:06:04 --> 03:06:08
he behavior, see, the head is sitting there. It's on the leftmost cell of the

03:06:07 --> 03:06:11
tape at the very beginning. It's in the start

03:06:10 --> 03:06:15
state, and the head is following an instruction. And the instruction says,

03:06:14 --> 03:06:19
"When you're in the start state," which it is, "and you're reading something on the tape,

03:06:18 --> 03:06:23
then you should write something and you should change to a new state, and you should either move

03:06:22 --> 03:06:27
left or right." But half of them move left.

03:06:27 --> 03:06:32
But if you move left and you are already at the end, then the head falls

03:06:31 --> 03:06:36
off. And so the computation stops because the head fell off the tape.

03:06:37 --> 03:06:41
That's a pretty stupid reason. Okay, but that's half of them already just like

03:06:40 --> 03:06:45
that. Okay, and then some of them went right, and they changed to a new

03:06:44 --> 03:06:49
state. And amongst those, you know, the new state, half of those ones are going left and

03:06:48 --> 03:06:53
half are going right from that place, and then most of those are

03:06:52 --> 03:06:57
changing to a new state. When there's a lot of states, it's very likely that the next state that you

03:06:56 --> 03:07:00
transition to is new. And so you get this

03:06:59 --> 03:07:04
random walk behavior, if you know what that means, where half go left and half go right at

03:07:03 --> 03:07:08
each step. And there's a theorem due to Pólya which is

03:07:07 --> 03:07:12
called the Pólya recurrence theorem, which says when you have

03:07:11 --> 03:07:16
a random walk, a one-dimensional random walk, then it's very

03:07:15 --> 03:07:19
likely to come back to where you started. And when that happens for

03:07:18 --> 03:07:23
us, then half of them from that place fall off on the next step.

03:07:23 --> 03:07:28
And so you can show using this kind of analysis that

03:07:28 --> 03:07:33
he probability one behavior of a random Turing machine is

03:07:32 --> 03:07:36
that the head falls off the tape before it repeats a

03:07:35 --> 03:07:40
state. And that is the stupid proof that

03:07:39 --> 03:07:43
shows how to solve the halting problem. Because when that happens, we can answer the halting problem

03:07:42 --> 03:07:47
saying, "No, the computation stopped because the machine crashed, not because

03:07:46 --> 03:07:51
it halted, so therefore it doesn't count as halting on some

03:07:50 --> 03:07:55
accounts." Or, you know, if you want to define that as halting, crashing as halting,

03:07:54 --> 03:07:59
then... But in any case, however it is that you set up your formalism, you're

03:07:58 --> 03:08:02
going to be able to answer the question for the behavior of the

03:08:01 --> 03:08:04
machine when the head falls off.

03:08:03 --> 03:08:09
- So statistically, in the limit, you solve the halting problem.

03:08:08 --> 03:08:12
- Yes, exactly. Computably solve it. Yeah.

03:08:11 --> 03:08:16
- What do we take from that? Because you didn't solve the halting problem.

03:08:15 --> 03:08:18
- No, it's impossible to fully solve...

03:08:17 --> 03:08:20
the halting problem correctly in all cases.

03:08:20 --> 03:08:23
- That's pretty cool. That's kind of... I mean, I don't know. This is...

03:08:22 --> 03:08:27
