- It's a probabilistic way... I mean, it's probabilistic in the sense that

03:08:26 --> 03:08:29
we're solving almost all instances...

03:08:28 --> 03:08:33
computably. There are versions of this that are maybe more interesting

03:08:32 --> 03:08:37
from the point of view of complexity theory and actually useful. I mean, there's the whole

03:08:36 --> 03:08:40
P-NP problem and so on. And there's this genre of NP-complete

03:08:39 --> 03:08:43
problems, which are problems that are

03:08:42 --> 03:08:47
infeasible. They would take exponential time to solve them in

03:08:46 --> 03:08:51
the ordinary way. And they're not known to be polynomial time solvable,

03:08:50 --> 03:08:55
although in these cases it's an open question whether there is a polynomial time

03:08:54 --> 03:09:02
algorithm, a feasible algorithm. And for most for most of the NP-complete

03:09:01 --> 03:09:05
problems, you can prove that there's a

03:09:04 --> 03:09:09
polynomial time approximation that solves almost all instances...

03:09:09 --> 03:09:13
in a feasible amount of time. So like the knapsack problem, you know,

03:09:12 --> 03:09:17
packing problems, and so on, other kinds of problems, satisfaction problems,

03:09:16 --> 03:09:21
when... Depending on how you set up the formalism, you can prove,

03:09:20 --> 03:09:24
and I've proven many instances of this but also I

03:09:23 --> 03:09:28
think it's widespread for almost all the NP-complete

03:09:27 --> 03:09:32
problems, the difficult problems, and these are important problems for industrial

03:09:31 --> 03:09:37
application when these are problems that we actually want to solve. We can have

03:09:37 --> 03:09:42
feasible algorithms that solve almost every instance of them.

03:09:42 --> 03:09:46
- The amount of fields and topics you've worked on is truly

03:09:45 --> 03:09:50
incredible. I have to ask about P versus NP. This is one of the big

03:09:50 --> 03:09:54
open problems in complexity theory. So for people who don't know, it's about the relation

03:09:53 --> 03:09:58
between computation time and problem complexity. Do you think it will ever be

03:09:57 --> 03:10:03
solved? And is there any chance the weird counterintuitive

03:10:03 --> 03:10:07
thing might be true that P equals NP?

03:10:06 --> 03:10:10
- Yeah, that's an interesting question. Sometimes people ask about whether it could be

03:10:09 --> 03:10:15
independent, which I think is... ...An interesting question for logicians.

03:10:15 --> 03:10:19
And of course, well, one has to say if you're entertaining

03:10:19 --> 03:10:23
the idea of independence, you know, over which theory? Because

03:10:23 --> 03:10:27
very statement is going to be independent over an extremely weak

03:10:26 --> 03:10:30
theory. So that's, you know, it doesn't make sense to say it's independent all by

03:10:29 --> 03:10:34
itself. You're only independent relative to a theory, right? So the way I think

03:10:33 --> 03:10:40
about P-NP is that... I mean, of course it's a theoretical question about

03:10:40 --> 03:10:45
he asymptotic behavior of these problems. I mean, for a

03:10:44 --> 03:10:49
problem to be in P means that there, you know, there is a computable

03:10:48 --> 03:10:53
decision procedure that runs in time bounded by

03:10:52 --> 03:10:56
some polynomial. But the coefficients on that polynomial could be

03:10:55 --> 03:11:03
normous, and the degree could be incredibly high. And so for small values of

03:11:02 --> 03:11:07
inputs, then it doesn't make sense to talk about this polynomial

03:11:06 --> 03:11:11
time feasibility with respect to, say, the range of problem inputs that we will

03:11:10 --> 03:11:15
ever give it in our lifetime or in

03:11:14 --> 03:11:19
the span of human civilization or whatever. I mean, because it's an

03:11:18 --> 03:11:23
asymptotic property, it's really in the limit as the

03:11:22 --> 03:11:27
size of the inputs goes to infinity, that's the only time that polynomial

03:11:26 --> 03:11:30
or NP becomes relevant. And so maybe it's

03:11:29 --> 03:11:33
important to keep that in mind when. Sometimes you find

03:11:33 --> 03:11:38
kind of overblown remarks made about, you know, if P

03:11:37 --> 03:11:42
equals NP, then this will be incredibly important for human civilization

03:11:41 --> 03:11:45
because it means that we'll have feasible algorithms for solving these incredibly important-

03:11:45 --> 03:11:50
... problems in NP. You know, that it would

03:11:49 --> 03:11:53
cause immense wealth for human societies and so on because we

03:11:53 --> 03:11:57
would be able to solve these otherwise intractable problems, and

03:11:56 --> 03:12:01
that would be the basis of new technology and industry and so forth. I

03:12:00 --> 03:12:04
mean, people make these kind of remarks, but-

03:12:03 --> 03:12:04
Of course

03:12:04 --> 03:12:08
- ... you have to temper those remarks by the realization

03:12:07 --> 03:12:12
that P and P equal NP or P not equal NP are

03:12:11 --> 03:12:16
not about these practical things at all because of the asymptotic

03:12:15 --> 03:12:20
nature of the question itself.

03:12:19 --> 03:12:23
Okay, that's on the one hand. But on the second hand, we already have the

03:12:22 --> 03:12:27
algorithm, so we could use it already, except it's a terrible algorithm because

03:12:26 --> 03:12:31
it involves all this incredible amount of coding and so on.

03:12:30 --> 03:12:34
- And on the third hand, like you said, we already have approximation algorithms

03:12:33 --> 03:12:35
that-

03:12:34 --> 03:12:35
Yes

03:12:34 --> 03:12:39
- ... that from a pragmatic perspective, solve

03:12:38 --> 03:12:43
all the actual real engineering problems of human civilization.

03:12:42 --> 03:12:47
- Like the SAT solvers work amazingly well, you know, in lots and lots of

03:12:46 --> 03:12:51
cases, even though we can prove we don't expect... If P is not equal to NP, then there won't

03:12:50 --> 03:12:56
be a polynomial time SAT solver. But the actually the SAT solver

03:12:55 --> 03:12:59
approximations, you know, are really quite amazing.

03:12:59 --> 03:13:03
- Sorry to ask the ridiculous question, but who is the greatest mathematician...

03:13:02 --> 03:13:07
of all time? Who are the possible candidates? Euler,

03:13:06 --> 03:13:11
Gauss, Newton, Ramanujan, Hilbert. We

03:13:10 --> 03:13:15
mentioned Gödel, Turing, if you throw him into the bucket.

03:13:14 --> 03:13:19
- So this is, I think, an incredibly difficult question to

03:13:19 --> 03:13:23
answer. Personally, I don't really think this way

03:13:23 --> 03:13:29
about ranking the mathematicians by greatness.

03:13:28 --> 03:13:32
- So you don't have, like... You know, some people have a Taylor Swift poster in their dorm room.

03:13:31 --> 03:13:33
You don't have it.

03:13:33 --> 03:13:38
- I mean, if you forced me to pick someone, it would probably be Archimedes because-

03:13:37 --> 03:13:42
... he had such incredible achievements in such an early

03:13:43 --> 03:13:50
era, which totally transcended the work of the other people in his era.

03:13:49 --> 03:13:54
But I also have the view that I want to learn

03:13:53 --> 03:13:57
mathematics and gain mathematical insight from whoever can

03:13:57 --> 03:14:01
provide it and wherever I can find it. And this isn't always

03:14:00 --> 03:14:05
just coming from the greats. And sometimes the greats are doing things

03:14:05 --> 03:14:09
that are just first and not... You know, somebody else could have easily

03:14:08 --> 03:14:13
been first. And so there's a kind of luck aspect to it when you go back and

03:14:12 --> 03:14:17
look at, you know, the achievements. And because of this progress

03:14:16 --> 03:14:21
issue in mathematics that we talked about earlier, namely, we really do understand things

03:14:20 --> 03:14:25
much better now than they used to. And when you look back at the

03:14:24 --> 03:14:28
achievements that had been made, then maybe you can imagine, you know,

03:14:27 --> 03:14:32
thinking, "Well, you know, somebody else could've, could've had

03:14:31 --> 03:14:36
that insight also." And maybe they

03:14:35 --> 03:14:40
would have. It's already a known phenomenon that disparate mathematicians

03:14:40 --> 03:14:44
end up proving essentially similar results at approximately the

03:14:43 --> 03:14:49
same time. But okay, the person who did it first is getting the credit and so on.

03:14:48 --> 03:14:53
- What do you make of that? Because I see that sometimes when mathematicians...

03:14:52 --> 03:14:57
This also applies in physics and science, where completely separately,

03:14:57 --> 03:15:02
discoveries are made... ...Maybe at a very similar time. What does that mean?

03:15:01 --> 03:15:06
- It's relatively common. I mean, I think it's certain

03:15:05 --> 03:15:11
ideas are in the air and being thought about but not fully articulated, and so

03:15:11 --> 03:15:14
this is the nature of growth in knowledge.

03:15:13 --> 03:15:16
- Do you understand where ideas come from?

03:15:16 --> 03:15:17
- Not really.

03:15:17 --> 03:15:22
- I mean, what's your own process when you're thinking through a problem?

03:15:22 --> 03:15:26
- Yeah, that's another difficult question. I suppose it has to do

03:15:25 --> 03:15:31
with... I mean, my mathematical style, my style as a mathematician, is that

03:15:32 --> 03:15:37
I don't really like difficult mathematics.

03:15:37 --> 03:15:46
What I love is simple, clear, easy-to-understand arguments that

03:15:45 --> 03:15:49
prove a surprising result. That's my favorite

03:15:48 --> 03:15:52
situation. And actually, so the question of whether it's a new result

03:15:52 --> 03:15:56
or not is somehow less important to me. And so that has to do with this

03:15:55 --> 03:16:00
question of the greats and so on, whoever does it first. Because I think, for example, if

03:15:59 --> 03:16:06
you prove a new result with a bad argument or complicated argument,

03:16:07 --> 03:16:11
that's great because you proved something new. But I still

03:16:10 --> 03:16:18
want to see the beautiful simple because that's what I can understand. Also,

03:16:18 --> 03:16:22
I mean, I'm kind of naturally skeptical about any

03:16:22 --> 03:16:27
complicated argument because it might be wrong. And-

03:16:26 --> 03:16:31
...if I can't really understand it fully, like every single

03:16:30 --> 03:16:35
step all at once in my head, then I'm just worried maybe it's

03:16:34 --> 03:16:38
wrong. And so these different styles, sometimes mathematicians get

03:16:37 --> 03:16:42
involved with these enormous research projects that involve

03:16:41 --> 03:16:45
huge numbers of working parts and-

03:16:44 --> 03:16:49
...different technology coming together. I mean, mathematical technology, not physical

03:16:48 --> 03:16:49
technology.

03:16:48 --> 03:16:53
- And sometimes it actually involves now more and more something like the Lean programming

03:16:52 --> 03:16:55
language where some parts are automated, so you have this gigantic-

03:16:54 --> 03:16:59
Yeah, yeah, I see. Well, that's another issue because maybe those things are, you

03:16:58 --> 03:17:03
know, less subject to skepticism when it's validated-

03:17:02 --> 03:17:07
...by Lean. But I'm thinking about the case where the arguments

03:17:06 --> 03:17:11
are just extremely complicated, and so I sort of worry whether

03:17:10 --> 03:17:14
it's right or not, whereas you know, I like the simple thing. And

03:17:13 --> 03:17:18
so, so I tend to have often worked on things that are

03:17:17 --> 03:17:22
a little bit off the beaten path from what other people are

03:17:21 --> 03:17:24
working on from that point of view.

03:17:23 --> 03:17:26
- Your curiosity draws you towards simplicity.

03:17:25 --> 03:17:30
- Yeah. I wanna work on the things that I can understand and that are s-

03:17:29 --> 03:17:37
And luckily, I've found that I've been able to make contributions

03:17:36 --> 03:17:41
that other people seem to like, you know, in this way,

03:17:40 --> 03:17:45
in this style. And so I've been kind of fortunate from that point of view.

03:17:44 --> 03:17:51
I mean, my process always, though, and I've recommended this always to my students,

03:17:51 --> 03:17:56
is just a kind of playful curiosity. So whenever I have...

03:17:55 --> 03:18:04
whenever there's an idea or a topic, then I just play around with it and

03:18:03 --> 03:18:08
change little things or understand a basic case and then

03:18:07 --> 03:18:12
make it more complicated or press things a little bit on this

03:18:11 --> 03:18:18
ide or apply the idea to my favorite example, you know, that's relevant or,

03:18:17 --> 03:18:22
and see what happens, or you just play around with ideas, and this

03:18:21 --> 03:18:25
often leads to insights that then lead to

03:18:24 --> 03:18:29
more methods or more, you know, then pretty soon you're making

03:18:28 --> 03:18:33
progress on the problem. And so this is basically my

03:18:32 --> 03:18:37
method, is I just, you know, fool around with the ideas until I can

03:18:36 --> 03:18:41
see a path through towards something interesting.

03:18:41 --> 03:18:46
And then prove that, and that's worked extremely well for me.

03:18:45 --> 03:18:48
So I'm pretty pleased with that method.

03:18:47 --> 03:18:52
- You do like thought experiments where you anthropomorphize, like you mentioned?

03:18:51 --> 03:18:56
- Yeah, yeah. So this is a basic tool. I mean, I use this all the time. You know, you

03:18:55 --> 03:19:01
imagine a set-theoretic model, a model of ZFC as like

03:19:00 --> 03:19:05
a place where you're living, and you might travel to distant lands by

03:19:04 --> 03:19:09
forcing, and this is a kind of metaphor for what's going on. Of course, you know, the actual

03:19:08 --> 03:19:10
arguments aren't anything

03:19:10 --> 03:19:14
like that because there's not land, and you're not traveling and you're not...

03:19:13 --> 03:19:17
- But you allow your mind to visualize that kind of thing, in the natural real world.

03:19:16 --> 03:19:20
- And it helps you to understand, particularly when there's parts of the argument that are in

03:19:19 --> 03:19:24
tension with one another, then you can imagine that people are fighting or

03:19:23 --> 03:19:28
something. And those kind of metaphors, you know, or you imagine it in

03:19:27 --> 03:19:32
terms of a game-theoretic, you know, two players trying to win. So that's kind of

03:19:31 --> 03:19:36
tension. And those kind of metaphorical ways of understanding a mathematical

03:19:35 --> 03:19:39
problem often are extremely helpful in realizing,

03:19:38 --> 03:19:43
aha, the enemy is going to pick this thing to be like

03:19:42 --> 03:19:47
that because, you know, it makes it more continuous or whatever,

03:19:46 --> 03:19:51
and then we should do this other thing in order to... So it

03:19:50 --> 03:19:55
makes you realize mathematical strategies for finding the

03:19:54 --> 03:19:58
answer and proving the theorem that you want to prove because of the

03:19:57 --> 03:20:02
ideas that come out of that anthropomorphization.

03:20:01 --> 03:20:06
- What do you think of somebody like Andrew Wiles, who spent seven years

03:20:05 --> 03:20:10
grinding at one of the hardest problems in the history of mathematics? And

03:20:09 --> 03:20:14
maybe contrasting that a little bit with somebody who's also brilliant, Terence

03:20:13 --> 03:20:18
Tao, who basically says if he hits a wall, he

03:20:17 --> 03:20:22
just switches to a different problem and he comes back and so on. So it's

03:20:21 --> 03:20:25
less of a focused grind for many years without

03:20:25 --> 03:20:31
any guarantee that you'll get there, which is what Andrew Wiles went through.

03:20:30 --> 03:20:33
Maybe Grigori Perelman did the same.

03:20:32 --> 03:20:37
- I mean, Wiles proved an amazing theorem, the Fermat's Last Theorem result is

03:20:36 --> 03:20:41
incredible. This is a totally different style than my own

03:20:40 --> 03:20:45
practice, though, of working in isolation. I mean, for me,

03:20:44 --> 03:20:49
mathematics is often a kind of social activity. I have-

03:20:48 --> 03:20:54
I counted, I mean, it's pushing towards a hundred collaborators, co-authors on

03:20:53 --> 03:20:58
various papers and so on. And, you know, anybody has an idea they

03:20:57 --> 03:21:02
want to talk about with me, if I'm interested in it, then I'm gonna wanna

03:21:01 --> 03:21:05
collaborate with them and we might solve the problem and have a joint paper or

03:21:04 --> 03:21:07
whatever. You wanna have a joint paper? Let me-

03:21:06 --> 03:21:09
Yeah, exactly. Let's go.

03:21:08 --> 03:21:13
- So my approach to, like, making mathematical progress

03:21:12 --> 03:21:17
tends to involve working with other people quite a lot rather than just working on

03:21:16 --> 03:21:21
my- ...own, and I enjoy that aspect very much. So I,

03:21:20 --> 03:21:25
personally, I couldn't ever do what Wiles did. Maybe I'm missing out. Maybe if

03:21:24 --> 03:21:29
I locked myself, you know, in the bedroom and just worked on whatever, then,

03:21:29 --> 03:21:34
uh, I would solve it. But I tend to think that no, actually, like

03:21:33 --> 03:21:38
being on MathOverflow so much and I've gotten so

03:21:37 --> 03:21:42
many ideas, so many papers have grown out of the MathOverflow

03:21:41 --> 03:21:46
conversations and back and forth. Someone posts an, you know, someone posts a

03:21:45 --> 03:21:50
question and I post an answer on part of it, and then someone else has an idea and it turns into a

03:21:49 --> 03:21:53
full solution, and then we have a three-way paper coming out of that. That's happened

03:21:52 --> 03:21:57
many times. And so for me, it's... I enjoy this

03:21:56 --> 03:22:01
kind of social aspect to it. And it's not just the social

03:22:00 --> 03:22:07
part. Rather, that's the nature of mathematical investigation as I see it, is

03:22:08 --> 03:22:12
putting forth mathematical ideas to other people and they

03:22:12 --> 03:22:16
respond to it in a way that helps me learn, helps them

03:22:16 --> 03:22:21
learn, and I think that's a very productive way of undertaking mathematics.

03:22:20 --> 03:22:25
- I think it's when you work solo on mathematics, from my

03:22:24 --> 03:22:29
outsider perspective, it seems terrifyingly lonely.

03:22:28 --> 03:22:33
And because you're, especially if you do stick to a single

03:22:32 --> 03:22:37
problem, especially if that problem has broken many brilliant

03:22:36 --> 03:22:41
mathematicians in the past, that you're really putting all your chips

03:22:40 --> 03:22:42
in. And just the torment-

03:22:41 --> 03:22:46
...the rollercoaster of day to... that day. Because I imagine

03:22:47 --> 03:22:52
you have these moments of hopeful break, mini breakthroughs, and then

03:22:52 --> 03:22:57
you have to deal with the occasional realization

03:22:56 --> 03:23:01
that, no, it was not a breakthrough, and that disappointment.

03:23:00 --> 03:23:05
And then you have to go, like, a weekly, maybe daily

03:23:05 --> 03:23:09
disappointment where you hit a wall, and you have no other person to

03:23:09 --> 03:23:17
brainstorm with. You have no other avenue to pursue. And it's

03:23:17 --> 03:23:21
I don't know. The mental fortitude it takes to go through that. But every-

03:23:20 --> 03:23:24
Everybody's different. Some people are recluse and just

03:23:24 --> 03:23:33
really find solace in that lone grind. I have to ask about

03:23:32 --> 03:23:37
Grisha Grigori Perelman. What do you think of him famously

03:23:36 --> 03:23:39
declining the Fields Medal and the Millennial Prize?

03:23:39 --> 03:23:44
So he stated, "I'm not interested in money or fame. The prize is

03:23:43 --> 03:23:48
completely irrelevant to me. If the proof is correct, then no

03:23:47 --> 03:23:53
ther recognition is needed." What do you think of him turning down the prize?

03:23:52 --> 03:23:57
- I guess what I think is that mathematics is full of a

03:23:56 --> 03:24:00
lot of different kinds of people. And my attitude is that,

03:24:00 --> 03:24:04
hey, it doesn't matter. Maybe they have a good math idea, and so I want to

03:24:04 --> 03:24:08
talk to them and interact with them. And so I think the Perelman

03:24:07 --> 03:24:15
case, you know, is maybe an instance where, you know, he's

03:24:15 --> 03:24:20
uch a brilliant mind and he solved this extremely famous and

03:24:19 --> 03:24:24
ifficult problem, and that is a huge achievement.

03:24:23 --> 03:24:28
But he also had these views about, you know,

03:24:27 --> 03:24:33
prizes and somehow, I don't really fully understand why he would turn it down.

03:24:33 --> 03:24:37
- I do think I have a similar thing, just observing Olympic

03:24:36 --> 03:24:41
athletes that, in many cases, don't get paid very much,

03:24:40 --> 03:24:43
and they nevertheless dedicate their entire lives for the pursuit...

03:24:42 --> 03:24:48
of the gold medal. I think his case is a reminder that some of the greatest

03:24:47 --> 03:24:52
mathematicians, some of the greatest scientists and human beings do the thing

03:24:51 --> 03:24:55
they do, take on these problems for the love

03:24:55 --> 03:25:00
of it, not for the prizes or the money or any of that.

03:24:59 --> 03:25:03
Now, as you're saying, if the money comes, you could use it for

03:25:02 --> 03:25:07
stuff. If the prizes come, and the fame, and so on, that might be

03:25:06 --> 03:25:13
useful. But the reason fundamentally the greats do it is because of the art itself.

03:25:13 --> 03:25:17
- Sure, I totally agree with that. I mean, I share the view. That's,

03:25:16 --> 03:25:21
you know, that's why I'm a mathematician

03:25:20 --> 03:25:25
is because I find the question so compelling and I've spent my whole life

03:25:24 --> 03:25:32
thinking about these problems. But, you know, if I won an award -

03:25:32 --> 03:25:36
Yeah, it's great. It's great. I mean, I'm pretty sure you don't contribute to

03:25:35 --> 03:25:44
MathOverflow for the wealth, and the power. That you gain.

03:25:43 --> 03:25:47
I mean, it's genuine curiosity.

03:25:46 --> 03:25:51
- Well, you asked who the greatest mathematician is, and

03:25:50 --> 03:25:54
of course, if we want to be truly objective about it, we would need a kind of an

03:25:53 --> 03:25:56
objective criteria.

03:25:55 --> 03:25:56
- Criteria, yeah.

03:25:55 --> 03:25:59
- About how to evaluate the relative strength and the

03:25:58 --> 03:26:03
reputation of various mathematicians. And so, of course, we should use

03:26:02 --> 03:26:05
MathOverflow score. ...Because -

03:26:06 --> 03:26:11
That you're definitively. I mean, nobody's objectively the greatest mathematician of

03:26:10 --> 03:26:11
all time.

03:26:10 --> 03:26:15
- Yes, that's true. I've also argued that tenure and promotion decisions

03:26:14 --> 03:26:16
hould be based...

03:26:15 --> 03:26:17
- Based on MathOverflow.

03:26:16 --> 03:26:21
- ...Yeah. So my daughter introduced me to her boyfriend.

03:26:20 --> 03:26:26
and told me that she had a boyfriend. And I, um-

03:26:25 --> 03:26:27
asked him what his MathOverflow...

03:26:26 --> 03:26:31
- I wanted to know, first of all, what is his chess rating,

03:26:30 --> 03:26:34
and secondly, what is his MathOverflow score?

03:26:34 --> 03:26:39
- Oh, man. Well, that's the only way to judge a person, I think. That's, I

03:26:38 --> 03:26:41
think, objectively correct. Yeah.

03:26:41 --> 03:26:46
I mean, since you bring up chess, I've got to ask you about infinite chess. I can't let

03:26:45 --> 03:26:50
you go. You've, I mean, you worked on a million things, but infinite chess is one of

03:26:49 --> 03:26:55
them. Somebody asked on MathOverflow, the mathematical definition of chess.

03:26:54 --> 03:26:55
- Right.

03:26:54 --> 03:26:59
- So can we talk about the math of chess and the math of infinite chess? What

03:26:58 --> 03:27:00
is infinite chess?

03:26:59 --> 03:27:04
- Oh, yeah, absolutely. Infinite chess is fantastic. Chess ordinarily is

03:27:03 --> 03:27:08
played on this tiny, tiny board. It's an eight by eight board, right? So

03:27:07 --> 03:27:11
when you play chess, normally it's on the eight by eight board. But we want to play

03:27:10 --> 03:27:15
infinite chess, so on the, on the

03:27:15 --> 03:27:19
integer board. It's infinite in all four directions, you

03:27:18 --> 03:27:23
know, but it still has the chessboard pattern, and maybe there's pieces on this board,

03:27:22 --> 03:27:27
maybe infinitely many pieces we allow. But one difference from

03:27:26 --> 03:27:34
finite ordinary chess, in infinite chess, we don't play from a standard starting

03:27:33 --> 03:27:38
position. Rather, you... The

03:27:37 --> 03:27:41
interesting situation is that you present a position where there's a lot of

03:27:40 --> 03:27:45
pieces already on the board in a complicated way, and you say, "What would it

03:27:44 --> 03:27:49
be like to start from this position or from that one?" You know, and we want to

03:27:48 --> 03:27:52
produce positions that have interesting features, meaning

03:27:51 --> 03:27:57
mathematically interesting features. And so I can tell you for

03:27:56 --> 03:28:05
example, probably a lot of people are familiar with, say, the

03:28:04 --> 03:28:08
mate in two genre of chess problem.

03:28:07 --> 03:28:12
You know, you have a chess problem and it's white to mate in two, which means that

03:28:11 --> 03:28:16
white is going to make two moves, but the second move is going to be a checkmate.

03:28:15 --> 03:28:20
Or maybe mate in three or mate in five or whatever. We can have mate in

03:28:19 --> 03:28:26
N positions for any N. I mean, in infinite chess, you can create a position

03:28:26 --> 03:28:32
which is not mate in N for any N, but

03:28:33 --> 03:28:40
white has a winning strategy that will win infinitely many moves. So in other words,

03:28:39 --> 03:28:44
let me say it again. There are positions in infinite chess that

03:28:43 --> 03:28:48
white can definitely win. Infinitely many moves, white is

03:28:47 --> 03:28:52
going to make checkmate. But there's no particular N

03:28:51 --> 03:28:57
for which white can guarantee to win in N moves.

03:28:56 --> 03:28:58
- There's no N?

03:28:57 --> 03:29:02
- No N. So it's not mate in N for any N, but it's a white

03:29:01 --> 03:29:05
win, infinitely many. The way to think about it is,

03:29:05 --> 03:29:10
white is going to win, but black controls how long it takes.

03:29:09 --> 03:29:11
- Ah, got it.

03:29:10 --> 03:29:15
- But it's doomed. Black can say, "Well, I know you're gonna win, but this time it's gonna...

03:29:14 --> 03:29:18
you're gonna take a thousand moves at least."

03:29:17 --> 03:29:22
Or maybe in a different way of playing, black can say, "Well, I know you're gonna win, but this time you're

03:29:21 --> 03:29:26
gonna have to take a million moves." For any number, black can say that. So it's

03:29:25 --> 03:29:29
these really interesting positions. There's a position in my first

03:29:29 --> 03:29:34
infinite chess paper. So it's black to play in this position, and

03:29:34 --> 03:29:38
if black doesn't move that rook there,

03:29:38 --> 03:29:42
then white is gonna checkmate pretty quickly.

03:29:41 --> 03:29:45
- By the way, can we describe the rules of infinite chess?

03:29:44 --> 03:29:49
- Right. So the rules of infinite chess are there's just the ordinary pieces,

03:29:48 --> 03:29:53
and they move on this infinite board, which is just a chessboard, but extended in

03:29:52 --> 03:29:57
all directions- infinitely, with no edge. So there's no boundary.

03:29:57 --> 03:30:02
But the pieces move just like you'd expect. So the knights move just the same and the

03:30:02 --> 03:30:06
rooks move, you know, on the ranks and files, and the bishops move on the same color

03:30:06 --> 03:30:10
diagonals and, just like you would expect, except they can move as far as they

03:30:10 --> 03:30:13
want, you know, if there's no intervening piece in the way.

03:30:13 --> 03:30:18
The one thing is that, okay, so the white pawns always

03:30:17 --> 03:30:22
move upwards and the black pawns always move downwards, but when they're

03:30:21 --> 03:30:26
capturing, the pawns, you know, capture on the diagonal. So I

03:30:25 --> 03:30:29
think the piece movement is pretty clear. There's a couple

03:30:28 --> 03:30:33
of differences that you have to pay attention to from ordinary chess. For

03:30:32 --> 03:30:37
example, there's this threefold repetition

03:30:36 --> 03:30:41
rule in ordinary chess, but we just get rid of this for infinite

03:30:40 --> 03:30:45
chess because, of course, threefold repetition is just a proxy for infinite

03:30:44 --> 03:30:48
play. The real rule is infinite play is a draw, not

03:30:47 --> 03:30:52
hreefold repetition is a draw. That's just a kind of convenient

03:30:51 --> 03:30:56
approximation to the, what I view as the actual rule, which is that

03:30:55 --> 03:31:00
infinite play is a draw. So the only way to win is to make checkmate on

03:30:59 --> 03:31:04
the board at a finite stage of play. And if you play infinitely, you

03:31:03 --> 03:31:06
haven't done that, and so it's a draw.

03:31:05 --> 03:31:07
- And the pawns can't be converted into-

03:31:06 --> 03:31:11
And there's no promotion 'cause there's no edge. Right, exactly. And

03:31:10 --> 03:31:15
this position that we were just talking about is a position with game value omega, which

03:31:14 --> 03:31:19
means that because it has an ordinal value, white is

03:31:18 --> 03:31:22
going to win, but black can play as though

03:31:21 --> 03:31:26
counting down from omega. What is the nature of counting down from

03:31:25 --> 03:31:30
ega? If you're black and you need to count down from omega, then

03:31:30 --> 03:31:33
you have to say a finite number,

03:31:33 --> 03:31:37
and then after that, it's gonna be at most that many numbers

03:31:36 --> 03:31:41
afterwards to count down, right? So the nature of counting down from omega is

03:31:40 --> 03:31:45
that you take this giant step on the first count,

03:31:44 --> 03:31:49
and then after that, you subtract one each time. You can't subtract one from

03:31:48 --> 03:31:53
ega because that's not an ordinal. So if you count down from omega, you have

03:31:52 --> 03:31:57
to go to some finite number, and then if you just subtract one each time, then

03:31:56 --> 03:31:59
that's how many more moves you get.

03:31:58 --> 03:32:03
So that's the sense in which black can make it take as long as he wants

03:32:02 --> 03:32:06
because he can pick his initial number to be whatever he wants.

03:32:05 --> 03:32:10
- By the way, I just noticed that you were citing a MathOverflow question, which is really

03:32:09 --> 03:32:11
cool.

03:32:10 --> 03:32:15
- That's right, yeah. My interest in infinite chess was born on MathOverflow 'cause someone asked

03:32:14 --> 03:32:16
this question.

03:32:15 --> 03:32:20
- Noam Elkies asked this question. That's so cool to see a MathOverflow citation

03:32:19 --> 03:32:27
in an arXiv paper. That's cool. How do you construct the position-

03:32:28 --> 03:32:29
Right

03:32:28 --> 03:32:32
- the position that satisfies this? Is there an algorithm for construction?

03:32:31 --> 03:32:36
- No. This is an act of mathematical creativity, really, to come up

03:32:35 --> 03:32:39
with... I had a co-author, my co-author, Corey Evans. He's a

03:32:38 --> 03:32:43
US national master chess player.

03:32:42 --> 03:32:50
A very strong chess player. He's also a philosophy professor of law.

03:32:49 --> 03:32:54
- Your collaborations are wonderful. That's great.

03:32:53 --> 03:32:57
- So I met him because he was a grad student at CUNY where I was at the time in New York.

03:32:57 --> 03:33:02
And also he was my son's chess coach for when my son was

03:33:01 --> 03:33:06
playing chess competitively in elementary school.

03:33:05 --> 03:33:09
Then Corey was the coach. And so we knew him that

03:33:08 --> 03:33:13
way. And that was right around the time when I was getting interested in

03:33:12 --> 03:33:20
finite chess, and I knew I needed a chess-knowledgeable partner. And

03:33:19 --> 03:33:24
so Corey was invaluable for the paper because

03:33:25 --> 03:33:29
the proofs in infinite chess are extremely

03:33:28 --> 03:33:33
finicky because you create these positions,

03:33:32 --> 03:33:37
but the details of the argument have to do with kind of chess

03:33:36 --> 03:33:41
reasoning, you know? My chess reasoning

03:33:40 --> 03:33:45
wasn't quite up to it because I would create

03:33:44 --> 03:33:49
the positions. Almost all the positions are ones that I made,

03:33:48 --> 03:33:51
but this is like after many generations,

03:33:50 --> 03:33:55
of being corrected by Corey because Corey would come and say, "Hey, you know, this pawn

03:33:54 --> 03:33:58
is hanging, and it breaks your argument, and"

03:33:57 --> 03:34:04
or, or, you know, this bishop can leak out" "of the cage," or whatever. And so...

03:34:03 --> 03:34:08
the process was I knew kind of

03:34:07 --> 03:34:11
in terms of these ordinals what we needed to create with

03:34:10 --> 03:34:15
e position, and I would struggle to do it and create something that sort

03:34:14 --> 03:34:19
of had the features that I wanted, and then I would show it to Corey and he would say,

03:34:18 --> 03:34:23
"Look, it doesn't work because of this and that," and so on. And so this kind of back and

03:34:22 --> 03:34:27
forth was extremely helpful to me, and eventually we, you know, converged

03:34:26 --> 03:34:30
on arguments that were correct.

03:34:30 --> 03:34:34
So it's... yeah, it's quite interesting. Also, maybe

03:34:33 --> 03:34:38
another thing to say is the follow-up paper to this one was a three-way

03:34:37 --> 03:34:42
paper with also Corey and myself and my

03:34:41 --> 03:34:45
PhD student, Norman Perlmutter, in which we improved the bound.

03:34:44 --> 03:34:49
So we were aiming to produce more and more chess positions with higher and higher ordinal values.

03:34:52 --> 03:34:57
So the initial position was value omega, and then we made omega-squared and omega-cubed in the first paper,

03:34:56 --> 03:35:01
and then in this three-way collaboration, we made omega to the 4th.

03:35:03 --> 03:35:08
- The title of the paper: The Position in Infinite Chess with Game Value

03:35:07 --> 03:35:10
Omega to the 4th.

03:35:09 --> 03:35:14
- Right. And so, at the time, this was the best-known result,

03:35:13 --> 03:35:18
the sort of state of the art, but since that time, it's been improved

03:35:17 --> 03:35:23
now dramatically. And, in fact, we know now that every countable ordinal

03:35:22 --> 03:35:29
arises as the game value of a position in infinite chess, so it's a fantastic result.

03:35:28 --> 03:35:37
- Before I forget, let me ask about your views on AI and LLMs that are getting

03:35:37 --> 03:35:42
better and better in mathematics. We've spoken about collaborators, and you have so many

03:35:41 --> 03:35:48
collaborators. Do you see AI as a potential great collaborator to you as a

03:35:47 --> 03:35:51
mathematician, and what do you think the future role of those-

03:35:50 --> 03:35:52
kinds of AI systems is?

03:35:52 --> 03:35:57
- I guess I would draw a distinction between what we have currently

03:35:56 --> 03:36:04
and what might come in future years. I've played around with it and I've tried

03:36:03 --> 03:36:08
experimenting, but I haven't found it helpful at all,

03:36:07 --> 03:36:11
basically zero. It's not helpful to me.

03:36:10 --> 03:36:15
And, you know, I've used various systems and so on, the

03:36:14 --> 03:36:23
paid models and so on. My typical experience is interacting with AI on a

03:36:22 --> 03:36:27
mathematical question is that it gives me garbage

03:36:26 --> 03:36:31
answers that are not mathematically correct.

03:36:30 --> 03:36:37
And so I find that not helpful and also frustrating.

03:36:36 --> 03:36:41
Like, if I was interacting with a person, the frustrating

03:36:40 --> 03:36:45
thing is when you have to argue about whether or not, you know, the

03:36:44 --> 03:36:48
argument that they gave you is right, and you point out exactly the error,

03:36:47 --> 03:36:51
in the AI saying, "Oh, it's totally fine." And,

03:36:52 --> 03:36:57
you know, if I were having such an experience with a person, I would simply

03:36:56 --> 03:36:59
refuse to talk to that person again.

03:36:58 --> 03:37:03
But okay, one has to overlook these kind of flaws.

03:37:02 --> 03:37:10
And so I tend to be a kind of skeptic about the value of the current AI systems as

03:37:09 --> 03:37:14
far as mathematical reasoning is concerned. It seems not

03:37:13 --> 03:37:18
reliable. Okay, but I know for a

03:37:17 --> 03:37:22
fact that many, that there are several prominent mathematicians whom I have

03:37:21 --> 03:37:28
normous respect for, who are saying that they are using it in a way

03:37:27 --> 03:37:32
that's helpful, and I'm often very surprised to hear that

03:37:31 --> 03:37:37
based on my own experience, which is quite the opposite. And so

03:37:37 --> 03:37:42
maybe my process isn't any good, although, you know, I use it for other

03:37:41 --> 03:37:45
things, like, you know, for programming things or

03:37:44 --> 03:37:49
for image generation and so on. It's amazingly

03:37:48 --> 03:37:53
powerful and helpful. But for mathematical arguments,

03:37:52 --> 03:38:00
I haven't found it helpful, and maybe I'm not interacting with it in the right way.

03:37:59 --> 03:38:03
Yet, or it could be. And so maybe I just need to improve my

03:38:03 --> 03:38:11
skill. But also maybe I wonder, like, these examples that

03:38:10 --> 03:38:14
are provided by other people maybe involved quite a huge amount of

03:38:13 --> 03:38:18
interaction, and so I wonder if maybe the mathematical

03:38:17 --> 03:38:22
ideas are really coming from the person, you know, these great mathematicians

03:38:21 --> 03:38:28
who are doing it rather than the AI. And so so I tend to be kind of skeptical. But

03:38:27 --> 03:38:32
also, I'm skeptical for another reason, and that is

03:38:34 --> 03:38:38
because of the nature of the large language model approach

03:38:38 --> 03:38:49
to AI doing mathematics. Um, I recognize that the AI is trying to give me

03:38:48 --> 03:38:53
an argument that sounds like a proof rather than an

03:38:52 --> 03:39:00
argument that is a proof. The motivation is misplaced. And so I

03:38:59 --> 03:39:04
worry that this is a very dangerous source of error

03:39:03 --> 03:39:08
because it often happens in mathematics that, I mean, if I

03:39:07 --> 03:39:12
think back to when I was an undergrad, you know, here at Caltech, and I

03:39:11 --> 03:39:16
was a math major eventually, and at that

03:39:15 --> 03:39:20
ime, LaTeX was a pretty new thing, and I was learning LaTeX, and

03:39:19 --> 03:39:25
so I was typing up my homeworks in LaTeX and they looked beautiful.

03:39:24 --> 03:39:29
Actually, they looked like garbage. From my current standards,

03:39:28 --> 03:39:30
I'm sure it was terrible.

03:39:29 --> 03:39:34
Except at the time, you know, I didn't know anything. I was an undergrad,

03:39:33 --> 03:39:38
and LaTeX was sort of unheard of.

03:39:37 --> 03:39:41
And so I was producing these beautifully typeset, you know,

03:39:40 --> 03:39:45
problem sets, solutions, and so on. And

03:39:44 --> 03:39:48
I would print it up and submit it and so on, and the grades would come back,

03:39:47 --> 03:39:53
terrible grades. And I realized what was happening

03:39:52 --> 03:39:58
is that the, you know, the copy was so beautiful

03:39:58 --> 03:40:02
mathematically typeset in this way. It looked like the kind of mathematics you find in a

03:40:01 --> 03:40:03
book, you know?

03:40:02 --> 03:40:07
Because basically that's the only time you saw that kind of mathematical typesetting was in

03:40:06 --> 03:40:11
a, in a professional, you know, published book.

03:40:10 --> 03:40:14
And those me- that mathematics was almost always correct-

03:40:13 --> 03:40:21
... in a book, right? And so I had somehow, you know, lost my-

03:40:20 --> 03:40:25
... because it was so beautiful, and I'm used to only seeing that kind of type

03:40:24 --> 03:40:28
setting when an argument was, you know, totally right-

03:40:27 --> 03:40:32
... I wasn't critical enough, and making these sort of bonehead

03:40:31 --> 03:40:37
mistakes in the proofs. And, and so, okay, so I, I corrected this, of course.

03:40:36 --> 03:40:41
- But this kind of effect is very much real with the modern LLM system. That's right.

03:40:40 --> 03:40:45
- And so I think that the chat programs

03:40:44 --> 03:40:47
and so on are producing these arguments that look

03:40:46 --> 03:40:51
really, they look like a... that's what they're striving to do,

03:40:50 --> 03:40:55
that it's what they're designed to do. They're not designed to

03:40:54 --> 03:40:59
make a logically correct argument. They're designed to make something that

03:40:58 --> 03:41:02
looks like a logically correct argument. And it's easy to get

03:41:02 --> 03:41:06
fooled if you're not skeptical. And so that's why I worry a bit when

03:41:05 --> 03:41:10
people rely on AI for mathematical arguments.

03:41:10 --> 03:41:14
I mean, using... tying them to Lean in the formal proof,

03:41:13 --> 03:41:18
um verification systems and so on, this is a totally different

03:41:17 --> 03:41:21
way of operating. But for the sort of ordinary person sitting down and using

03:41:21 --> 03:41:24
chat to come up with a mathematical

03:41:23 --> 03:41:28
argument, I think it's a dangerous source of error if you're

03:41:27 --> 03:41:32
not especially attuned to this very issue that

03:41:31 --> 03:41:36
he AI is going to produce something that's not grounded in

03:41:35 --> 03:41:40
mathematical understanding, but rather something that is trying

03:41:39 --> 03:41:42
to look like something that is grounded

03:41:41 --> 03:41:46
in mathematical understanding. And those are not the same thing at all. And

03:41:46 --> 03:41:51
furthermore, I really wonder if one can make a kind of system for

03:41:50 --> 03:41:57
producing genuine mathematical insight that isn't based in what I would view as

03:41:56 --> 03:42:01
mathematical understanding as opposed to the text

03:42:00 --> 03:42:05
generation systems. The methods that are used, you know, they

03:42:04 --> 03:42:09
don't seem close enough grounded in understanding of the underlying mathematical

03:42:08 --> 03:42:13
concepts, but rather grounded in

03:42:12 --> 03:42:17
the way words appear on a page in arguments about those concepts, which are

03:42:16 --> 03:42:18
not the same.

03:42:17 --> 03:42:23
- So there's a couple of things to say there. So one, I think there is a real skill in

03:42:23 --> 03:42:30
providing the LLM system with enough information to be a good collaborator.

03:42:29 --> 03:42:34
Because you really are dealing with a different... It's not a human being. You really have to load

03:42:33 --> 03:42:38
in everything you possibly can from your body of

03:42:37 --> 03:42:41
work, from the way you're thinking, and that's a real skill. And then the

03:42:41 --> 03:42:45
other thing is, you know, for me, if it's at all anything

03:42:44 --> 03:42:49
like programming, because I have a lot of colleagues and friends who

03:42:48 --> 03:42:56
are programmers who kind of feel similarly to you. And for me, I've

03:42:55 --> 03:43:00
gotten better and better and better at giving as much

03:42:59 --> 03:43:04
information as possible to the systems in a really structured way, maybe

03:43:03 --> 03:43:08
cause I just like natural language as a way to express

03:43:07 --> 03:43:12
my thinking. And then the benefit comes from the

03:43:11 --> 03:43:16
inspiration that the system can provide by its ability to

03:43:16 --> 03:43:21
know a lot of things and make connections between

03:43:20 --> 03:43:26
disparate fields and between disparate concepts. And in that way, it provides

03:43:26 --> 03:43:30
not the answer but the inspiration, the

03:43:29 --> 03:43:34
handholding, the camaraderie that helps me get to the

03:43:33 --> 03:43:42
answer, because it does know a lot more than me. Know, like knowledge. And if you

03:43:41 --> 03:43:48
give it a lot of information and ask the broader questions, it can make some really

03:43:47 --> 03:43:52
beautiful connections. But I do find that I have to be

03:43:51 --> 03:43:54
xtremely patient, like you said.

03:43:53 --> 03:43:58
The, the amount of times I'll do something dumb where

03:43:57 --> 03:44:02
I feel like, "Uh, you don't get this at all, do you?" That's a source of a lot

03:44:01 --> 03:44:05
of frustration for us humans. Like, "This... Wait, this

03:44:04 --> 03:44:09
thing doesn't understand at all." If you can have the patience to look past

03:44:08 --> 03:44:16
hat, there might be some brilliant little insights that it can provide.

03:44:16 --> 03:44:17
- Right.

03:44:16 --> 03:44:21
- At least for me in the realm of programming. I should say

03:44:20 --> 03:44:24
programming, there's just so much training data. There's so much

03:44:23 --> 03:44:28
there. And at least I see

03:44:27 --> 03:44:32
the light at the end of the tunnel of promising possibilities

03:44:31 --> 03:44:34
of it being a good collaborator, versus like

03:44:35 --> 03:44:39
something that gives you really true genius-level insights.

03:44:39 --> 03:44:44
- Right. It's probably true. Uh, I also find it likely that a

03:44:43 --> 03:44:48
lot of the... As far as mathematical training data is concerned,

03:44:48 --> 03:44:53
I just have to assume that math overflow answers are part of the training data.

03:44:52 --> 03:44:54
- Yes, of course.

03:44:53 --> 03:44:55
- It's so...

03:44:54 --> 03:44:57
- And you're...

03:44:56 --> 03:44:57
- So-

03:44:56 --> 03:44:58
I mean, you're talking to yourself, essentially.

03:44:57 --> 03:45:01
- Yeah, maybe.

03:45:00 --> 03:45:06
- Sorry for the ridiculously big question, but what idea in mathematics

03:45:06 --> 03:45:10
is most beautiful to you? We've talked about so many.

03:45:10 --> 03:45:15
- The most beautiful idea in mathematics is the

03:45:14 --> 03:45:18
transfinite ordinals. These were the number system

03:45:17 --> 03:45:22
invented by Georg Cantor about counting beyond

03:45:21 --> 03:45:24
infinity, just the idea of counting beyond infinity.

03:45:24 --> 03:45:29
I mean, you count through the ordinary numbers, the natural numbers: zero, one, two,

03:45:28 --> 03:45:36
three, and so on. And then you're not done because after that comes omega, and then

03:45:35 --> 03:45:40
omega plus one, and omega plus two, and so on. And you can always

03:45:39 --> 03:45:44
add one. And so of course after you count through

03:45:43 --> 03:45:47
all those numbers of the form omega plus N, then you get to

03:45:47 --> 03:45:52
mega plus omega, the first number after all those. And

03:45:51 --> 03:45:56
then comes omega plus omega plus one, and so on. You can always add

03:45:55 --> 03:45:59
one. And so you can just keep counting through the ordinals. It never

03:45:58 --> 03:46:03
ends. Eventually, you get to omega times three, omega times

03:46:02 --> 03:46:07
four, and so on. And then the limit of those numbers, the first number that

03:46:06 --> 03:46:10
comes after all those numbers will be omega squared.

03:46:10 --> 03:46:14
And this one is the first compound limit ordinal

03:46:13 --> 03:46:17
because it's a limit ordinal, is one of these numbers, an

03:46:16 --> 03:46:21
ordinal that doesn't have an immediate predecessor like omega and

03:46:20 --> 03:46:25
omega times two, omega times three. Those are all limit ordinals.

03:46:24 --> 03:46:29
But omega squared is a limit ordinal, but it's also a

03:46:28 --> 03:46:33
limit of limit ordinals because the omega times three,

03:46:32 --> 03:46:37
omega times four, and so on, those are all limit ordinals that limit up to omega

03:46:36 --> 03:46:41
squared. And then, of course, you form omega squared plus one, and

03:46:40 --> 03:46:45
then omega squared plus two, and so on, and it never stops. And it's

03:46:44 --> 03:46:48
just absolutely beautiful and amazing, and

03:46:47 --> 03:46:52
furthermore, forms the foundation for these

03:46:51 --> 03:46:55
transfinite recursive constructions that came later. I mean

03:46:55 --> 03:47:03
starting with the Cantor-Bendixson theorem that I mentioned. And continuing with,

03:47:02 --> 03:47:07
the construction of the V hierarchy

03:47:06 --> 03:47:11
and Gödel's constructible universe is built this way,

03:47:10 --> 03:47:15
and Zermelo's proof of the well-order principle using the

03:47:14 --> 03:47:19
axiom of choice is a transfinite recursive construction. And,

03:47:18 --> 03:47:23
and so the idea of just counting past infinity

03:47:22 --> 03:47:28
is so simple and elegant, and has led to so much fascinating mathematics.

03:47:27 --> 03:47:31
- Yeah, the infinity's not the end. And what about

03:47:30 --> 03:47:35
philosophy? What to you is the most beautiful idea in philosophy?

03:47:35 --> 03:47:40
- So I have a foot in both fields: philosophy and mathematics, and

03:47:41 --> 03:47:48
in some contexts I seem to be required to choose whether I'm a mathematician or a

03:47:47 --> 03:47:51
philosopher. I mean, my training is in mathematics. My PhD, all my degrees are

03:47:50 --> 03:47:55
mathematics. But somehow I turned myself into a philosopher over

03:47:54 --> 03:47:59
the years because my mathematical work was engaging with these philosophical

03:47:58 --> 03:48:03
issues. And so when I went... In New

03:48:02 --> 03:48:07
York, I had appointments first in mathematics only, but then eventually I was

03:48:06 --> 03:48:10
also joining the philosophy faculty at the graduate

03:48:09 --> 03:48:14
center. And when I went to Oxford for the first time, my main appointment was in

03:48:13 --> 03:48:16
philosophy, and that's also true now

03:48:16 --> 03:48:21
at Notre Dame although I'm also a concurrent professor in

03:48:20 --> 03:48:26
mathematics. And I have math PhD students still and philosophy PhD students. And so

03:48:26 --> 03:48:33
I don't really care to decide whether I'm a mathematician or a philosopher.

03:48:32 --> 03:48:36
And my work is engaging with mathematics and with

03:48:35 --> 03:48:39
philosophical issues in mathematics and with plain

03:48:38 --> 03:48:43
philosophy, and there's this ample region between

03:48:42 --> 03:48:47
these re- between these two subjects. So it's not necessary to

03:48:46 --> 03:48:50
choose. I remember when I first went to Oxford and I told my

03:48:49 --> 03:48:54
daughter that I was going to become professor of philosophy in Oxford,

03:48:54 --> 03:48:59
And she looked at me plaintively and said, "Uh,

03:48:59 --> 03:49:04
but, but Papa, you're not a philosopher. Because in her

03:49:03 --> 03:49:07
mind, you know, her father was the mathematician and her mother was the

03:49:07 --> 03:49:11
philosopher 'cause my wife, Barbara, is a

03:49:10 --> 03:49:18
philosopher. Now also at Notre Dame. We're together there. And okay, but

03:49:17 --> 03:49:22
fortunately, I don't really have to choose between them. So you

03:49:21 --> 03:49:26
ask about the most beautiful idea in philosophy, and I would have to say

03:49:25 --> 03:49:33
that I think it's the distinction between truth and proof, the one that we discussed

03:49:33 --> 03:49:40
already. Um, it's, it's so profound and

03:49:40 --> 03:49:45
gets at the heart of so many philosophical issues. I

03:49:44 --> 03:49:49
mean, of course this is a distinction that's maybe born in mathematics

03:49:48 --> 03:49:53
or mathematical logic, but that's already philosophical to a

03:49:52 --> 03:50:00
degree, and it's ph- you know, fundamentally a philosophical distinction. The

03:49:59 --> 03:50:02
truth is about the,

03:50:03 --> 03:50:07
nature of the world and the way things are. It's about objective

03:50:06 --> 03:50:11
reality in a sense. Whereas proof is about our

03:50:10 --> 03:50:15
understanding of the world and about how we come

03:50:14 --> 03:50:19
to know the things that we know about the world.

03:50:18 --> 03:50:26
And so to focus on proof is to focus on the interaction that we have with

03:50:26 --> 03:50:30
e objective reality. And, okay, I'm talking about the reality

03:50:30 --> 03:50:34
of mathematics, not the physical world, because, as I said, I live in the Platonic realm

03:50:33 --> 03:50:38
and I interact with mathematical reality, and so

03:50:37 --> 03:50:42
proof is about the interaction and how we come to know the

03:50:41 --> 03:50:45
facts that are true in this mathematical

03:50:45 --> 03:50:49
reality, whereas truth is about what's really the case,

03:50:48 --> 03:50:57
sort of apart from our knowledge of it. And this is, I think, such a

03:50:56 --> 03:51:01
core way that I have of understanding the world

03:51:00 --> 03:51:04
and the nature of logic and reasonings.

03:51:03 --> 03:51:07
- And the gap between the two is full of fascinating

03:51:06 --> 03:51:11
mysteries, both in the Platonic realm, but also in the

03:51:10 --> 03:51:15
physics realm, and I would even say in the human

03:51:15 --> 03:51:19
psychology, sociology, politics, geopolitics, all of

03:51:18 --> 03:51:23
it, if you think about proof more generally, which is the process

03:51:22 --> 03:51:31
of discovery versus the truth itself. And that's our journey whatever

03:51:31 --> 03:51:38
field we're in. Well, I for one, am grateful for

03:51:37 --> 03:51:41
how marvelous of a philosopher, mathematician, and human being you

03:51:40 --> 03:51:44
are. It's truly an honor to speak with you today.

03:51:43 --> 03:51:48
- Well, thank you so much. It's such a pleasure to be here, and thank you for inviting me.

03:51:47 --> 03:51:52
- Thanks for listening to this conversation with Joel David Hamkins.

03:51:51 --> 03:51:55
To support this podcast, please check out our sponsors in the description

03:51:54 --> 03:51:59
where you can also find links to contact me, ask questions, get

03:51:58 --> 03:52:05
feedback, and so on. Thank you for listening. As always, happy New Year.

03:52:05 --> 03:52:08
I love you all.
